### [1] [From ML Predictions to Informed Diagnostic Assistance Using the Toulmin Model of Argumentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09664)
*Anca Marginean,Adrian Groza*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an argumentation-based framework for interpretable retinal diagnosis by decomposing ML-generated claims into Toulmin model components (grounds from biomarker extraction, warrant via MedGemma agent, qualifier from quantitative evaluation, rebuttal via MedSigLip similarity), enabling more informed human expert assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a structured and interpretable assessment of ML-based diagnoses rather than accepting claims at face value or relying solely on standard XAI methods.

Method: Decomposes diagnosis using Toulmin's argumentation model: grounds from biomarker extraction model, warrant from MedGemma agent with medical knowledge, qualifier from quantitative evaluation, and rebuttal from MedSigLip-based image similarity measures.

Result: All argumentation components (claim, grounds, warrant, qualifier, rebuttal) are presented to human experts for more informed and critical assessment of ML-generated retinal diagnosis.

Conclusion: The argumentation-based framework enhances interpretability and trustworthiness of ML diagnoses by providing structured, evidence-backed reasoning to human experts.

Abstract: To provide a structured and interpretable assessment, we decompose the image-based diagnosis into components following the Toulmin model of argumentation. This model consists of a claim, grounds, warrant, qualifier, rebuttal, and backing. Consider a claim generated by a machine learning (ML) model for retinal diagnosis. Rather than accepting this claim at face value, one could either apply explainable AI (XAI) methods or adopt an argumentation-based approach. In our framework, a model specialized in biomarker extraction from images provides the grounds. The warrant-linking the grounds to the claim - is analyzed by an agent equipped with medical knowledge; in our architecture, this role is fulfilled by a MedGemma agent. The qualifier is determined based on the overall quantitative evaluation of both the warrant and grounds models. Finally, a rebuttal is constructed using image similarity measures computed with MedSigLip. All these components are presented to the human expert, enabling a more informed and critical assessment of the ML-generated diagnosis.

</details>


### [2] [Format Sensitivity Index: Token-Controlled Prompt Wrapper Robustness and Schema Compliance in LLM Benchmarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09665)
*Deep Pankajbhai Mehta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Prompt formatting (wrappers) causes large accuracy variance across models (up to 30x FSI differences), driven mainly by compliance failures; parseability strongly predicts accuracy even after controlling for confounds, so reporting accuracy alone is statistically fragile.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prompt wrappers differ only in formatting but can flip leaderboard conclusions, yet this variance is understudied. The authors aim to quantify this sensitivity and its root causes.

Method: Token-controlled protocol across 140K generations, 7 QA tasks, 5 wrapper families, 4 instruct models (7B–72B). Introduces FSI (accuracy range from wrappers) and PSI (parseability range). Uses fixed-effects regression to isolate parseability's effect on accuracy.

Result: Mean FSI varies >30× across models; variance is largely explained by compliance failures. Parseability remains a strong predictor of accuracy after controlling for task, model, and wrapper.

Conclusion: Reporting accuracy without wrapper variance and compliance is statistically fragile. Recommends benchmarking practices and structured-output deployment guidelines that account for format sensitivity.

Abstract: Prompt wrappers often differ only in formatting, yet they can change model scores enough to flip leaderboard conclusions. We study this variance under a token-controlled protocol and introduce two complementary metrics: the Format Sensitivity Index (FSI), the accuracy range induced by wrapper choice, and the Parseability Sensitivity Index (PSI), the corresponding range in answer parseability. Across 140,000 OpenRouter generations spanning 7 QA tasks, 5 wrapper families, and 4 instruct models from 7B to 72B parameters, we find that mean FSI varies by over 30x across models and is largely explained by compliance failures. A fixed-effects regression shows that parseability remains a strong predictor of accuracy even after controlling for task, model, and wrapper. We argue that reporting accuracy without wrapper variance and compliance is statistically fragile, and we give practical recommendations for both benchmarking and structured-output deployments.

</details>


### [3] [Faithful, Not Corrective: Message-Format Effects in Multi-Hop Agent Relays Are Tier-Dependent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09678)
*Zayx Shawn*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Message format effects on LLM agent relay accuracy are tier-dependent: strong relays are nearly lossless regardless of format, while weak relays show large format-dependent differences (JSON key-value best), and injected errors persist across all formats with no collateral damage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two conflicting literatures (format-optimization vs. format-restriction) disagree on whether structured messages help or hurt LLM communication, but neither measures multi-hop relay fidelity where copy fidelity dominates over one-shot generation.

Method: Controlled relay testbed with 12 programmatic atomic facts re-encoded hop-by-hop across 6 hops in 5 formats (free NL, precision-instructed NL, JSON, triples, key-value), tested under two relay-capability tiers, cognitive-load condition, and paired-fork error injection.

Result: (i) Strong relay is nearly lossless under faithful-relay instructions; cognitive load raises cost 24-53% but leaves fidelity unchanged. (ii) Weak relay (1.5B) shows 8.7x across-format spread increase (2.3→20.5 points), driven by encoding toll and drift resistance. (iii) Injected errors persist 83-100% across all formats, matching true-value retention with no collateral damage.

Conclusion: Structure provides a faithful, error-localizing channel (not error-correcting), and format choice should follow the weakest relay in the pipeline.

Abstract: When LLM agents hand off information to one another, does the message format matter? Two literatures disagree: format-optimization work reports that structured messages cut cost without hurting accuracy, while format-restriction work finds that imposing structure degrades generation -- and neither measures what happens when a message traverses multiple hops, where copy fidelity, not one-shot generation, dominates. We introduce a controlled relay testbed: briefs of twelve programmatically generated atomic facts are re-encoded hop-by-hop in five formats (free NL, precision-instructed NL, JSON, triples, key-value) over six hops, scored by a fixed strong grader against programmatic ground truth, across two relay-capability tiers, a cognitive-load condition, and a paired-fork error injection. We find that message-format effects are tier-dependent. (i) Under faithful-relay instructions a strong relay is nearly lossless -- the documented "telephone-game" collapse does not occur -- and adding per-hop cognitive load leaves format-level fidelity unchanged (within +/-1.8 points) while raising generation cost by 24-53%. (ii) Under a weak (1.5B) relay the across-format spread of six-hop recall grows by a factor of 8.7 (from 2.3 to 20.5 points), driven by two opposing mechanisms -- an encoding toll paid by the rigid formats and drift resistance specific to the fixed-key JSON schema -- that flip the format ranking in transit. (iii) In a paired-fork injection, an injected wrong value, once present, persists to the final hop in 83-100% of chains in every format, closely matching each format's retention of the true value, with no detectable collateral damage to neighboring facts. Structure buys a faithful, error-localizing channel -- not an error-correcting code -- and format choice should follow the weakest relay in the pipeline.

</details>


### [4] [Boltzmann MapReduce: A Partition-Function Reduce for Forkable Sandboxes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09689)
*Yossi Eliaz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Under local asymptotic normality, the confidence density from a data chunk follows a Gibbs-Boltzmann measure with inverse temperature equal to sample size. This yields independent Boltzmann factors for disjoint chunks, making the MapReduce reduce operation a partition function whose mode is precision-weighted pooling, and frequentist consistency corresponds to the zero-temperature limit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a thermodynamic/statistical mechanics interpretation of confidence densities under local asymptotic normality (LAN), linking Bayesian and frequentist inference through the Gibbs-Boltzmann formalism.

Method: The paper derives that under LAN, the confidence density for a data chunk of size n is a Gibbs-Boltzmann measure exp{-n E(θ)} with inverse temperature β=n. It shows that for disjoint chunks, Boltzmann factors are independent, allowing the MapReduce reduce operation to be interpreted as a partition function. The mode of this partition function corresponds to precision-weighted (inverse-variance) pooling.

Result: Three exact consequences for Gaussian/linear cases (first-order otherwise): (1) disjoint chunks yield independent Boltzmann factors; (2) MapReduce reduce is a partition function Z = ∫∏_k h_k dθ with mode = precision-weighted pooling; (3) frequentist consistency corresponds to zero-temperature limit T=1/n→0.

Conclusion: The Gibbs-Boltzmann interpretation of confidence densities provides a unified framework connecting distributed inference (MapReduce), Bayesian updating, and frequentist asymptotics through a thermodynamic lens.

Abstract: To leading order under local asymptotic normality (LAN), the confidence density a worker emits over a chunk of size $n$ is a Gibbs--Boltzmann measure $\exp\{-βE(θ)\}$ whose inverse temperature is the sample size, $β=n$. Three consequences are exact in the Gaussian/linear case and first-order otherwise: disjoint chunks carry independent Boltzmann factors, so the MapReduce \emph{reduce}, read literally, is a partition function $Z=\int\prod_k h_k\,dθ$ whose mode is precision-weighted (inverse-variance) pooling; frequentist consistency is the zero-temperature limit $T=1/n\to0$

</details>


### [5] [Interpreting Latent CoT Reasoning as Dynamical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09698)
*Sabari Iyyappan Duraipandian,Shreya Sanjay Boyane,Manju Nagesh,Jerome Francis,Archana Vaidheeswaran,Kevin Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the interpretability problem of latent reasoning methods (CODI, COCONUT) by modeling latent token sequences as trajectories in representation space and applying dynamical systems analysis. The authors find that CODI behaves as a stable attractor, COCONUT as an unstable expanding system, and SIM-CoT supervision tightens both behaviors without changing underlying dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Latent reasoning methods (CODI, COCONUT) suffer from an interpretability problem—they maintain multiple superimposed candidate traces in hidden space unlike explicit Chain-of-Thought. Existing mechanistic methods describe compression, shortcuts, and superposition but fail to explain how reasoning evolves across latent steps.

Method: The authors model latent token sequences as trajectories in representation space and apply dynamical systems analysis with quantitative measures (step-to-step change, direction consistency, Lyapunov sensitivity) and qualitative projections (UMAP, DMD/PHATE).

Result: Latent CoT exhibits structured, non-random dynamics with two distinct stability classes: CODI behaves as a stable attractor, COCONUT as an unstable expanding system. SIM-CoT supervision tightens both behaviors without altering the underlying dynamics.

Conclusion: This dynamical systems framework advances the interpretability of latent CoT reasoning and provides actionable insights for improving latent reasoning performance.

Abstract: Recent latent reasoning methods, such as CODI and COCONUT, face a fundamental interpretability problem: they maintain multiple superimposed candidate traces in the hidden space at each step, unlike explicit- CoT, which follows a single transparent reasoning trace. Existing mechanistic methods show compression, shortcuts, and superposition without explaining how reasoning evolves across latent steps. To address this gap, we model latent token sequences as trajectories in representation space and apply dynamical systems analysis to characterize the evolution of reasoning. Using quantitative measures, such as step-to-step change, direction consistency, and Lyapunov sensitivity, alongside qualitative projections, such as UMAP and DMD/PHATE, we show that latent CoT exhibits structured, non-random dynamics with two distinct stability classes. CODI behaves as a stable attractor, while COCONUT behaves as an unstable expanding system, and SIM-CoT supervision tightens both behaviors without changing the underlying dynamics. This framework advances the interpretability of latent CoT reasoning dynamics and provides actionable insights for improving latent reasoning performance. Code1 and Project page2 available online.

</details>


### [6] [YUKTI: From Natural-Language Situations to Robust, Verifiable Decisions An Uncertainty-Typed Proposition IR, Assumption-Robust Pareto Frontiers, and a Regret Certificate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09706)
*Suyash Mishra*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: YUKTI replaces fragile single-objective point-estimate autoformulation with a typed-proposition graph that captures uncertainty, uses distributional Pareto hand-offs across solvers, and introduces Assumption-Robust Pareto Frontiers (ARPF) with a regret-bound metric (ρ). It cuts regret by >90% under misspecification, beats a naive point rule by 4% and status quo by 34% on real data, and incurs ~47× less regret than LLM-based single-objective pipelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current language-model-driven optimization pipelines (NL4Opt, OptiMUS, ORLM, OR-LLM-Agent) commit to a single objective with point-valued coefficients and solve once, which is fragile when those numerical assumptions are wrong — a critical failure mode for real budget, effort, or clinical allocation decisions.

Method: YUKTI builds a typed-proposition graph with shape priors, coefficient uncertainty, and provenance; routes stages to exact/nonlinear/evolutionary solvers; couples stages via a distributional Pareto hand-off; and introduces Assumption-Robust Pareto Frontiers (ARPF) that resamples assumptions under structural ε-contamination to compute ρ — a survival score with a proven bound linking it to decision regret. A synthetic data foundation (SRJANA) is also generated when no benchmark exists.

Result: Under controlled misspecification, YUKTI cuts mean and tail regret by >90% vs. a naive point plan. On a regulated commercial decision, it optimizes within a lawful action space and prices downside in euros. On 41,188 real decisions, it beats the logged status quo by 34% and a naive point rule by 4% while reducing optimizer's curse. In head-to-head, an LLM (given correct numbers) plus single-objective optimization incurs ~47× the held-out regret of YUKTI.

Conclusion: LLMs are formulators, not solvers; single-objective point-estimate optimization is fragile. YUKTI's assumption-robust, multi-solver framework yields auditable, regret-bounded decisions. However, under long-range causal coupling the forward hand-off becomes unsound, indicating a need for backward-induction causal policies.

Abstract: Language models turn a worded situation into a numeric plan, and the dominant pipelines (NL4Opt, OptiMUS, ORLM, OR-LLM-Agent) commit to a single objective and point-valued coefficients, then solve once. For decisions that allocate real budget, effort, or clinical attention, that confidence is the failure mode: every objectified number is an assumption, and a plan optimal only if the guesses are exactly right is fragile -- mimicry of computation. YUKTI changes the target of autoformulation. Its representation is a typed-proposition graph whose relationships carry shape priors, coefficient uncertainty, and provenance. YUKTI routes each stage to an exact, nonlinear, or evolutionary solver; couples stages by a distributional Pareto hand-off; and introduces Assumption-Robust Pareto Frontiers (ARPF), resampling assumptions (including structural epsilon-contamination) to score how often each action survives (rho). We prove a bound making rho an exact factor of decision regret, add auditable traceability, and synthesize a benchmark-faithful data foundation when none exists (SRJANA). We validate three ways: under controlled misspecification the robust compromise cuts mean and tail regret by over 90% versus a naive point plan; on a regulated commercial decision we optimize inside a lawful action space and price the downside in euros; and on a real public dataset of 41,188 decisions an out-of-sample backtest beats the logged status quo by 34% and a naive point rule by 4% while reducing the optimizer's curse. The solvers are standard; we claim no benchmark-SOTA win. A head-to-head shows an LLM given the correct numbers, and single-objective optimization, both incur about 47x the held-out regret of YUKTI -- an LLM is a formulator, not a solver. Under long-range causal coupling, the forward hand-off becomes unsound, locating where it must become a backward-induction causal policy.

</details>


### [7] [GES-TSP: Graph Edge Sparsification for TSP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09708)
*Tianfeng Chen,Xianyue Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GES is a learning-based graph sparsification method for Euclidean TSP that prunes up to 95-99% of edges while keeping solution gaps within 1% of optimal, by incorporating geometric structure and combinatorial optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional TSP sparsification methods rely on fixed heuristics and fail to exploit instance-specific structural information, making exact solving of large-scale TSP instances computationally expensive.

Method: Proposes Graph Edge Sparsification (GES), a learning-based approach that adaptively generates sparsification graphs for Euclidean TSP by incorporating geometric structural information and combinatorial optimization techniques.

Result: Prunes up to 95% of edges on MATILDA dataset with <1% optimality gap; on TSPLIB, pruning rate exceeds 99% while optimality gap remains below 1%.

Conclusion: Learning-based sparsification effectively reduces graph size and accelerates TSP solving while preserving near-optimal solution quality, with strong generalization to unseen benchmarks.

Abstract: Solving large-scale instances of the Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) exactly is computationally expensive. Researchers often employ graph sparsification methods to improve computational efficiency. Traditional sparsification methods typically rely on fixed heuristics and fail to fully exploit instance-specific structural information. In this paper, we propose Graph Edge Sparsification (GES), a learning-based sparsification approach for Euclidean TSP. By incorporating geometric structural information and combinatorial optimization technology, our proposed method adaptively generates a sparsification graph for different instances, significantly reducing the graph size and accelerating the solving process. Experimental results demonstrate that our sparsification method can prune up to 95% of edges on the MATILDA dataset, while keeping the solution gap within 1% of the optimal value. Moreover, our approach exhibits strong generalization capability on the TSPLIB benchmark.In some large-scale instances, the pruning rate exceeds 99%, while the optimality gap remains below 1%.

</details>


### [8] [The Verifier is the Curriculum: Execution-Gated Self-Distillation for Cross-Family Game Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09709)
*Chenyu Zhou,Qiliang Jiang,Shuning Wu,Xu Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Using a strict-launch filter (deterministic, ungameable) for rejection-sampling self-distillation on a 14B code generation model raises clean game project generation from 8.8% to 42.2% and achieves perfect 25/25 gold ceiling coverage on unseen game families, while lenient filters and gold-duplication controls fail or regress, demonstrating that verifier precision drives learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current post-training methods for code generators often optimize proxy metrics via learned judges that can be gamed, leading to score improvements without actual quality gains. The authors seek a verifiable, ungameable signal to drive genuine improvement.

Method: Rejection-sampling self-distillation under a deterministic "strict-launch" filter: generated Godot projects must launch cleanly under a headless engine. Applied iteratively over three rounds on a 14B Qwen3-14B+LoRA model, with controls including gold-duplication, count-matched decomposition, and lenient filter (BUILD check) swap.

Result: Clean generation rose from 8.8% to 42.2% per-candidate; best-of-K coverage reached 25/25 (gold ceiling). Gold-duplication regressed to 5.6%. Lenient BUILD filter erased all gains (back to base). Headless grounding signal rose monotonically, yielding 16 vs. 5 grounded candidates vs. gold-duplication.

Conclusion: The verifier acts as the curriculum: what it certifies determines what the model learns. Deterministic, ungameable filters enable reliable generalization gains, while lenient or proxy-based signals do not.

Abstract: Post-training a code generator against a learned judge can optimize proxy features that raise the score without improving the artifact. We study the opposite signal: a deterministic, judge-free, ungameable filter -- whether a generated project launches cleanly under a headless engine (strict-launch). Under this gate, rejection-sampling self-distillation compounds out-of-family generalization. On GameCraft-Bench (mapping a natural-language brief to a complete Godot project), a 14B model (Qwen3-14B+LoRA) distilled under strict-launch raises clean generation on four unseen game families from 8.8% to 42.2% per-candidate and best-of-K coverage from 18/25 to 25/25 (the gold ceiling) over three rounds, each a significant gain (p=0.0019, p<1e-4, p<1e-4). The gain is not from merely adding data: an exactly-matched gold-duplication control regresses below the base model (5.6% vs. 8.8%, p=0.019), while a count-matched decomposition splits the round-1-to-2 jump into comparable quality (+8.8pp) and quantity (+8.5pp) channels. Most directly, rerunning the loop with only the filter swapped -- the lenient BUILD check, which passes 99.9% of generations, in place of the launch gate -- erases the gain entirely (back to base, p=1e-3 vs. the launch-gated round), isolating verifier precision rather than the optimizer. A second ungameable signal, headless execution grounding, rises monotonically across rounds and yields far more grounded candidates than gold-duplication at a matched budget (16 vs. 5), confirming the gains are functional, not launch-but-empty. Game generation is a verifiable testbed for one lesson: the verifier is the curriculum -- what it certifies is what the model learns.

</details>


### [9] [Closed-Loop Control with Rule-Aligned Small Language Models and Multi-Agent Self-Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09713)
*Yuchen Wang,Javal Vyas,Tong Liu,Mehmet Mercangoz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A compact Small Language Model (Qwen2.5-1.5B) aligned via GRPO and embedded in a validator-guided correction loop achieves 91.5% action-alignment accuracy and 95% in-range rate at 3.84s inference latency for autonomous control from natural-language requirements, demonstrating feasibility for edge deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable autonomous industrial operation by creating/reconfiguring control policies from natural-language specifications with minimal manual redesign, while addressing latency and compute constraints of large cloud-based models for edge closed-loop use.

Method: Uses Qwen2.5-1.5B aligned via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), combined with an action agent, symbolic/digital-twin-style validation layer, and a reprompting agent for iterative steering toward valid actions.

Result: 91.5% average action-alignment accuracy (86.3%–100% range) at 3.84s mean inference latency across 30 randomized thermal-control simulations (500 steps each); 95% in-range rate under symbolic re-mapping.

Conclusion: SLM+validator architectures offer a practical path toward reconfigurable autonomous control at the edge.

Abstract: A key step toward autonomous industrial operation is the ability to create and reconfigure control policies from natural-language requirement specifications, with minimal or no manual redesign. In this setting, policy generation by AI agents can be a credible path when paired with a plant-aware validator (e.g., a digital twin) that can check generated candidate actions before execution. However, practical deployment is constrained by inference latency and compute footprint: large cloud-based models are often too slow, opaque, or data-sensitive for edge closed-loop use. This work investigates whether a compact Small Language Model (SLM) can be retrained for control reasoning and embedded in a validator-guided correction loop. We use a Qwen2.5-1.5B model aligned via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), combined with (i) an action agent, (ii) a symbolic/digital-twin-style validation layer, and (iii) a reprompting agent that iteratively steers outputs toward valid actions. In randomized thermal-control simulations (30 experiments with 500 steps each), the framework achieves 91.5% average action-alignment accuracy (86.3%--100% across cases) at 3.84\,s mean inference latency. Under symbolic re-mapping, it maintains a 95% in-range rate, indicating robust physical regulation despite reduced token-level agreement. These results support SLM+validator architectures as a practical path toward reconfigurable autonomous control at the edge.

</details>


### [10] [Feedback-Coupled Memory Systems in Continuous Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09714)
*Stefano Grassi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The FCMS architecture is instantiated in continuous time by defining agent updates via MBI (decentralized price mechanism) and environmental updates via CMGP (non-Markovian physical substrate), achieving Lyapunov global dissipativity under the threshold 4β² < 2ημγ², which generalizes prior discrete and physical stability conditions, with numerical and mean-field validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the axiomatic undefinedness of the agent update operator f_i and environmental update operator Ψ in the original FCMS framework by providing concrete, principled definitions grounded in economics (MBI) and non-Markovian physics (CMGP), thereby enabling a fully specified continuous-time closed-loop system.

Method: f_i is defined via Mechanism-Based Intelligence (decentralized price mechanism, economic principles); Ψ is defined via the Coupled Memory Graph Process (non-Markovian, environment as physical substrate recording trajectory history). Continuous-time FCMS is analyzed via Lyapunov methods, yielding a global dissipativity threshold 4β² < 2ημγ². Numerical simulation (N=2) and mean-field validation (N=10⁶) are conducted.

Result: The stability threshold 4β² < 2ημγ² generalizes the discrete FCMS condition 4ηβ² < γ and CMGP's physical bifurcation threshold α_c = 1/K. Violation of the threshold leads to a self-reinforcing coordination cascade. Numerical and mean-field results confirm the theoretical predictions.

Conclusion: Memory dissipation must outpace feedback gain as a universal organizing principle for closed-loop coordination in FCMS, a result that holds across discrete, continuous, and physical formulations.

Abstract: The Feedback-Coupled Memory Systems (FCMS) architecture formalizes closed-loop coordination through four abstract operators, two of which - the agent update operator $f_i$ and the environmental update operator $Ψ$ - are left axiomatically undefined in the original framework. To address this, $f_i$ is defined by Mechanism-Based Intelligence (MBI), where agents update locally through a decentralized price mechanism and economic principles, and $Ψ$ is defined by the Coupled Memory Graph Process (CMGP), a non-Markovian framework where the environment is treated as a physical substrate that records and responds to trajectory history coherently without external forcing. The resulting continuous-time FCMS instantiation achieves Lyapunov global dissipativity governed by the computable threshold $4β^2 < 2ημγ^2$. This generalizes both the discrete FCMS stability condition $4ηβ^2 < γ$ and CMGP's physical bifurcation threshold $α_c = 1/K$, confirming that memory dissipation must outpace feedback gain as a universal organizing principle. Numerical simulation with $N=2$ agents and mean-field validation at $N=10^6$ confirm the stability threshold and the self-reinforcing coordination cascade that emerges when it is violated.

</details>


### [11] [AGM-like Paraconsistent Partial Meet Abductive Expansion Operation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09729)
*Ulisses Franceschi Eliano*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents the first paraconsistent AGM-like abductive expansion operation (AGMpabd) that can incorporate contradictory explanatory hypotheses without trivialization, based on Pagnucco's work and the paraconsistent logic RCbr.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To create a paraconsistent AGM-like abductive expansion operation that can handle contradictory explanatory hypotheses without leading to trivialization or absurd epistemic states, which previous AGM operations could not address.

Method: The operation is built upon Pagnucco's abductive expansion, Aliseda's taxonomy of abductive reasoning components, and the paraconsistent logic RCbr (an LFI that is self-extensional). It provides postulates and a transitively relational partial meet construction for the new operation.

Result: A new paraconsistent abductive expansion operation (AGMpabd) is formally developed with its postulates and construction, capable of assimilating contradictory hypotheses without trivialization. However, it does not yet assign epistemic roles to paraconsistent negation and consistency operators.

Conclusion: This is the first of two papers; the current operation (AGMpabd) is the first of its kind in AGM literature. A follow-up paper will enhance the operation (AGMcircabd) to incorporate epistemic roles for paraconsistent operators of negation and consistency.

Abstract: In his 1996 doctoral thesis, Maurice Pagnucco created the first AGM-like abductive expansion operation. Taking his operation as a basis, as well as a taxonomy -- inspired by Atocha Aliseda -- responsible for highlighting and formalizing the main components of abductive reasoning, the main aim of this paper is to present a new paraconsistent AGM-like abductive expansion operation -- capable of assimilating contradictory explanatory hypotheses without trivialization and the consequent absurd epistemic state -- with its postulates and its transitively relational partial meet construction. To a large extent, the formal development presented in this paper was only made possible by the recent creation of the paraconsistent logic RCbr, an LFI (Logics of Formal Inconsistencies) that establishes properties especially relevant to belief revision contexts, in particular, the ability to be self-extensional -- i.e., to satisfy the replacement property. This is the first of two papers: the paraconsistent abductive expansion operation announced here -- which is part of a new system called AGMpabd -- despite bringing many interesting features, does not assign any relevant epistemic role to the paraconsistent operators of negation and consistency. Only in a second paper will an analogous paraconsistent abductive expansion operation -- which is part of another new system, AGMcircabd -- be enhanced in this direction. Nevertheless, to the best of my knowledge, the operation developed in this paper is the first of its kind in the AGM literature.

</details>


### [12] [Coresets Before Score Sets: Evaluation-Unsupervised Prompt Subset Selection for LLM Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09739)
*Jihan Yao,Gantavya Bhatt,Arnav Das,Peter Jin,Ke Bao,Qiaolin Yu,Khushi Bhardwaj,Chang Su,Jialei Wang,Yikai Zhu,Sugam Devare,Damon Mosk-Aoyama,Zhen Dong,Venkat Krishna Srinivasan,Yineng Zhang,Oleksii Kuchaiev,Jiantao Jiao,Banghua Zhu,Jeff Bilmes*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes evaluation-unsupervised benchmark coreset selection using submodular subset selection, specifically facility location functions on semantic embeddings, to efficiently approximate full benchmark suite evaluations with a small subset of prompts. The method scales to 35 heterogeneous benchmarks, 18 LLMs, and 61K+ prompts, outperforming 12 baselines in preserving model scores and rankings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating LLMs on large benchmark suites is computationally expensive. The authors aim to reduce this cost by selecting a small subset of prompts (coreset) that can accurately approximate the model performance and rankings obtained from the full benchmark suite.

Method: The authors propose evaluation-unsupervised coreset selection using submodular functions (DPP, submodular mutual information, facility location) on semantic prompt embeddings. They do not use any model evaluation outcomes in the selection process. The method operates at fine granularity (individual prompt level) across multiple benchmarks rather than selecting entire benchmarks.

Result: Facility location (FL) on inexpensive semantic embeddings outperforms 12 score-based and diversity-based baselines across various coreset budgets on a large-scale suite of 35 benchmarks, 18 LLMs, and 61K+ prompts. The same objective also matches or outperforms SOTA on MMLU and MTEB leaderboards for whole-benchmark selection while being computationally cheaper.

Conclusion: Submodularity, particularly facility location functions, is a strong and reliable tool for benchmark compression, working effectively in both evaluation-unsupervised (prompt-level) and score-available (benchmark-level) settings.

Abstract: We study LLM benchmark coreset selection: selecting a small subset of prompts over multiple benchmarks whose induced model scores and rankings approximate those obtained from the full benchmark suite. In evaluation-unsupervised benchmark coreset selection (our approach), the selection algorithm uses no model evaluation outcomes, and operates on a fine granularity by producing subsets of prompts over multiple benchmarks rather than producing a sub-collection of entire benchmarks. We use submodular subset selection, and we develop and evaluate many different submodular functions for this purpose, including determinantal point process (DPP) based approaches, submodular mutual information functions, and facility location-based functions. On a new large-scale suite of 35 heterogeneous benchmarks spanning five different capability categories, 18 frontier LLMs, and over 61K prompts, we find that the facility location (FL) function operating exclusively on inexpensive semantic prompt embeddings preserves LLM scores better than twelve separate score-based and diversity-based baselines, across a range of coreset budgets. Moreover, we show our proposed objective is not limited to the evaluation-unsupervised regime: in the setting where only a handful of whole benchmarks must be selected and a large amount of model scores are available, the same objective matches or outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on the MMLU and MTEB leaderboards, while being substantially cheaper to compute. Together, our results suggest that submodularity, in general, is a strong and reliable tool for benchmark compression.

</details>


### [13] [A Dynamic Scene Interaction Reasoning Framework for Scene-level Lane-Change Intention and Trajectory Prediction of Multiple Interacting Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09740)
*Joshua Kofi Asamoah,Blessing Agyei Kyem,Eugene Denteh,Armstrong Aboah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DSiGAT is a dynamic scene graph attention framework that jointly predicts lane-change intentions and future trajectories for all vehicles in a traffic scene using temporal graph-attention message passing and scene-level consistency, achieving superior accuracy and reduced collision rates on NGSIM and highD datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing lane-change prediction methods focus on a single target vehicle, while multi-agent forecasting approaches only predict future positions without explicit maneuver information, limiting scene-level understanding for safe motion planning.

Method: The scene is modeled as a time-varying interaction graph with vehicles as nodes and explicit spatial/kinematic edge features. Temporal graph-attention message passing captures evolving inter-vehicle dependencies. An intention-guided decoder links predicted maneuvers to future motion, and a scene-level consistency objective enforces compatible multi-vehicle predictions.

Result: Intention prediction accuracies of 90.12% (I-80) and 90.97% (US-101); trajectory RMSE reduced by up to 52.94% over strongest baseline; lower inter-agent collision rates and joint displacement errors.

Conclusion: The scene-focused graph attention formulation with explicit maneuver prediction and compatibility constraints yields more coherent and accurate multi-vehicle future predictions, validated through ablation, sensitivity, robustness, and qualitative analyses.

Abstract: Safe motion planning in advanced driver-assistance systems and autonomous vehicles requires an accurate understanding of how the surrounding traffic scene is likely to evolve. However, many existing lane-change prediction methods remain centered on a single target vehicle, while multi-agent forecasting approaches often describe scene evolution only through future positions and provide limited explicit information about the maneuver associated with each vehicle. This study proposes a dynamic scene graph attention framework that predicts the lane-change intention and future trajectory of every relevant vehicle within a local traffic scene. The scene is represented as a time-varying interaction graph in which vehicles are modeled as nodes and their spatial and kinematic relationships are encoded through explicit edge features. Temporal graph-attention message passing captures evolving inter-vehicle dependencies and pre-maneuver cues, while an intention-guided decoder links each predicted maneuver to its corresponding future motion. A scene-level consistency objective further encourages compatible multi-vehicle futures. Experiments on the NGSIM I-80, NGSIM US-101, and highD datasets demonstrate consistent improvements over competing baselines. DSiGAT achieves intention prediction accuracies of 90.12% and 90.97% on NGSIM I-80 and US-101, respectively, and reduces trajectory RMSE by up to 52.94% relative to the strongest baseline. It also produces lower inter-agent collision rates and joint displacement errors, indicating more coherent scene-level predictions. Ablation, sensitivity, robustness, and qualitative analyses further validate the contribution of the proposed components and the effectiveness of the scene-focused formulation.

</details>


### [14] [Scaffolding the Strategist: Architecture-Dependent Reasoning Interventions in Hotelling Spatial Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09743)
*Pratyush Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Structured reasoning interventions produce a crossover interaction with model architecture: commitment scaffolding improves standard models but degrades reasoning-optimized models, while principled separation shows the opposite effect. Adversarial stress-testing harms both, especially the reasoning model. A declarative-procedural gap persists, with separation closing it only for the reasoning model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether structured reasoning interventions improve strategic economic reasoning in LLMs, and whether their effectiveness depends on model architecture (standard instruction-following vs. reasoning-optimized).

Method: Using Hotelling's linear city model, the authors evaluated GPT-4.1-mini (standard) and GPT-5-mini (reasoning-optimized) under five conditions (baseline + four scaffolding interventions), across eight questions (deductive/abductive), three prompt framings, and three repetitions, totaling 720 judged responses.

Result: Found a significant crossover interaction: commitment scaffolding helped the standard model (+0.21) but hurt the reasoning model (-0.63); principled separation helped the reasoning model (+0.31) but hurt the standard model (-0.40). Adversarial stress-testing degraded both models, with 2.6× greater harm to the reasoning model. A declarative-procedural gap was identified; separation closed it for the reasoning model but no intervention helped the standard model.

Conclusion: Structured reasoning interventions interact strongly with model architecture, with no one-size-fits-all benefit. The declarative-procedural gap suggests models may "know" correct strategies without being able to execute them, and this gap's closure depends on both intervention type and model design.

Abstract: We investigate whether structured reasoning interventions improve the strategic economic reasoning of large language models, and whether their effects depend on model architecture. Using Hotelling's linear city model as a diagnostic vehicle, we evaluate GPT-4.1-mini (a standard instruction-following model) and GPT-5-mini (a reasoning-optimized model) under five conditions - an unscaffolded baseline and four reasoning interventions - across eight questions spanning deductive and abductive reasoning, three prompt framings, and three repetitions per condition, yielding 720 individually judged responses. We find a statistically significant crossover interaction between scaffolding type and model architecture ($t(7) = 4.79$, $p = 0.002$, $d = 1.69$): commitment scaffolding improves the standard model ($+0.21$) while degrading the reasoning model ($-0.63$), and principled separation shows the opposite pattern ($-0.40$ vs. $+0.31$). Both crossovers are individually significant (commitment: $p = 0.040$; separation: $p = 0.002$) and hold across all eight questions with 7/8 directional consistency. Adversarial stress-testing harms both models, with $2.6\times$ greater degradation for the reasoning model ($-1.47$ vs. $-0.57$; $p = 0.038$), and the damage correlates negatively with baseline difficulty ($R^2 = 0.36$, $p = 0.014$). We further document a persistent declarative-procedural gap in which both models identify correct strategies at rates far exceeding their ability to execute them; separation fully closes this gap for the reasoning model while no intervention helps the standard model.

</details>


### [15] [A Theory of Least Autonomy in AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09744)
*Christophe Parisel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that the traditional least privilege principle is insufficient for agentic AI systems, and proposes "least autonomy" as a generalization. It develops a formal theory including a compositional blast radius metric, a directed agent influence graph with policy threshold calibration, and a collusion predicate for detecting authorization composition and cross-domain capability amplification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Least privilege is insufficient for agentic AI systems because agents can combine, approve, and amplify permissions across workflows and system boundaries, not merely hold them.

Method: (1) Define compositional blast radius d(a,b) using ultrametric tree and lattice-valued labels; (2) Define directed agent influence graph G(θ) with shared-resource and A2A communication edges, conditioned on policy threshold θ; (3) Define collusion predicate over graph reachability.

Result: A formal theory of least autonomy with blast radius measurement, influence graph with threshold calibration, and collusion detection for authorization composition and capability amplification.

Conclusion: Least autonomy is the appropriate generalization of least privilege for agentic AI systems, supported by a rigorous formal framework.

Abstract: Least privilege, the principle that an identity should hold only the permissions strictly required for its task, has been a foundational primitive of access control for decades. We argue that this principle is insufficient for agentic AI systems, which do not merely hold permissions but can combine, approve, and amplify them across workflows and system boundaries.
  We propose least autonomy as an appropriate generalization and develop a formal theory. First, we define a compositional blast radius d(a,b) that measures structural separation between actions in an enterprise hierarchy, combining an ultrametric tree with lattice-valued confidentiality, integrity, and control-context labels. Second, we define a directed agent influence graph G(theta). An arc from U to V requires a directed shared-resource write-to-read meeting or a conservative undirected agent-to-agent (A2A) communication meeting, and a meeting-conditioned influence potential at or above an externally selected policy threshold theta. A catalogue-radius profile supports calibration and audit of theta. Finally, we define a collusion predicate over graph reachability that detects authorization composition, decision manipulation, and cross-domain capability composition.

</details>


### [16] [SupplyNetPy: An Open-Source Python Library for High-Fidelity Modeling and Simulation of Arbitrary Supply Chain and Inventory Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09745)
*Tushar Lone,Neha Karanjkar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SupplyNetPy is an open-source Python library for discrete-event simulation of multi-echelon supply chain networks, supporting perishable inventory, disruptions, stochastic demand/lead times, and extensible components, with validation against benchmarks, commercial tools, and case studies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable programmatic generation and simulation of complex supply chain models for design-space exploration, what-if analysis, training data generation, and digital twins, filling a gap for an open-source, well-documented, and extensible Python library.

Method: Users describe supply chains as graphs with node/link attributes; the library handles discrete-event simulation with support for multi-echelon structures, multiple replenishment policies, perishable inventory, node disruptions, and stochastic demand/lead times. All components are extensible via inheritance.

Result: Validated against analytical benchmarks, a commercial tool, and a published case study; provides simulation logs and node/network-level performance reports.

Conclusion: SupplyNetPy offers a flexible, validated open-source tool for supply chain simulation with programmatic model generation capabilities.

Abstract: This paper introduces SupplyNetPy, an open-source, well-documented Python library for modeling and discrete-event simulation of supply chain networks with arbitrary multi-echelon structures. It supports multiple replenishment policies, perishable inventory, node disruptions, and stochastic demand and lead times. All components are extensible via inheritance. Users describe a supply chain as a graph with node and link attributes, while the library handles simulation, providing logs and extensive node and network level performance reports. This paper presents the motivation, design, key features, and architecture of SupplyNetPy, along with detailed validation results (against analytical benchmarks, a commercial tool, and a published case study). A key motivation behind SupplyNetPy's development is programmatic generation and simulation of complex models, enabling design-space exploration, what-if analysis, training data generation, and supply chain digital twins.

</details>


### [17] [Replicating Belief, Not Bits: Epistemic State Replication for Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09748)
*Jun He,Deying Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Epistemic State Replication (ESR), a belief-replication layer for agentic distributed systems that shifts from bitwise state agreement to semantic belief agreement, formalized through epistemic node states, Semantic Linearizability, Bounded Eventual Coherence, and Verifiable Semantic Rollbacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical State Machine Replication (SMR) assumes deterministic, bitwise identical replicas, which is insufficient for agentic distributed systems where stochastic, model-driven agents (e.g., LLMs) may diverge in reasoning yet reach semantically correct decisions. Forcing bitwise agreement degrades flexibility and performance.

Method: Formalizes ESR with epistemic node state K=(L,B) separating deterministic evidence log (L) from stochastic belief lineage (B). Defines Semantic Linearizability (semantic compatibility within verifier bounds), Bounded Eventual Coherence (bounds on semantic divergence), structured epistemic deltas for propagating derived insights, and Verifiable Semantic Rollbacks for pruning faulty premises.

Result: Prototype implementation and preliminary simulations show feasibility under stated assumptions and illustrate reductions in secondary cognitive faults.

Conclusion: Belief-level agreement (ESR) is a viable alternative to bitwise SMR for stochastic, agentic distributed systems, improving execution flexibility while maintaining safety guarantees through semantic compatibility metrics.

Abstract: In distributed systems, the classical State Machine Replication (SMR) model assumes that correct replicas execute deterministic transitions to yield identical bitwise states. However, the rise of agentic distributed systems -- where autonomous, stochastic, and model-driven agents orchestrate infrastructure -- presents scenarios where deterministic, bitwise replication is insufficient. Replicas operating with generative models may exhibit divergent reasoning paths, summaries, and token boundaries, yet reach semantically equivalent and correct operational decisions. Forcing bitwise agreement across these stochastic participants degrades execution flexibility, induces context amnesia, and limits performance.
  We argue that in such settings replicas should agree on belief, not bits. We propose Epistemic State Replication (ESR), a belief-replication layer for agentic distributed systems that shifts the replication boundary from data visibility to knowledge visibility. We formalize the epistemic node state as a pair K = (L, B) separating the deterministic, immutable evidence log (L) from the stochastic, evolving belief lineage (B). To govern execution safety, we define Semantic Linearizability, which requires operations to reflect the latest committed operational meaning within a verifier-bounded semantic compatibility metric, and Bounded Eventual Coherence, which bounds expected semantic divergence under fair delivery, monotonic evidence, bounded verifier disturbance, and a contractive graft operator. We outline protocols for propagating derived insights using structured epistemic deltas, and formalize Verifiable Semantic Rollbacks to prune faulty premises from belief lineages without inducing context amnesia. We prototype ESR and report preliminary simulation results that show feasibility under the stated assumptions and illustrate reductions in secondary cognitive faults.

</details>


### [18] [Task-Conditioned Synthetic Data Generation for Improving Machine Learning Performance in Agricultural Prediction Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09751)
*Hamid Ebrahimy,Moritz Lucas,Martin Atzmueller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a Task-Conditioned Synthetic Data Generation (TCSDG) algorithm that combines a Bayesian Network generator with a transformer-based tabular foundation model (TabICL) to improve machine learning performance in agricultural prediction tasks. TCSDG outperformed six benchmark SDG algorithms across crop yield prediction and crop type classification tasks at twelve study sites, achieving performance improvements in 89% of classification and 74% of regression experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ML algorithms for agricultural variable estimation are constrained by limited or incomplete reference data, as training data quantity and quality strongly influence ML performance. Synthetic Data Generation offers a practical solution to address this data scarcity issue.

Method: The TCSDG algorithm pairs a Bayesian Network generator with a transformer-based tabular foundation model (TabICL), leveraging teacher-student knowledge transfer and in-context learning for tabular data. It was evaluated on crop yield prediction and crop type classification across twelve study sites, two training-data fractions, four multiplication ratios, and three predictive ML algorithms, compared against six benchmark SDG algorithms.

Result: TCSDG improved ML performance in 89% of crop type classification experiments and 74% of crop yield prediction experiments. It substantially outperformed all six benchmark SDG algorithms and was the only method to consistently improve ML performance across both tasks at the aggregate level.

Conclusion: Carefully designed synthetic data can improve ML performance in precision-agriculture applications. TCSDG provides a practical and extensible framework for generating synthetic data to support downstream ML agricultural prediction. The implementation is publicly available as open source.

Abstract: Machine Learning (ML) algorithms have been widely used to estimate agricultural variables across diverse contexts. However, because the quantity and quality of training data strongly influence performance of ML algorithms, their use can be constrained by limited or incomplete reference data. Synthetic Data Generation (SDG) offers a practical approach to address this issue by producing artificial but realistic samples that preserve key characteristics of the original data. Building on teacher-student knowledge transfer and in-context learning for tabular data, this study proposes a Task-Conditioned SDG (TCSDG) algorithm that pairs a Bayesian Network generator with a transformer-based tabular foundation model (TabICL). The proposed algorithm was evaluated on two agricultural prediction tasks: crop yield prediction and crop type classification. Six benchmark SDG algorithms were also utilized to compare their performance with that of TCSDG. Across twelve study sites, two training-data fractions, four multiplication ratios, and three predictive ML algorithms, augmenting the original data with TCSDG-generated synthetic data improved ML performance in 89% of the crop type classification experiments and 74% of the crop yield prediction experiments. TCSDG also substantially outperformed benchmark SDG algorithms and was the only method to consistently improve ML performance across both tasks at the aggregate level. The study demonstrates that carefully designed and processed synthetic data can improve ML performance in precision-agriculture applications. TCSDG offers a practical and extensible framework for generating synthetic data that supports downstream ML agricultural prediction. The full implementation of TCSDG is publicly available as open source at https://github.com/HamidEbrahimy/TCSDG.

</details>


### [19] [LegalFarePlan: A Label-Setting Framework for Fare-Transparent Urban Rail Route Planning under Non-Additive Fare Rules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09755)
*Tanghui Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LegalFarePlan is a fare-transparent route-planning framework that models legal exit-and-reentry operations to reduce fares under non-additive urban rail fare systems, achieving positive fare reductions for 71.11% of OD pairs in a semi-synthetic benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Urban rail fare systems may be non-additive, where the fare for a single journey can differ from the sum of fares over multiple legally separated legs. Current route planners lack transparency in modeling such fare structures, motivating a framework that explicitly models legal exit-and-reentry as auditable constraints.

Method: The paper proposes LegalFarePlan, which models transit network, fare function, transfer rules, station-level exit/re-entry costs, extra-time budget, and split limit. It implements Dijkstra shortest-time and direct route-planner baselines, a greedy split heuristic, bounded exact label-setting, and Pareto-frontier search.

Result: On a 57-station semi-synthetic benchmark with 360 OD pairs, bounded exact search identified positive modeled fare reductions for 71.11% of OD pairs, with mean reduction 3.78 and maximum reduction 9.0 synthetic fare units under a 45-minute extra-time budget.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate method behavior and reproducibility, but are not empirical conclusions about any real transit operator.

Abstract: Urban rail fare systems may be non-additive: the fare of a single paid journey from an origin to a destination can differ from the sum of fares over multiple legally separated journey legs. This paper presents LegalFarePlan, a fare-transparent route-planning framework that models legal exit-and-reentry operations as explicit, auditable constraints. Given a transit network, fare function, transfer rules, station-level exit/re-entry costs, an extra-time budget, and a split limit, the planner computes explainable route plans over paid journey segments. The artifact implements Dijkstra shortest-time and direct route-planner baselines, a greedy split heuristic, bounded exact label-setting, and Pareto-frontier search. Evaluation uses controlled synthetic data and a 57-station semi-synthetic benchmark with 360 OD pairs. On the semi-synthetic benchmark, bounded exact search identifies positive modeled fare reductions for 71.11% of OD pairs, with mean reduction 3.78 and maximum reduction 9.0 synthetic fare units under a 45-minute extra-time budget. These results demonstrate method behavior and reproducibility; they are not empirical conclusions about MTR or any transit operator.

</details>


### [20] [BatteryLake: Agentic, Physics-Grounded Curation of Heterogeneous Battery Aging Data and Benchmarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09762)
*Tianwen Zhu,Hao Wang,Yonggang Wen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BatteryLake is a governed data lakehouse that converts raw public battery aging data into benchmark-ready assets via an LLM-agent-based, physics-grounded curation framework, featuring automated metadata extraction with evidence grounding, human-in-the-loop verification with 26 validation rules, and an open benchmark of 41 datasets with standardized SOH/RUL tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public battery aging datasets are hindered by inconsistent formats, unclear schemas, and scattered metadata; current curation is manual and non-reproducible, while generic data tools lack domain-specific semantics for electrochemical time-series data.

Method: (1) LLM agents extract metadata and synthesize dataset-specific converters, grounding outputs in verbatim evidence and abstaining when unsupported; (2) Human-in-the-loop verification via selective prediction gated by 26 schema, statistical, and physical-plausibility rules; (3) Release of an open benchmark with 41 datasets, standardized SOH/RUL tasks, three split protocols, and eight baseline model families.

Result: An open benchmark of 41 datasets from over 25 institutions, with standardized SOH and RUL tasks, three split protocols, and eight baseline model families.

Conclusion: BatteryLake provides a reproducible, physics-grounded, and governed pipeline for curating battery aging data, delivering benchmark-ready assets to the community.

Abstract: Public battery aging datasets are a critical asset for advanced health management, but their practical use is often limited by inconsistent formats, unclear schemas, and metadata scattered across repositories and publications. Current curation remains largely manual and hard to reproduce, while general-purpose data integration tools miss the domain-specific semantics of electrochemical time-series data. We present BatteryLake, a governed data lakehouse that turns raw public battery data into benchmark-ready assets through an agentic, physics-grounded curation framework, with three contributions. First, LLM agents extract metadata and synthesize dataset-specific converters, grounding every output in verbatim evidence and abstaining when none supports a value. Second, a human-in-the-loop mechanism frames verification as selective prediction and gates admitted data through 26 schema, statistical, and physical-plausibility rules. Third, we release an open benchmark of 41 datasets from over 25 institutions, with standardized SOH and RUL tasks, three split protocols, and eight baseline model families. The platform, benchmark, and curation protocol are publicly available at https://tianwen1209.github.io/batterylake/.

</details>


### [21] [How Much Does Correctness Cost? Budgeted Placement of Strong Correctors in a Weak Multi-Agent Swarm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09765)
*Igor Itkin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies how to optimally place a limited number of costly "oracle" correctors of varying strengths on a graph to steer a swarm of unreliable agents toward correct consensus, showing submodularity of the coherence measure H(R) and deriving budget-correctness frontiers for different graph structures and oracle cost functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine the minimal cost and optimal placement strategy for oracle correctors needed to guide a cheap swarm of unreliable agents toward correct consensus, balancing the trade-off between spending on few strong oracles versus many medium-strength ones.

Method: Models the swarm as consensus on a graph where each oracle pins one node toward truth at a cost-coupled, concave strength; uses coherence H(R)=tr M(R)^{-1} as quality measure; proves submodularity of H even with heterogeneous oracle strengths; derives closed-form budget-correctness frontier B*(eps) for complete graphs and minimal oracle count k* for equal-cost oracles; empirically measures cost-quality curvature on Qwen3 models (0.6-32B) for math verification vs. emergent code tracing.

Result: H is submodular (diminishing returns) with heterogeneous oracle strengths, enabling a cost-benefit greedy algorithm within 1-1/e of optimal. The budget-correctness frontier is derived in closed form for complete graphs. The curvature of the cost-quality law is task-dependent: concave for math verification (favoring spreading oracles) and convex for emergent code tracing (favoring concentrated oracles).

Conclusion: Optimal oracle placement depends on task-specific cost-quality curvature: diminishing returns favor spreading oracles across the swarm, while sharply increasing returns favor concentrating budget on fewer strong oracles. The verdict is genuinely task-dependent, as shown empirically on the Qwen3 model ladder.

Abstract: A cheap swarm of unreliable agents can be steered to a correct consensus by a few strong, expensive "oracle" correctors. We ask how much one must spend, and where to place the oracles. We model the swarm as a consensus on a graph in which each oracle pins one node toward the truth at a cost-coupled, concave strength, and measure quality by the coherence H(R)=tr M(R)^{-1}. Our first result is that H stays submodular (each added oracle helps less than the last) even when the oracles differ in strength, so a cost-benefit greedy comes within 1-1/e of the best placement at any budget. Inverting the budget gives the budget-correctness frontier B*(eps), the least spend that guarantees an eps-correct consensus: closed-form on the complete graph, and a minimal oracle count k* when oracles cost the same. Whether a budget then buys a few strong oracles or many medium onese curvature of the cost-quality law: diminishing returns favour spreadsharply increasion. Measured onthe Qwen3 ladder (0.6-32B), the law is concave for math verificatio convex foremergent code tracing, so the verdict is genuinely task-dependent.https://github.com/YehudaItkin/budgeted-oracle-placemen

</details>


### [22] [Norm Enforcement for AI Agents: Robustly Shaping Behavior in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09766)
*Yaowen Ye,Jacob Steinhardt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies norm enforcement mechanisms for language model agents in competitive multi-agent environments, finding that simple enforcement mechanisms are exploitable by misaligned agents. The authors develop robust mechanisms combining reliability estimation over time with escalating penalties for repeated violations, showing these resist exploitation across three simulated environments while maintaining comparable or lower costs than baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents pursuing diverse goals in shared environments may engage in collectively harmful behaviors (e.g., marketing agents posting misleading content for engagement). Drawing from human societies' use of norms and enforcement, the authors aim to design robust norm enforcement mechanisms for language model agents that cannot be exploited for competitive advantage.

Method: The paper identifies two key ingredients for robust norm enforcement: (1) estimating each agent's reliability over time, and (2) updating this estimate with escalating penalties for repeated misbehavior. These mechanisms are tested across three simulated environments with varied agent populations.

Result: Simple enforcement mechanisms are exploited by misaligned agents even without explicit training or prompting to do so. Mechanisms incorporating reliability estimation and escalating penalties resist exploitation while penalizing norm violations at comparable or lower cost than baselines.

Conclusion: Norm enforcement mechanisms can serve as scalable levers for shaping agent behavior, but only when designed to anticipate becoming part of the system they govern — i.e., robust to strategic exploitation by the agents they are meant to constrain.

Abstract: AI agents are increasingly deployed in shared environments where they pursue diverse goals and compete for rewards. This multi-agent competition can lead to behaviors that serve individual gains at collective cost -- for instance, marketing agents may post misleading content as a result of competing for engagement on social media. Human societies address such problems through norms that constrain acceptable behavior, supported by enforcement mechanisms that detect and penalize violations. Motivated by this, we study norm enforcement mechanisms for language model agents. We find that simple enforcement mechanisms are exploited by misaligned agents for competitive advantage, even when they are not explicitly trained or prompted to do so. We thus turn our attention to designing more robust mechanisms, and identify two key ingredients: estimating each agent's reliability over time, and updating this estimate with escalating penalties for repeated misbehavior. Across three simulated environments and a variety of agent populations, mechanisms built on these principles resist exploitation, while still penalizing norm violations at comparable or lower cost than baselines. Our results position norm enforcement mechanisms as scalable levers for shaping agents' behavior, but only when designed to anticipate becoming part of the system they govern. Our code and data are available at https://yaowenye.com/norm-enforcement.

</details>


### [23] [Verification of Adaptive Agentic Controllers through Finite Rule Revision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09770)
*Roberto Garrone*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a bounded verification protocol for adaptive agentic controllers using finite symbolic rules, diagnostic predicates, explanation logs, and held-out re-evaluation. It classifies controller failures as detectable, locally repairable (via rule addition/deletion/priority revision), or rejectable, demonstrated on a financially constrained inventory-control benchmark where some failures are non-repairable, others violate guardrails, and a one-step repair is achieved via rule removal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial agentic AI systems exhibit a gap between prototype capability and production deployment; adaptive agents generate plausible yet difficult-to-verify outputs under non-determinism, confidentiality, limited context, and weak observability.

Method: Formulates a bounded verification protocol treating the controller as a finite revisable object with diagnostic failures mapped to predefined rule-level edits (addition, deletion, priority revision). Repaired controllers are evaluated on held-out simulation seeds or cloned initial states.

Result: Experiments in a financially constrained inventory-control benchmark show three outcomes: non-repairable resource-induced failures, partial repairs rejected due to threshold/guardrail violations, and a local one-step repair of an order-volatility failure via smoothing rule removal.

Conclusion: Provides a simulation-compatible procedure for testing whether controller-level failures can be made observable, explainable, locally revisable, and empirically re-tested under controlled conditions, contributing a methodological framework for bridging the deployment gap.

Abstract: Industrial agentic AI systems increasingly exhibit a gap between prototype capability and production deployment. In particular, adaptive agents may generate plausible outputs while remaining difficult to verify under non-determinism, confidentiality constraints, limited context, and weak observability. This paper formulates a bounded verification protocol for adaptive agentic controllers represented by finite symbolic rules, explicit diagnostic predicates, explanation logs, and held-out re-evaluation. The central research question is: when an adaptive agentic controller is represented through finite rules, explicit diagnostic predicates, explanation logs, and held-out re-evaluation, which classes of controller failure can be detected, locally repaired, or rejected without relying on unrestricted human-in-the-loop judgment? The proposed framework treats the controller as a finite revisable object. Diagnostic failures are mapped to predefined rule-level edits, including rule addition, rule deletion, and priority revision. Repaired controllers are then evaluated on held-out simulation seeds or cloned initial states. Experiments in a stylized financially constrained inventory-control benchmark show three outcomes: resource-induced failures that remain non-repairable by one rule edit, partial repairs that are rejected because they violate thresholds or guardrails, and a local one-step repair of an order-volatility failure induced by removing a smoothing rule. The contribution is methodological and provides a simulation-compatible procedure for testing whether specific controller-level failures can be made observable, explainable, locally revisable, and empirically re-tested under controlled conditions.

</details>


### [24] [EvoCUA-1.5: Online Reinforcement Learning for Multi-turn Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09773)
*Mianqiu Huang,Taofeng Xue,Chong Peng,Jinrui Ding,Sicheng Fan,Jiale Hong,Yufei Gao,Xiaocheng Zhang,Linsen Guo,Xin Yang,Dengchang Zhao,Yuchen Xie,Peng Pei,Xunliang Xie,Xipeng Qiu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EvoCUA-1.5 extends computer-use agents from offline to online RL, using Step-Level Policy Optimization (STEPO), policy-aware filtering, Dynamic Tri-Adaptive Curriculum (DTAC), and asynchronous infrastructure to handle multi-turn interaction challenges, achieving 63.2% success on OSWorld-Verified and outperforming comparable open-weight baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static offline traces cannot cover the causal feedback loop of real computer use where each action changes screen state, future action space, and recovery options, motivating the shift from imitation learning/offline refinement to online RL with executable environments.

Method: EvoCUA-1.5 introduces: (1) Step-Level Policy Optimization (STEPO) for trajectory-level advantage balance after step decomposition; (2) policy-aware filtering and pass-rate calibration over verifiable tasks; (3) Dynamic Tri-Adaptive Curriculum (DTAC) combining learnable tasks, difficult positive replay, and controlled infeasible-task exposure; (4) fully asynchronous RL infrastructure with staleness control and mini-group batching.

Result: Achieves 63.2% success on OSWorld-Verified, outperforming comparable 32B/35B-scale open-weight baselines and approaching models with significantly larger parameter counts.

Conclusion: EvoCUA-1.5 provides a practical framework for scaling online RL in multi-turn computer-use agents, demonstrating that the proposed components improve training stability and downstream performance.

Abstract: Computer-use agents must solve long-horizon tasks through repeated interaction with partially observable, multimodal desktop environments. Although imitation learning and offline trajectory refinement provide strong priors, static traces cannot cover the causal feedback loop of real computer use: each action changes the screen state, future action space, and recovery options. EvoCUA-1.5 extends self-evolving computer-use agents from offline experience learning to online reinforcement learning, where policies interact with executable sandbox environments and improve from verifiable task outcomes. Online RL in this setting requires more than directly reusing single-turn language-RL recipes. Multi-turn interaction introduces context-managed observations, sparse terminal rewards, variable-length trajectories, and slow environment feedback. EvoCUA-1.5 addresses these challenges with Step-Level Policy Optimization (STEPO), which preserves trajectory-level advantage balance after decomposition into step-level samples; policy-aware filtering and pass-rate calibration over verifiable synthesized tasks; Dynamic Tri-Adaptive Curriculum (DTAC), which combines learnable tasks, difficult positive replay, and controlled infeasible-task exposure; and a fully asynchronous RL infrastructure with staleness control and mini-group batching. Experiments show that these components improve training stability and downstream performance. EvoCUA-1.5 achieves 63.2\% success on OSWorld-Verified, outperforming comparable 32B/35B-scale open-weight baselines and even approaching models with significantly larger parameter counts. Overall, EvoCUA-1.5 provides a practical framework for scaling online RL in multi-turn computer-use agents.

</details>


### [25] [From Patterns to Maze Structures: SMT-Based Path Synthesis and 2D/3D Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09781)
*Shengyi Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents an SMT-based pipeline that encodes maze path-synthesis as global constraints (adjacency, continuity, pattern-constrained coverage), solving fixed-bound instances in one call to produce planar self-avoiding routes or layered traversals with over-under crossings, serving as scaffolds for planar and 3D woven maze constructions; it extends a Bridges 2026 paper with additional SMT-LIB examples and detailed construction methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable automated synthesis of maze structures from arbitrary input patterns (text/shapes) where the central path-planning problem is solved declaratively via SMT rather than procedural algorithms, allowing both planar and 3D woven maze realizations.

Method: The path-synthesis problem is encoded as SMT constraints covering adjacency, continuity, and pattern-constrained coverage; solved in a single SMT solver call per fixed-bound instance; the resulting path is used as a scaffold for constructing either planar mazes or 3D woven mazes with specified over-under crossings.

Result: The SMT-based pipeline successfully synthesizes planar self-avoiding routes and layered traversals with prescribed over-under crossings from input patterns, enabling concrete planar and three-dimensional maze constructions.

Conclusion: SMT-based declarative constraint solving provides a flexible and sound approach for maze path synthesis from arbitrary patterns, with natural extension to 3D woven maze constructions.

Abstract: We present a pipeline for constructing maze structures from input patterns such as text or shapes. The central path-synthesis problem is encoded in Satisfiability Modulo Theories as global constraints on adjacency, continuity, and pattern-constrained coverage, allowing each fixed-bound instance to be solved in one call. The resulting path is either a planar, self-avoiding route or a layered traversal with prescribed over--under crossings, and it serves as a scaffold for constructing planar mazes and three-dimensional realizations of woven mazes. This report extends the published Bridges 2026 conference paper with more representative SMT-LIB examples and a fuller account of how synthesized paths become concrete maze constructions in planar and three-dimensional form.

</details>


### [26] [Length Penalties Make Chain-of-Thought Less Monitorable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09786)
*Bryce Little*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Length-penalized reinforcement learning compresses chain-of-thought reasoning, preserving answer accuracy while making the influences behind answers harder to detect. Compression preferentially removes cues a monitor needs to see what drove the answer, revealing a compression-monitorability tradeoff.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether length-penalized reinforcement learning, which shortens chain-of-thought reasoning, also hides the true influences driving model answers, and to assess whether token-accuracy evaluations miss this hidden influence.

Method: Train Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-14B variants with different target chain lengths under length-penalized RL. Evaluate with biasing-hint interventions on MMLU-Pro-R and four transfer benchmarks. Randomly delete sentences from uncompressed baseline chains for length-matching comparison.

Result: Compression sharply reduces reasoning tokens, preserves most accuracy, but leaves hint influence near baseline. Lower-bound faithfulness drops to 63.1% (14B) and 69.4% (4B) of baseline. Monitor catch rate falls from 69%→49% (14B) and 60%→48% (4B). Compressed chains disclose the hint 7-35 percentage points less often than length-matched random baseline deletions.
