Conventional simulation improves repeatability, but an offfine simulator alone cannot continuously connect physical trafffc states, virtual reconstruction, algorithm evaluation, and scenario evolution. This paper proposes a risk-ffeld enhanced closed-loop digital twin framework for autonomous driving safety validation. The framework integrates physical data acquisition, data synchronization, virtual twin reconstruction, risk-aware scenario generation, autonomous driving algorithm evaluation, and safety analysis. A driving risk ffeld is introduced as a uniffed intermediate representation to describe obstacle, lane-departure, road-boundary, time-to-collision, and comfort-related risks around the ego vehicle. The risk ffeld ranks high-risk scenarios in the digital twin scenario library and provides dense safety guidance for reinforcement learning-based driving policies. A simulation-style evaluation protocol is designed to compare conventional reinforcement learning baselines, risk-penalty baselines, and the proposed risk-ffeld guided method. The study indicates that embedding explicit risk structure into digital twins can make autonomous driving validation more targeted, interpretable, and reusable, while its practical effectiveness remains bounded by model ffdelity, risk calibration, and sim-to-real transfer.

</details>


### [125] [Maximizing Human Efficiency in Large-Scale Robot Post-Training via VLAC-Cut Guided Pipeline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09776)
*Shaopeng Zhai,Qi Zhang,Tianyi Zhang,Haoran Zhang,Fuxian Huang,Zhanhui Lin,Zijun Xu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes a human-efficient post-training pipeline for VLA models that divides labor between Teleoperators (remote interventions, recovery demos) and Floor Operators (monitoring, resets), plus uses VLAC-CUT for automatic rollout curation to filter autonomous trajectory segments. Achieves 80–95% success rates and 1.7×–4.2× throughput improvement across four tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multiple rounds of post-training are needed to iteratively address weaknesses in VLA models, but human labor is expensive and limited. The goal is to maximize policy improvement and task throughput per unit of human time and effort.

Method: (1) Specialized division of labor: Teleoperator handles high-value remote interventions/recovery, Floor Operator monitors robots and triggers takeovers. (2) VLAC-CUT: automatic curation tool that segments trajectories into progress-making, idle, failure-inducing, and recovery portions, filtering harmful/uninformative segments. (3) Curated rollout data combined with HITL data for iterative post-training rounds.

Result: Across four real-world tasks, final policies achieve 80–95% success rates; task throughput improves 1.7×–4.2× over base model. VLAC-CUT guided rollout reuse outperforms HITL-only training under the same human-intervention budget.

Conclusion: The proposed human-efficient post-training pipeline with role specialization and automatic rollout curation enables a small number of operators to supervise multiple robots effectively, improving both success rate and throughput while reducing human labor costs.

Abstract: When adapting Vision Language Action (VLA) models to downstream tasks, multiple rounds of post training are required because a single round of data cannot resolve all issues, making continuous iterations necessary to progressively address the weaknesses exposed in previous rounds. In this report, we aim to maximize human efficiency during post-training, defined as the policy improvement and task throughput achieved per unit of human labor and time.
  We propose a human-efficient post-training pipeline that enables a small number of human operators to supervise multiple robots. The pipeline is built around a specialized division of labor: a trained Teleoperator focuses on high-value remote interventions and recovery demonstrations, while a Floor Operator monitors multiple robots, triggers takeovers, and performs physical resets. This role specialization reduces task switching, lowers operator training costs, and allows limited human labor to supervise more robot interaction across a larger fleet. To improve data utilization efficiency, we introduce VLAC-CUT as an automatic rollout curation tool. It segments autonomous robot trajectories into progress-making, idle, failure-inducing, and recovery portions, preserving useful segments while filtering harmful or uninformative ones. The curated rollout data are combined with Human-in-the-Loop data for the next post-training round. We validate the proposed pipeline on four real-world manipulation tasks. Across iterative post-training rounds, the final policies achieve 80\%--95\% success rates and improve task throughput by 1.7$\times$--4.2$\times$ over the base model. Under the same human-intervention budget, VLAC-CUT guided rollout reuse outperforms HITL-only training in both success rate and throughput.

</details>


### [126] [A Comprehensive Survey and Systematic Real-World Evaluation of Embodied Vision-and-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09792)
*Liuyi Wang,Kai Sheng,Zongtao He,Jinlong Li,Yongrui Qin,Haojie Dai,Xiangyi Wang,Jingwei Yang,Qingqing Yan,Chengju Liu,Qijun Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This survey comprehensively reviews Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) research, organizing methods along action paradigms (hierarchical vs. monolithic) and model paradigms (discriminative vs. generative). Real-world evaluation on a physical robot across ten scenes reveals a significant simulation-to-reality performance gap (e.g., 61% simulation vs. 22% real-world for a monolithic RGB-only method), with hierarchical frameworks showing superior robustness (51% real-world success). Key challenges in perception, decision-making, and control are highlighted.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing autonomous navigation systems rely on structured models and strong priors, limiting robustness in open real-world environments. Although VLN has attracted increasing attention, systematic methodological taxonomy and real-world validation remain limited, motivating this comprehensive survey and physical evaluation.

Method: The survey organizes VLN methods along two orthogonal dimensions: (1) action paradigms (hierarchical vs. monolithic frameworks) and (2) model paradigms (discriminative vs. generative approaches). Systematic real-world evaluation of representative VLN configurations is conducted on a physical robotic platform across ten diverse scenes.

Result: A representative monolithic RGB-only method achieves 61% success in simulation but drops to 22% in real-world deployment. A hierarchical framework achieves a higher real-world success rate of 51%, indicating stronger robustness in the evaluated setting. A substantial simulation-to-reality performance gap is demonstrated across tested configurations.

Conclusion: Key challenges in perception, decision-making, and control must be addressed to bridge the simulation-to-reality gap and improve VLN robustness for real-world autonomous systems.

Abstract: Navigation is a fundamental capability of autonomous systems, yet most existing approaches rely on highly structured models and strong prior assumptions, limiting their robustness in open and uncertain real-world environments. Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) offers a promising direction by enabling robots to integrate natural language understanding with visual perception in a data-driven manner. Although VLN has attracted increasing research attention, systematic methodological taxonomy and real-world validation remain limited. This survey presents a comprehensive review of VLN research. Specifically, state-of-the-art methods are organized along two orthogonal dimensions: action paradigms, including hierarchical and monolithic frameworks, and model paradigms, including discriminative and generative approaches. A critical analysis of their respective strengths and limitations is provided. Additionally, we conduct a systematic real-world evaluation of representative VLN system configurations on a physical robotic platform. Experiments across ten diverse real-world scenes show a substantial performance gap between simulation and real-world deployment under the tested configurations: a representative monolithic RGB-only method achieves 61% success in simulation but drops to 22% in real-world deployment, while a hierarchical framework achieves a higher real-world success rate of 51%, suggesting stronger robustness in our evaluation setting. Finally, we highlight key challenges in perception, decision-making, and control that must be addressed in future research.

</details>


### [127] [A Biomimetic Myoelectric Tentacle Prosthesis with Sensorless Object Detection and Vibrotactile Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09807)
*Gabrielle Marion,Olivier Lecompte,Amandine Gesta,Abolfazl Mohebbi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A myoelectric tentacle-shaped prosthesis using EMG control, sensorless object detection via motor current analysis, and vibrotactile feedback achieved 77 ms response time, >90% detection accuracy, and reliable haptic perception of tentacle configuration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a responsive, intuitive assistive device that adapts to various object shapes while providing sensory feedback, prioritizing expressive functionality over anthropomorphic form.

Method: EMG signal normalization/filtering with threshold-based intention detection; logarithmic spiral curling geometry; slope-based motor current analysis for sensorless contact detection; cumulative vibrotactile stimulation for spatial feedback.

Result: 77 ms average response time; >90% object-detection success rate; users reliably identified tentacle folding zones via haptic feedback.

Conclusion: The biomimetic design demonstrates effective real-time control and sensory feedback, supporting further exploration of non-anthropomorphic expressive artificial limbs.

Abstract: This paper presents the design and evaluation of a myoelectric tentacle-shaped prosthesis integrating electromyographic (EMG) control, sensorless object detection, and vibrotactile feedback. The objective was to develop a responsive and intuitive assistive device that adapts to various object shapes while providing sensory feedback to the user. The system relies on EMG signals to control the motion of a flexible, biomimetic structure whose curling geometry follows a logarithmic spiral, enabling it to coil around objects. To ensure stable control, the EMG signal is normalized and filtered, and a threshold-based method identifies user intention. Object contact is detected through a slope-based analysis of motor current, eliminating the need for external sensors, and a haptic feedback strategy based on cumulative vibrotactile stimulation conveys spatial information about the tentacle's configuration. The system was evaluated through quantitative and qualitative tests. The results demonstrate a low response time (77 ms on average), enabling smooth real-time interaction; an object-detection success rate above 90%, confirming robustness despite EMG variability; and an effective haptic feedback strategy that allowed users to reliably identify the folding zone of the tentacle. The proposed biomimetic design promotes further investigation of expressive artificial limbs by prioritizing expressive functionality over adherence to a predefined, anthropomorphic form factor.

</details>


### [128] [RASR: Range-Aware Scale Recovery for Metric UAV Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09815)
*Hongtao Liang,Xinyu Shao,Chenxu Wang,Yiyao Wan,Jiahuan Ji,Fangwei Ye,Fuhui Zhou,Qihui Wu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RASR introduces a Range-Aware Scale Recovery method that separates transferable scale recovery from protocol-specific calibration to improve metric last-meter navigation under GNSS denial, achieving a total score of 0.003189 on the UAVs in Multimedia 2026 PairUAV online evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Under GNSS denial, UAV controllers require accurate metric last-meter navigation (distance and heading commands). Existing dense pair-geometry foundation models like MASt3R transfer relative structure well but produce poorly calibrated distance scales, and simply correcting average scale leaves distance-dependent residuals near the goal.

Method: RASR separates a transferable scale-recovery core from a protocol-specific calibration module fixed at inference. The core compresses frozen MASt3R-style geometry into a compact descriptor and uses global calibration to recover dominant metric signal. Range-bucket residual correction and command-grid alignment are confined to the calibration module to match PairUAV's command format and evaluation protocol.

Result: On the UAVs in Multimedia 2026 PairUAV online evaluation, RASR achieves a total score of 0.003189.

Conclusion: Frozen pair geometry yields stable per-pair distance and heading estimates under the PairUAV protocol, while protocol-specific adjustments remain confined to a pre-inference calibration module, validating the separation design.

Abstract: Under Global Navigation Satellite System (GNSS) denial, a UAV controller still needs a distance and heading command it can execute, making accurate metric last-meter navigation essential. Dense pair-geometry foundation models transfer relative structure well, yet the distance scale of their raw metric outputs remains poorly calibrated. Under the relative error metric of PairUAV, correcting only the average scale can still leave costly, distance-dependent residuals near the goal. To address this scale mismatch, Range-Aware Scale Recovery (RASR) separates a transferable scale-recovery core from a protocol-specific calibration module in a per-pair system fixed at inference. The core compresses frozen Matching And Stereo 3D Reconstruction (MASt3R)-style geometry into a compact descriptor and uses global calibration to recover the dominant metric signal. Range-bucket residual correction and command-grid alignment stay inside the calibration module, so they match the command format and evaluation protocol of PairUAV. On the UAVs in Multimedia 2026 PairUAV online evaluation, RASR reaches a total score of 0.003189. Under the PairUAV protocol, frozen pair geometry thus yields stable per-pair distance and heading estimates, while every protocol-specific adjustment stays confined to a calibration module fixed before inference. Code and materials are available at https://github.com/lht-research/rasr-pairuav.

</details>


### [129] [TS-Mask VLA: 2D Temporal-Spatial Masking for Vision-Language-Action Model with Effective Bridging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09818)
*Shengzhuo Yang,Ronghao Yu,Chuanjie Lv,Linpeng Peng,Hang Yu,Jie Ren,Jiajun Lv,Yong Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TS-Mask VLA is a vision-language-action framework for robot manipulation that uses a Discrete Diffusion Action Expert with Bridge Attention and a temporal-spatial 2D masking strategy, achieving 95.7% success on LIBERO and 4.19 average sequence length on CALVIN with only 0.5B parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing autoregressive token-based VLA models reduce action generation to next-token prediction, lacking explicit modeling of spatiotemporal structure and disentanglement between vision-language representations and actions, limiting performance in long-horizon and complex scenarios.

Method: TS-Mask VLA introduces two key designs: (1) a Discrete Diffusion Action Expert with a Bridge Attention conditioning bridge for multi-layer VLM conditioning and stable action generation; (2) a temporal-spatial 2D masking strategy for discrete action tokens to enhance cross-time dependencies and inter-dimensional coupling.

Result: Achieves 95.7% average success rate on LIBERO with only 0.5B parameters, outperforming larger models. On CALVIN, achieves best average sequence length of 4.19 with strong long-horizon performance.

Conclusion: The proposed designs of diffusion-based action generation with spatial-temporal masking effectively improve action structural consistency and long-horizon performance, validated through comprehensive experiments and ablations.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models aim to understand natural-language instructions and visual observations, and to generate and execute corresponding actions as embodied agents. Recently, autoregressive token-based action generation has driven the development of many representative VLA models. However, this paradigm often reduces action generation to next-token prediction, thereby lacking explicit modeling of the spatiotemporal structure of action sequences and the disentanglement between vision-language representations and actions, which can limit performance in long-horizon and complex scenarios. In this paper, we propose TS-Mask VLA, a vision-language-action framework for robot manipulation. TS-Mask VLA is built upon two key designs: (1) a Discrete Diffusion Action Expert equipped with a Bridge Attention conditioning bridge, which enables multi-layer conditioning from the VLM and facilitates more accurate and stable action generation; and (2) a temporal-spatial 2D masking strategy for discrete action tokens that strengthens the model's understanding of cross-time dependencies and inter-dimensional coupling, leading to more structurally consistent action sequences. We conduct extensive experiments on simulation benchmarks and real-world tasks. On LIBERO, TS-Mask VLA achieves a 95.7 percent average success rate with only 0.5B parameters, outperforming significantly larger models. On CALVIN, it attains the best average sequence length of 4.19 and strong long-horizon performance. Comprehensive analyses and ablations further validate the effectiveness of our design.

</details>


### [130] [More Structure, Not More Capacity: Object-Centric Representations for Visuomotor Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09825)
*Yi Li,Alexandre Chapin,Liming Chen,Jan Peters,Alap Kshirsagar*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Object-centric slot representations (SPOT) outperform both global scene embeddings and dense patch grids in robotic manipulation (PickCube-v1), achieving 55.0% success vs 32.6% for DINO global features, with spatial grounding pushing performance to 68.7% (near the 71.7% oracle upper bound). A kinematic failure taxonomy reveals that spatial grounding reduces Near-Miss errors but does not affect No-Grasp errors, with occlusion as the main bottleneck in harder tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing pre-trained vision models used in robotic manipulation—whether global scene embeddings or dense patch grids—mix task-relevant and task-irrelevant features indiscriminately. Object-centric slot representations offer a structured alternative by grouping features into per-object slots, potentially improving task performance and interpretability.

Method: The authors test SPOT (DINO ViT-B/16 + Slot Attention) on ManiSkill3 PickCube-v1 with a frozen encoder and held-out-seed evaluation, keeping policy, goal token, rendering, and calibration fixed. They compare against DINO global features and dense patch grids, and also introduce explicit 2D spatial goals and native-resolution rendering. A kinematic failure taxonomy (spatial-precision Near-Miss vs. object-tracking No-Grasp) is used to analyze failures, and the taxonomy is transferred to StackCube-v1.

Result: SPOT achieves 55.0±2.9% success, which is 22.4% above the DINO global-feature baseline (32.6±1.5%). Dense patch grids with 16x the tokens perform no better than global features. Adding explicit 2D spatial goals and native-resolution rendering raises the full system to 68.7±4.2%, just below the 3D-oracle upper bound (71.7±4.1%). The failure taxonomy shows spatial grounding reduces Near-Miss but not No-Grasp errors. On StackCube-v1, occlusion is identified as the main bottleneck.

Conclusion: Object-centric slot representations provide structured, task-relevant features that significantly improve manipulation policy performance without encoder fine-tuning. Spatial grounding further boosts performance by reducing spatial-precision failures, while object-tracking failures (e.g., occlusion) remain a key challenge for harder tasks like StackCube-v1.

Abstract: Robotic manipulation policies rely on pre-trained vision models that give either a global scene embedding or a dense patch grid. Both mix task-relevant and task-irrelevant features. Object-centric slot representations are a structured alternative: they group features into a few per-object slots. We test what this structure buys on ManiSkill3 PickCube-v1, with a frozen encoder and a held-out-seed evaluation. Holding the policy, goal token, rendering, and calibration fixed and changing only the encoder, a frozen object-centric SPOT representation (DINO ViT-B/16 + Slot Attention) reaches 55.0$\pm$2.9% success, 22.4% above a dense DINO global-feature baseline (32.6 $\pm$ 1.5%), with the same trainable policy and no encoder fine-tuning. More tokens alone do not help: a dense patch grid with 16x the tokens performs no better than the global feature. Adding an explicit 2D spatial goal and native-resolution rendering raises the full system to 68.7$\pm$4.2%, just below a privileged 3D-oracle upper bound (71.7$\pm$4.1%). An automated kinematic failure taxonomy then separates spatial-precision (Near-Miss) failures from object-tracking (No-Grasp) failures: spatial grounding reduces Near-Miss while leaving No- Grasp unchanged. The same taxonomy transfers to the harder StackCube-v1 and points to occlusion as the main bottleneck.

</details>


### [131] [Robo-ValueRL: Reliable Value Estimation for Offline-to-Online Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09866)
*Wenke Xia,Pei Ren,Wenbo Yu,Yizhuo Zhang,Jifan Li,Yixue Zhang,Yinuo Zhao,Qingyang Gao,Jianlong Fu,Jian Tang,Ji-Rong Wen,Zhengping Che,Di Hu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Robo-ValueRL is a unified framework for offline-to-online RL that uses a history-conditioned value estimator with global-progress and local-preference reliability metrics, enabling quality-conditioned policy pretraining and residual online adaptation. Across extensive robotic experiments, it demonstrates that value-function reliability strongly correlates with downstream policy performance, achieving 86% success on precise chip insertion and 84% on block disassembly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The central question of how value-function reliability shapes policy optimization in offline-to-online reinforcement learning remains underexplored, despite value estimation playing a key role in prioritizing heterogeneous data for policy improvement.

Method: Proposes Robo-ValueRL framework with: (1) a history-conditioned value estimator, (2) global-progress and local-preference metrics for value reliability evaluation, (3) quality-conditioned consistency-policy pretraining, and (4) a residual adaptation module for online rollouts.

Result: Downstream performance is strongly associated with value reliability; reliable value functions enable better action-quality estimates and stabilize online improvement. Achieves 86% success on millimeter-level precise chip insertion and 84% on generalizable block disassembly across 240 hours of offline data and 3,000+ online trajectories.

Conclusion: Value-guided data utilization is critical for effective policy improvement from heterogeneous robotic experience, and integrating reliable value guidance through offline pretraining with online improvement yields strong performance on complex manipulation tasks.

Abstract: Offline-to-online reinforcement learning is promising for generalizable robotic manipulation, yet its full-stack complexity obscures reproduction and diagnosis. Within such systems, value estimation plays a central role in prioritizing heterogeneous data for policy improvement. Despite its importance, the central question remains underexplored: how value-function reliability shapes policy optimization in offline-to-online reinforcement learning. To answer this question, we propose Robo-ValueRL, a unified framework that enables reliable value estimation and systematically traces its downstream effects on policy pretraining and online improvement. Concretely, Robo-ValueRL learns a history-conditioned value estimator and evaluates its reliability through global-progress and local-preference metrics. These resulting value estimates are propagated into quality-conditioned consistency-policy pretraining and a residual adaptation module on online rollouts, providing a unified testbed for analyzing how value reliability shapes downstream policy performance. Across 240 hours of offline demonstrations and over 3,000 online rollout trajectories, our extensive experiments show that downstream performance is strongly associated with value reliability. Reliable value functions provide better action-quality estimates, allowing value-guided offline RL to scale more effectively than quality-agnostic behavior cloning, and stabilize online improvement by prioritizing high-quality rollout data. Integrating reliable value guidance through offline pretraining with online improvement, our system achieves 86% success on millimeter-level precise chip insertion and 84% on generalizable block disassembly. We hope these findings highlight the importance of value-guided data utilization for effective policy improvement from heterogeneous robotic experience.

</details>


### [132] [Diffusion for Long-Horizon Multi-Robot Path Planning in Human-Shared Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09911)
*Vaibhav Sanjay,Yorai Shaoul,Jiaoyang Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MRRD is a novel multi-robot path planning framework that combines rolling-horizon diffusion, parallelized inference, conflict-based search, urgency-based temporal conditioning, and differentiated guidance to achieve real-time, long-horizon navigation for up to 15 robots in human-shared environments, outperforming baselines in safety and mission success.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion-based generative planners for multi-robot path planning are limited by fixed path durations and high computational latency, making them poorly suited for varying goal distances and real-time deployment in human-shared environments.

Method: MRRD integrates (1) a rolling-horizon scheme to address limited human motion prediction, (2) parallelized diffusion inference for scalable human-like path generation, (3) conflict-based search for inter-robot collision resolution, (4) urgency-based temporal conditioning for variable-speed path generation, and (5) differentiated guidance terms to balance social awareness and inter-robot coordination.

Result: MRRD scales to 15 robots in real-time in crowded environments and significantly outperforms existing baselines in both safety and mission success rates.

Conclusion: The proposed MRRD framework effectively balances real-time performance, long-horizon planning, social awareness, and inter-robot coordination for large-scale multi-robot navigation in human-shared spaces.

Abstract: Multi-robot path planning in human-shared environments requires a delicate balance between robust inter-robot coordination and socially aware behavior. While diffusion models excel at generating predictable, human-like paths, existing generative planners are often restricted to paths of fixed duration and high computational latency, limiting their adaptability to varying goal distances and hindering real-time deployment. We present Multi-Robot Rolling Diffusion (MRRD), a novel framework that enables real-time, long-horizon navigation for large robot teams through dense crowds. MRRD combines a rolling-horizon scheme to accommodate the limited prediction horizon of human motion, parallelized diffusion inference for scalable generation of human-like paths, and a conflict-based-search mechanism for resolving inter-robot collisions. It further incorporates urgency-based temporal conditioning to generate paths with varying speeds and employs differentiated guidance terms to maximize both social awareness around humans and efficient coordination between robots. Experimental results in crowded environments demonstrate that MRRD successfully scales to 15 robots in real-time, significantly outperforming existing baselines in both safety and mission success rates.

</details>


### [133] [A Numerically-Robust ROS 2 Port of iG-LIO: Diagnosing and Fixing Toolchain-Induced Failures in Incremental GICP LiDAR-Inertial Odometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09947)
*Afonso E. Carvalho,David Portugal,Paulo Peixoto*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper describes the ROS 2 Jazzy port of the iG-LIO LiDAR-inertial odometry system, diagnoses two environment-induced numerical failures (QoS mismatch causing dropped IMU data and an uninitialized oneTBB+Eigen accumulator), corrects Ouster point-field parsing, adds Velodyne Velarray M1600 support, provides Livox driver-free path, and validates on Ouster OS0 Rev7, Ouster OS1 Rev7, and Livox MID-360 hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable iG-LIO's operation on the modern ROS 2 Jazzy framework and diagnose/correct numerical failures that emerged despite mathematically faithful migration, ensuring reliable LiDAR-inertial odometry on updated toolchains.

Method: Port of the original ROS 1 iG-LIO to ROS 2 Jazzy while keeping estimation mathematics unchanged; diagnosis of two failure sources (QoS mismatch for IMU topics and uninitialized oneTBB+Eigen parallel-reduce accumulator); correction of Ouster point-field parsing for newer sensor revisions; addition of Velodyne Velarray M1600 support; compile-time-gated Livox CustomMsg path and driver-free PointCloud2 path for Livox sensors; YAML-based runtime configuration.

Result: Successful ROS 2 port with validated operation on Ouster OS0 Rev7, Ouster OS1 Rev7, and Livox MID-360; numerical failures resolved by fixing QoS settings and accumulator initialization; expanded sensor support.

Conclusion: The port is a citable reference for the ROS 2 migration itself rather than a claim on the underlying algorithm; the modern ROS 2 toolchain introduced environment-specific bugs that had to be diagnosed and fixed despite algorithm-preserving migration.

Abstract: iG-LIO is a tightly-coupled LiDAR-inertial odometry system fusing generalized-ICP and point-to-plane constraints in an iterated error-state Kalman filter over an incremental voxel map. We report an open-source ROS 2 Jazzy port of the original ROS 1 implementation and, more importantly, the diagnosis of environment-induced numerical failures that appear only after the port: a mechanically faithful migration -- estimation mathematics left unchanged -- compiled and ran, yet diverged with NaN internal values. Both causes trace to the modern ROS 2 toolchain, not the algorithm: a Quality-of-Service (QoS) mismatch that silently drops and reorders IMU samples, and an uninitialized parallel-reduce accumulator arising from the oneTBB + Eigen combination shipped with current distributions. We further correct Ouster point-field parsing to ensure correct point cloud undistortion with newer Ouster revisions, add Velodyne Velarray M1600 support, provide both a compile-time-gated Livox CustomMsg path and a driver-free path for Livox sensors publishing standard PointCloud2 (e.g. Mid-360), and expose the runtime via YAML. The result has been validated in an Ouster OS0 Rev7, an Ouster OS1 Rev 7, and a Livox MID-360. This report is a citable reference for the port itself, not a claim on the underlying algorithm [1]. The ROS 2 port of iG-LIO described in this document can be found at https://github.com/Forestry-Robotics-UC/ig_lio/tree/ros2-jazzy.

</details>


### [134] [SEAMLiS: Visibility-Aware Safety for Perception-Limited Multi-Robot Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09959)
*Taekyung Kim,Rahul H Kumar,Aswin D. Menon,Tzu-Hsiang Lin,Dimitra Panagou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SEAMLiS is a modular safety framework for multi-robot exploration under limited sensing that enforces collision avoidance through perception-aware attitude and positional filters while preserving the upstream exploration stack.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard exploration separates informative planning from local safety, which can be unsafe under finite sensing: optimistic plans through unobserved space and sensor steering toward information gain cause hidden obstacles to be detected too late for avoidance with bounded actuation.

Method: SEAMLiS adds an execution-layer safety module with (1) a gatekeeper-based attitude filter switching between visibility-promoting yaw and velocity-tracking backup policy to preserve braking margin, and (2) a CBF-based positional filter to avoid known obstacles, newly detected obstacles, and other robots.

Result: Validated in randomized simulation, Isaac Sim, and Crazyflie hardware experiments; collision-free exploration in single- and multi-robot settings while retaining efficiency of visibility-promoting yaw control.

Conclusion: Perception-aware safety filtering at the execution layer can prevent collisions from optimistic exploration under limited sensing without modifying the upstream planner, with sufficient collision-avoidance conditions provided.

Abstract: Autonomous exploration in unknown environments is typically driven by informative frontiers, viewpoints, or trajectories, while local safety controllers avoid obstacles represented in the current map. Under finite sensing range and limited field of view, this separation can be unsafe: an exploration stack may plan optimistically through unobserved space and steer the sensor toward information gain rather than along the direction of motion, causing hidden obstacles to be detected too late for bounded-actuation avoidance. This paper presents SEAMLiS (Safe Exploration for Autonomous Multi-Robot Systems Under Limited Sensing), a modular execution-layer safety framework for decentralized multi-robot exploration. SEAMLiS preserves the upstream exploration stack, including the goal allocator and local planner, and enforces safety at the execution layer through perception-aware attitude and positional filters. A gatekeeper-based attitude filter switches between a visibility-promoting yaw policy and a velocity-tracking backup policy to preserve visibility of the critical known-free/unknown boundary with sufficient braking margin. A Control Barrier Function (CBF)-based positional filter then avoids known obstacles, newly detected obstacles, and other robots. We provide sufficient collision-avoidance conditions and validate the framework in randomized simulation, Isaac Sim, and Crazyflie hardware experiments. Results show collision-free exploration across tested single- and multi-robot settings while retaining much of the efficiency of visibility-promoting yaw control.

</details>


### [135] [Task Planning for Mobile Manipulation in Retail Stores using Foundation Models with Iterative Re-planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09962)
*Vismay Vakharia,Sanjana Garai,Rolif Lima,Nijil George,Vighnesh Vatsal,Kaushik Das*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents an LLM/VLM-based task-planning approach for robotic restocking in retail environments, demonstrated on a mobile manipulation platform in simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend automation from structured back-room operations to variable, human-centric retail environments by addressing the restocking problem, leveraging recent advances in robotic mobile manipulation and foundation models.

Method: A task-planning approach using Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs) with user-driven prompts and feedback-based iterative re-planning for error correction, deployed on a custom omnidirectional mobile manipulation platform in PyBullet simulation.

Result: The end-to-end system is validated in a PyBullet simulation environment for pick-and-place tasks.

Conclusion: LLM/VLM-based task planning with iterative re-planning enables robotic automation for restocking in retail scenarios, validated through simulation.

Abstract: Automation in industries such as retail, warehousing and logistics presents opportunities for greater throughput, cost reduction and mitigation of disruptions from labour shortages. Previously, such efforts have focused on back-room operations involving packing and sorting in relatively structured environments. With advances in robotic mobile manipulation hardware and foundation models, automation can now be applied to more variable and human-centric environments such as retail store shelves. In this work, we present a task-planning approach using Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to address the restocking problem in retail scenarios such as supermarkets. We demonstrate this system on a custom omnidirectional mobile manipulation platform, with user-driven prompts and a feedback-based iterative re-planning approach for error correction. The end-to-end system is validated in a PyBullet simulation environment for pick-and-place tasks.

</details>


### [136] [Chalito: An Extensible Library for Filtering-Based State Estimation in Quadruped Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09968)
*Hilton Marques Souza Santana,João Carlos Virgolino Soares,Marco Antonio Meggiolaro,Claudio Semini*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Chalito is an open-source MATLAB/Python library for benchmarking filter-based state estimation algorithms in quadruped robots, supporting multiple filtering approaches, URDF import, and evaluation on simulated and real datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing state estimation implementations are tied to specific robots or software stacks, hindering fair comparisons, reproducibility, and algorithmic innovation in quadruped robotics.

Method: The authors developed Chalito, an extensible MATLAB/Python library that imports robot models from URDF, supports multiple filter-based estimation approaches, and runs on both simulated and real datasets.

Result: Chalito is the first open-source library exclusively dedicated to benchmarking filtering algorithms for quadruped robots, enabling systematic evaluation across different robots and filters.

Conclusion: Chalito provides a general-purpose benchmarking framework that improves reproducibility and facilitates algorithmic innovation in quadruped robot state estimation.

Abstract: State estimation is essential for quadruped robots, enabling robust locomotion, navigation, and control. While many estimators have been proposed in the literature, existing implementations are often tied to specific robots or software stacks, making fair comparisons difficult. This lack of a general-purpose benchmarking framework hinders reproducibility and slows down algorithmic innovation. In this paper, we introduce Chalito, an extensible MATLAB/Python library for benchmarking filter-based state estimation algorithms in quadruped robots. Chalito imports robot models directly from URDF, supports multiple filtering approaches, and is designed to be easily extended with new methods. The framework runs on both simulated and real datasets, enabling systematic evaluation across robots and filters. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first open-source library exclusively dedicated to benchmarking filtering algorithms for quadruped robots.

</details>


### [137] [PinFT: Miniature 5-Axis Force/Torque Sensor Embeddable to Tweezer-like Tool](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10000)
*Leo King,Jenny Chen,Tae Myung Huh*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PinFT is a miniature five-axis capacitive force/torque sensor integrated into tweezer tips; it enables per-tip sensing with ~0.23 N and ~2.5 mN·m accuracy, and demonstrates utility in fine manipulation tasks (grasping SMD capacitors, hair pulling, tearing) by reliably detecting success vs. failure events via gradient-based force features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable direct tip-level force/torque sensing in tweezer-like tools for fine manipulation tasks, overcoming the lack of per-tip feedback in standard parallel-jaw grippers.

Method: A compact three-PCB stack with segmented plated through-hole electrodes and silicone elastomer dielectric, using a central 2 mm stainless steel pin for displacement-based 5-DOF sensing (Fx, Fy, Fz, Tx, Ty). Calibrated via higher-order polynomial mapping; integrated into 3D-printed tweezer tips on a parallel-jaw gripper.

Result: Mean absolute errors ~0.23 N for forces, ~2.5 mN·m for torques, R² > 0.97. Per-tip sensing reliably distinguished success from failure (slip, ejection) in three manipulation tasks using gradient-based features from grasp and interaction forces.

Conclusion: Direct per-tip force sensing enables standard parallel-jaw grippers to monitor and interpret fine manipulation tasks performed through a handheld tweezer.

Abstract: We present PinFT, a miniature five-axis capacitive force/torque sensor designed for direct tip-level integration into tweezer-like tools. The sensor employs a compact three-PCB stack with segmented plated through-hole electrodes and a silicone elastomer dielectric, enabling five-degree-of-freedom force and torque sensing ($F_x$, $F_y$, $F_z$, $T_x$, $T_y$) through displacement of a central 2\,mm-diameter stainless steel pin. The fabricated prototype was calibrated using a higher-order polynomial mapping, yielding mean absolute errors of approximately 0.23\,N for forces and 2.5\,mN$\cdot$m for torques, with coefficients of determination ($R^2$) exceeding 0.97 across all axes. To demonstrate practical utility, a 3D-printed tweezer integrating PinFT sensors at both tips was mounted on a parallel-jaw gripper and evaluated across three representative manipulation tasks: grasping a sub-millimeter SMD capacitor, pulling a simulated hair from a silicone substrate, and tearing a compliant silicone specimen. In all cases, per-tip force sensing reliably captured characteristic force signatures that distinguish successful manipulation from failure events -- including slip and object ejection -- using gradient-based features derived from internal grasp force and net interaction force. These results demonstrate that direct, per-tip force sensing enables standard parallel-jaw grippers to monitor and interpret fine manipulation tasks performed through a handheld tweezer.

</details>


### [138] [Runtime Safety Filtering for Learned Small UAS Separation Policies under GNSS Degradation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10014)
*Alex Zongo,Peng Wei*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper evaluates two safety architectures (action filtering vs. observation filtering) for learned sUAS separation policies under GNSS degradation, finding that observation filtering reduces near mid-air collisions by 90% while action filtering provides negligible improvement, suggesting preserving policy decision authority outperforms overriding actions with hand-designed constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning-based separation assurance for sUAS assumes accurate GNSS data, which fails in urban environments due to multipath, signal blockage, and interference. This raises a fundamental architectural question for deploying learned separation policies under GNSS degradation.

Method: Both architectures first estimate a worst-case traffic state consistent with bounded observation uncertainty, then diverge: (1) action filtering constrains policy outputs via discrete-time control barrier functions evaluated at the worst-case state; (2) observation filtering presents the worst-case state directly to the policy as corrected input.

Result: Action filtering provides negligible safety improvement; observation filtering reduces near mid-air collisions by 90% and remains robust to the barrier function's tradeoff between separation distance and closing rate.

Conclusion: For policies with learned safety behaviors, preserving the policy's decision authority (observation filtering) outperforms overriding its actions with hand-designed constraints (action filtering).

Abstract: Learning-based separation assurance for small Unmanned Aircraft Systems (sUAS) achieves near-zero collision rates in simulation, but assumes accurate position and velocity information from Global Navigation Satellite Systems (GNSS). This assumption fails in urban environments, where multipath propagation, signal blockage, and intentional interference degrade navigation integrity. This raises a fundamental architectural question for deploying learned separation policies under GNSS degradation: should runtime safety mechanisms filter the policy's actions or its observations? This work evaluates both approaches for multi-agent sUAS separation under adversarial GNSS degradation. Both architectures first estimate a worst-case traffic state consistent with bounded observation uncertainty, then diverge: action filtering constrains policy outputs via discrete-time control barrier functions evaluated at the worst-case state, while observation filtering presents the worst-case state directly to the policy as corrected input. Experimental results show that action filtering provides negligible safety improvement, while observation filtering reduces near mid-air collisions by 90% and remains robust to the barrier function's tradeoff between separation distance and closing rate. These results suggest that, for policies with learned safety behaviors, preserving the policy's decision authority outperforms overriding its actions with hand-designed constraints.

</details>


### [139] [Plug-and-Play Reweighting for Resilient Collaborative Decision-Making in Connected Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10037)
*Jiewen Liu,Rui Liu,Matthew Lee,Ming C. Lin,Xiaorui Liu,Peng Gao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Resilient Collaborative Decision-Making (RCDM) framework with a plug-and-play reweighting module that improves robustness against perceptual noise and adversarial attacks in multi-robot systems by down-weighting corrupted observations based on local structure consistency, achieving state-of-the-art performance (up to 26% improvement).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for resilient collaborative decision-making in multi-robot systems rely on retraining with attack-specific defenses or restrictive perturbation assumptions, limiting their practicality in real-world scenarios with perceptual noise and adversarial attacks.

Method: The RCDM framework uses an attention-based encoder for individual robot perceptual embeddings and an attention-based decoder for fusing collaborator perceptions. A plug-and-play reweighting module assigns smaller weights to corrupted inputs by analyzing neighborhood point consistency relative to local median, without requiring additional training.

Result: In high-fidelity simulations with perceptual noise and five attack types across diverse accident-prone scenarios, the approach outperforms existing methods by up to 26%.

Conclusion: The proposed RCDM framework achieves state-of-the-art resilient performance for collaborative decision-making in multi-robot systems, with a practical plug-and-play module that enhances robustness without retraining.

Abstract: Collaborative decision-making is a fundamental capability in multi-robot systems, such as connected autonomous vehicles. However, perceptual noise and adversarial attacks in collaborators can severely affect decision reliability. Overall, existing methods typically rely on retraining with attack-specific defenses or on restrictive perturbation assumptions to improve resilience, which limits their practicality. In this paper, we propose a novel Resilient Collaborative Decision-Making (RCDM) framework that consists of an attention-based encoder for extracting individual robot perceptual embeddings and an attention-based decoder for fusing collaborator perceptions and making decisions. To improve resilience to corrupted observations, we design a novel plug-and-play reweighting module that down-weights the influence of corrupted inputs by analyzing the consistency of neighborhood points relative to the local structure and assigning smaller weights to points that deviate strongly from the local median. This module can be seamlessly integrated into attention-based collaborative decision-making without requiring additional training. We evaluate our method in high-fidelity simulations, considering perceptual noise and five types of attacks across diverse accident-prone scenarios. Experimental results demonstrate that our approach consistently outperforms existing methods by up to 26% and achieves state-of-the-art resilient performance.

</details>


### [140] [Direct Rotor Thrust Sensing and Feedback Control for Disturbance Rejection of Multirotors Using Load-cells](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10099)
*Peter Böhm,Michael Brünig,Peyman Z. Moghadam,Pauline E. I. Pounds*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a new approach to multirotor disturbance rejection by measuring instantaneous rotor force directly using load-cells and implementing high-speed control, demonstrating feasibility and effectiveness with low-cost hardware in single/dual rotor setups and a flying aircraft.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gust disturbances, dynamic vertical inflow, and ground effect cause adverse aerodynamic phenomena that complicate multirotor flight control. Existing disturbance rejection methods rely on motion-based estimation (sensing disturbances only after the trajectory is affected), and challenges remain in achieving fast compensation.

Method: The approach directly measures instantaneous rotor force at the point of generation using load-cells and implements high-speed control to accurately track desired thrust. Purpose-built hardware from low-cost commodity components was tested on single-rotor, dual-rotor see-saw models, and a flying aircraft.

Result: Despite load-cells previously considered too noisy for meaningful input, experiments demonstrated both feasibility and effectiveness of the approach in the presence of complex aerodynamic phenomena.

Conclusion: Direct force measurement via load-cells with high-speed control is a viable and effective method for rejecting external disturbances in multirotors, using low-cost components.

Abstract: Gust disturbances, dynamic vertical inflow and ground effect are key adverse aerodynamic phenomena that induce variations in the forces acting on a multirotor and complicate its flight control. Miniature rotorcraft typically rely on simplified modelling of such effects to compute adjustments in thrust to counteract these forces. In the most basic case, disturbance force estimations are derived from the aircraft's motion and the generated thrust is assumed to exactly match that requested by the controller. However, such systems rely on the aircraft's trajectory to be affected before disturbances can be sensed and compensated. Numerous approaches presented over the last 15-20 years aim to reject external disturbances more quickly, but challenges remain.
  This paper presents a new approach in this category by measuring the instantaneous force of the rotors directly at the point of generation using load-cells and implementing high-speed control to accurately track the desired thrust. Measurements from load-cells were previously considered too noisy to provide meaningful input, but the experiments presented in the paper using purpose-built hardware from low-cost commodity components in single- and dual rotor see-saw models and a flying aircraft demonstrate both the feasibility and the effectiveness of the approach in the presence of complex aerodynamic phenomena.

</details>


### [141] [TAC-LOCO: Unified Whole-Body Control for Quadrupedal TACtile-Informed LOCO-Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10132)
*Muqun Hu,Yuhao Zhou,Kabir Ray Malik,Chi Lin,Won Suk Lee,Yu She,Yan Gu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TAC-LOCO is a tactile-augmented RL framework for dynamic loco-manipulation that encodes tactile array observations from compliant grippers into a latent representation, enabling unified control of legs, arm, and gripper to regulate grasp force and prevent object slip during dynamic whole-body tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tactile sensing has been widely studied for robotic manipulation but its role in dynamic whole-body control (loco-manipulation) remains largely unexplored. Existing works without tactile feedback rely on firm grasping rather than adaptive grasp regulation during interaction.

Method: A unified reinforcement learning framework that: (1) encodes tactile array observations from compliant grippers into a compact latent representation, (2) joins tactile latent with proprioception for unified control of legs, arm, and gripper, (3) uses grasp stability reward design to learn simultaneous body velocity and end-effector trajectory tracking, grasp force moderation, and object slip prevention.

Result: Zero-shot deployment on a Unitree Go2 with Interbotix WidowX 250 arm and tactile gripper demonstrates a 47% reduction in grasping force and an object drop rate of less than 1% under varying external interactions.

Conclusion: Tactile feedback enables adaptive grasp regulation in dynamic loco-manipulation, significantly reducing grasping force while maintaining extremely low object drop rates, outperforming approaches without tactile sensing.

Abstract: Dynamic loco-manipulation requires legged robots to coordinate whole-body motion while maintaining stable physical interaction with grasped objects under uncertain external forces. While tactile sensing has been widely studied for robotic manipulation, its role in dynamic whole-body control remains largely unexplored. Existing works without tactile feedback commonly grasp firmly rather than regulate the grasp according to the interaction. We propose TAC-LOCO, a tactile-augmented unified reinforcement learning framework that encodes tactile array observations from compliant grippers into a compact latent representation and joins it with proprioception for unified control of the legs, arm, and gripper. With effective grasp stability reward design, the policy learns to simultaneously track body velocity and end-effector trajectories, moderate grasp force, and prevent object slip under both gradual load changes and sudden release events. We deploy the policy zero-shot on a Unitree Go2 with an Interbotix WidowX 250 arm and tactile gripper, demonstrating dynamic tactile-informed loco-manipulation under varying external interactions, achieving a 47% reduction in grasping force and an object drop rate of less than 1%.

</details>


### [142] [Millimeter Wave Radar: From Synthetic Aperture to Probabilistic Mapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10161)
*Jui-Te Huang,Ruoyang Xu,Michael Kaess*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a complete pipeline from raw mmWave radar signals to probabilistic occupancy maps using Synthetic Aperture Radar (SAR) processing and probabilistic modeling, validated across indoor environments with downstream path planning evaluation. An open-source dataset and GPU-accelerated pipeline are provided.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional sensors fail in adverse conditions like smoke and fog, while mmWave radar offers reliable sensing but suffers from sparse and noisy data. The authors aim to create accurate probabilistic maps from radar data for robust autonomous navigation.

Method: A complete pipeline is established: raw radar signals → Synthetic Aperture Radar (SAR) processing → probabilistic modeling → probabilistic occupancy maps. Validation includes comparisons against different signal processing and probabilistic modeling approaches, downstream path planning performance analysis, and investigation of key parameters and antenna array configurations.

Result: The approach demonstrates both effectiveness and limitations in real-world robotic deployment. The paper provides an open-source cascaded mmWave radar dataset with a GPU-accelerated signal processing pipeline.

Conclusion: SAR-based probabilistic mapping shows promise for robotic applications in challenging environments, but has limitations. The released open-source resources aim to facilitate future research and broader adoption.

Abstract: Robust probabilistic mapping is essential for autonomous robotic systems operating in challenging environments. While traditional sensors fail in adverse conditions such as smoke and fog, millimeter wave (mmWave) radar sensors offer reliable sensing in such conditions. However, creating accurate probabilistic maps from radar data presents significant challenges due to the inherently sparse and noisy characteristics of radio wave measurements and signal processing steps. In an attempt to address these issues, we establish a complete pipeline from raw radar signals to probabilistic occupancy maps, incorporating Synthetic Aperture Radar processing followed by a probabilistic modeling step. We conduct extensive validation across indoor environments, comparing our approach against different signal processing and probabilistic modeling approaches. We also evaluate mapping quality through downstream path planning performance analysis. Furthermore, we investigate the impact of key parameters and antenna array configuration on mapping performance. The experimental results demonstrate both the effectiveness and limitations of SAR-based probabilistic mapping for real-world robotic deployment. To facilitate future research and broader adoption, we contribute an open-source cascaded mmWave radar dataset with an accompanying GPU-accelerated signal processing pipeline available at https://github.com/rpl-cmu/rpm.

</details>


### [143] [From Non-Rigid to Rigid: Safe Acquisition of Rigid Communication Graphs under Limited Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10170)
*Saharsh,Vedhas Talnikar,Pushpak Jagtap*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a distributed method for achieving and maintaining a rigid communication graph in multi-robot formation control under limited sensing ranges, without requiring an initial rigid graph, using a quadratic optimization-based controller with hierarchical second-order consensus for leader-follower architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Communication graph rigidity is fundamental for multi-robot formation control, but ensuring and maintaining rigid topologies is challenging due to limited sensing ranges and dynamic operating conditions. Existing methods often assume an initial rigid graph, which is impractical.

Method: A computationally lean, distributed quadratic optimization-based controller for leader-follower architecture, acquiring rigidity via hierarchical second-order consensus among robots. Communication links are dynamically established/broken based on limited sensing ranges. Follower agents do not require global absolute positions.

Result: Validated through both simulations and hardware experiments in a motion-capture environment, demonstrating reliable performance under limited sensing capabilities of individual robots.

Conclusion: The proposed approach guarantees inter-robot collision-free, rigid time-varying communication graphs for heterogeneous nonlinear multi-robot systems without assuming an initial rigid graph.

Abstract: Communication graph rigidity is a fundamental requirement in many multi robot formation control approaches. However, ensuring and maintaining a rigid communication topology becomes challenging in practice due to limited sensing ranges and dynamic operating conditions. This paper provides a method for achieving an inter robot collision free, rigid time varying communication graph, where communication links are established or broken according to limited sensing ranges, without assuming an initial rigid graph. In addition, the proposed approach guarantees the realization of a rigid graph for heterogeneous nonlinear multi robot systems. A computationally lean, distributed quadratic optimization-based controller is developed for a leader follower architecture, acquiring rigidity based on hierarchical second-order consensus among robots. Follower agents do not require global absolute positions of any agent, including their own. The proposed method is validated through both simulations and hardware experiments in a motion-capture environment, demonstrating reliable performance under the limited sensing capabilities of individual robots.

</details>


### [144] [On the Efficiency of LoRA Fine-Tuning for Vision-Language-Action Models in Industrial Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10172)
*Finn Ferchau,Daniel Pommer,Cristian Axenie*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: LoRA at rank 32 with full vision encoder fine-tuning matches full fine-tuning performance on billion-parameter VLA models for robotic assembly tasks, reducing VRAM from 36.2 to 10.8 GiB.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying billion-parameter VLA models on industrial hardware requires fine-tuning to bridge the embodiment gap, but Full Fine-Tuning demands data center-grade GPUs, which is impractical for many real-world settings.

Method: Systematic study of Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) applied to π₀, a flow-matching VLA, evaluated on four precision assembly tasks with a UR5e robot. Swept LoRA ranks (r=8 to 256), allocation strategies, and component-freezing ablations.

Result: No statistically significant advantage of FFT over certain LoRA configurations. Performance saturates at r=32. Uniform allocation across VLM backbone and action expert is sufficient. Freezing VLM or restricting vision encoder to LoRA degrades performance. VRAM reduced from 36.2 to 10.8 GiB.

Conclusion: LoRA at r=32 with full vision encoder fine-tuning is a practical, memory-efficient alternative to FFT for adapting VLA models to embodiment gaps, requiring both semantic and visual plasticity.

Abstract: Deploying billion-parameter Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models on industrial hardware requires fine-tuning to bridge the embodiment gap. Full Fine-Tuning (FFT) provides maximal plasticity but requires data centre-grade GPUs. We present a systematic study of Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) for $π_0$, a flow-matching VLA, evaluated on four precision assembly tasks with a UR5e robotic manipulator. Across a sweep of LoRA ranks (r=8 to 256), allocation strategies, and component-freezing ablations, we find no statistically significant advantage of FFT over certain LoRA configurations. Performance saturates at r=32, and uniform allocation across the Vision-Language-Model (VLM) backbone and action expert proves sufficient. Freezing the VLM or restricting the vision encoder to LoRA significantly degrades performance, indicating that embodiment adaptation requires both semantic and visual plasticity. These results suggest that LoRA at r=32 with full vision encoder fine-tuning is a practical approach, reducing static peak VRAM from 36.2 to 10.8 GiB (parameters and optimizer states, activation memory excluded) without detectable performance loss.

</details>


### [145] [ActiveFly-Bench: Aligning Embodied Question Answering with Vision-Language-Action for Aerial Embodied Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10180)
*Weichen Zhang,Shiquan Yu,Yinan Zhu,Peizhi Tang,Shilong Ji,Zhiyuan Deng,Tianyi Lyu,Haoyang Wang,Xin Zeng,Chen Gao,Yong Li,Xinlei Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ActiveFly-Bench is the first benchmark bridging cyberspace reasoning and physical-world interaction for UAV embodied perception, with three hierarchical tasks (Air-EQA, OBP, FLUC). They develop ActiveFly, a closed-loop UAV agent, and find current models struggle with behavior planning and viewpoint adjustment in active perception.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge the gap between cyberspace reasoning and physical-world interaction for UAV embodied perception, as existing benchmarks lack integration of high-level task understanding, behavior planning, and low-level control.

Method: Proposes ActiveFly-Bench with three hierarchical tasks (Aerial Embodied Question Answering, Observation Behavior Planning, Fine-grained Language-guided UAV Control) using real-world and simulated datasets, and develops ActiveFly, a closed-loop UAV agent integrating visual-language reasoning with fine-grained control deployed on a physical platform.

Result: Current UAV agents (VLMs and VLA models) struggle with behavior planning, viewpoint adjustment, and robust task completion in active perception tasks.

Conclusion: ActiveFly-Bench establishes a new testbed for embodied aerial intelligence, highlighting key deficiencies in existing models for active perception.

Abstract: We introduce ActiveFly-Bench, the first benchmark to bridge cyberspace reasoning and physical-world interaction for UAV embodied perception. The benchmark decomposes active perception into three hierarchical tasks: Aerial Embodied Question Answering (Air-EQA), Observation Behavior Planning (OBP), and Fine-grained Language-guided UAV Control (FLUC), explicitly connecting high-level task understanding, behavior planning, and low-level control. The datasets are collected from both real-world and simulated outdoor environments for training and evaluation. We further develop ActiveFly, a closed-loop UAV agent that integrates visual-language reasoning with fine-grained control, and deploy it on a physical UAV platform. Experiments with representative VLMs and VLA models show that current UAV agents still struggle with behavior planning, viewpoint adjustment, and robust task completion in active perception. These results establish ActiveFly-Bench as a new testbed for embodied aerial intelligence.

</details>


### [146] [Source-Lifted Flow Matching for Intervenable Multimodal Imitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10206)
*He Zhang,Ying Sun,Pengteng Li,Ziyang Chen,Yiren Zhao,Ziyang Rao,Weiyu Guo,Yandong Guo,Hui Xiong*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SL-FM proposes Source-Lifted Flow Matching, which makes the source endpoint of conditional flow intervenable via orthogonal source lifting, enabling users to select among multimodal action continuations without splitting the velocity field by mode.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flow-matching policies for imitation learning are passively stochastic—they produce diverse behaviors under repeated sampling but lack a direct handle for users to choose among valid continuations from the same state.

Method: Orthogonal Source Lifting lifts handle-specific sources into auxiliary orthogonal coordinates while keeping targets in the original action subspace, preventing path-crossing ambiguity. A state-dependent source mixture with a responsibility floor is learned end to end to keep handles usable across states and mitigate dead modes.

Result: SL-FM removes crossing-induced composite trajectories, changes future routes in 91.1% of matched-prefix interventions, and achieves strong free-deployment performance with improvements in several benchmark settings.

Conclusion: Source geometry provides actionable multimodal control without conditioning the velocity field on the selected mode, converting passive source randomness into an actionable intervention variable.

Abstract: Flow-matching policies are promising for imitation learning because they model complex multimodal action distributions. However, their stochasticity is largely passive: repeated sampling may yield diverse behaviors, but users cannot directly choose among valid continuations from the same state. We propose Source-Lifted Flow Matching (SL-FM), a source-intervenable flow-matching policy that exposes such a handle while keeping the velocity field shared and latent-free. The handle selects only the source endpoint of the conditional flow, not a mode-specific field, preserving the standard formulation while avoiding decomposition into separate mode-conditioned dynamics. The core mechanism is \textbf{Orthogonal Source Lifting}, designed to prevent path-crossing ambiguity. Instead of partitioning target actions by mode, SL-FM lifts handle-specific sources into auxiliary orthogonal coordinates and keeps targets in the original action subspace. This preserves the demonstrated action distribution while allowing one shared field to carry different branches without merging at crossings. To keep handles usable across states, we learn a state-dependent source mixture end to end and use a responsibility floor, giving each handle weak supervision and mitigating dead modes. Experiments on crossing-flow diagnostics and robot-control benchmarks show that SL-FM converts passive source randomness into an actionable intervention variable. It removes crossing-induced composite trajectories, changes future routes in 91.1\% of matched-prefix interventions, and achieves strong free-deployment performance, with improvements in several benchmark settings. Overall, source geometry provides actionable multimodal control without conditioning the velocity field on the selected mode.

</details>


### [147] [Diffusion-Residual Model Predictive Steering Control for Vehicle Stabilization at the Limit of Handling under Model Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10243)
*Bongsob Song*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A diffusion residual MPC (D-res) is proposed that learns uncertainty in yaw-rate reference and stable-handling envelope via a conditional diffusion model. It adjusts the reference mean and tightens constraints via chance back-off, with tabulated offline generation enabling real-time execution (4.08 ms/step on Jetson AGX Xavier). D-res reduces peak side-slip and restores directional stability on low-friction maneuvers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stabilizing MPC relies on a yaw-rate reference and stable-handling envelope that are operating-point-dependent and unknown a priori. Fixed or worst-case settings are either too conservative or unsafe.

Method: A conditional diffusion residual model learns the uncertainty. Its mean re-sizes the tracked yaw reference; its predictive spread tightens the stable-handling envelope via one-sided chance back-off over the prediction horizon. The generator is tabulated offline; online controller adds a single table lookup to baseline MPC (no in-loop diffusion).

Result: Runs within 100 Hz budget on NVIDIA Jetson AGX Xavier (worst-case 4.08 ms/step). Across 7-DOF model and high-fidelity CarMaker co-simulation, D-res reduces peak side-slip where fixed bicycle model is least accurate and restores directional stability on low-friction maneuvers where fixed reference over-commands grip.

Conclusion: D-res anticipates caution ahead of tracking error via learned uncertainty, rather than correcting after it with a high-gain loop, improving safety and performance over fixed-setting baselines.

Abstract: At the limit of handling, a stabilizing MPC depends on the yaw-rate reference it tracks and the stable-handling envelope it enforces, both operating-point-dependent and unknown a priori, so fixed or worst-case settings are either too conservative or unsafe. We learn this uncertainty with a conditional diffusion residual model and apply it to the controller's reference and constraints rather than its control law. Conditioned on the steering command, the model returns the residual's mean and a predictive spread: the mean re-sizes the tracked yaw reference, while the spread, propagated over the prediction horizon, tightens the stable-handling envelope through a one-sided chance back-off. Together these form the proposed diffusion-residual MPC (D-res), so caution is anticipated ahead of the tracking error rather than corrected after it by a high-gain loop. Because only two moments per command are needed, the generator is tabulated offline and the online controller adds a single table lookup to the baseline MPC, with no in-loop diffusion; it runs within the 100 Hz budget on an NVIDIA Jetson AGX Xavier (worst-case 4.08 ms per step). Across a 7-DOF model and high-fidelity CarMaker co-simulation spanning vehicle, tire, road, and maneuver diversity, D-res reduces peak side-slip where the fixed bicycle model is least accurate and restores directional stability on low-friction maneuvers, where the fixed reference over-commands the available grip.

</details>


### [148] [PIER-Flow: Physics-Informed Efficient Rectified Flow for Real-Time Mobile Robot Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10288)
*Shibo Li,Zhongcheng Wang,Jiahe Cao,Jianhua Yang,Ke Wu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PIER-Flow is a lightweight navigation policy that distills MPC expertise into a continuous-time ODE, achieving single-step action generation via parallel latent sampling and feasibility selection. It uses physics-informed training for kinematic consistency and asynchronous action chunking for robust deployment. It achieves 98.85% success rate, zero collisions, ~1.29 ms inference (37.2× faster than MPC, 800× faster than diffusion), and stable ~5.3 ms latency on edge devices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous navigation in dense, highly dynamic environments requires both physically feasible control and low-latency replanning. Existing methods struggle: MPC handles constraints but is slow due to repeated nonlinear optimization; behavior-cloning policies are fast but fail at multimodal avoidance; diffusion policies capture multimodality but suffer from time-consuming iterative denoising.

Method: PIER-Flow distills an MPC expert into a continuous-time ODE (Rectified Flow) for single-step action generation. It employs parallel latent sampling with lightweight feasibility selection, a physics-informed training objective enforcing kinematic consistency, and an asynchronous action chunking architecture for sim-to-real transfer.

Result: 98.85% success rate with zero collisions in simulation; average inference of ~1.29 ms (37.2× faster than MPC, over 800× faster than standard diffusion models); stable ~5.3 ms latency on a resource-constrained edge computer without latency spikes or freezing events.

Conclusion: PIER-Flow demonstrates that distilling MPC into a rectified flow ODE yields a navigation policy that is both physically consistent and extremely fast, suitable for real-time deployment on edge hardware in dense dynamic environments.

Abstract: Autonomous navigation in dense and highly dynamic environments requires both physically feasible control and low-latency replanning. Optimization-based methods such as Model Predictive Control (MPC) explicitly handle robot kinematics and safety constraints, but repeated nonlinear optimization can limit real-time responsiveness. Deterministic behavior-cloning policies enable efficient inference but may fail to represent multimodal avoidance behaviors, whereas diffusion policies capture multimodality at the cost of time-consuming iterative denoising. We propose PIER-Flow (Physics-Informed Efficient Rectified Flow), a lightweight navigation policy for mobile robots. By distilling an MPC expert into a continuous-time Ordinary Differential Equation (ODE), PIER-Flow achieves single-step action generation through parallel latent sampling and lightweight feasibility selection. We introduce a physics-informed training objective to enforce kinematic consistency, paired with an asynchronous action chunking architecture for robust sim-to-real deployment. Extensive simulations demonstrate that PIER-Flow achieves a 98.85\% success rate and zero collisions, with an average inference of $\sim$1.29 ms, which accelerates planning by 37.2$\times$ compared to MPC and over 800$\times$ against standard diffusion models. Crucially, real-world deployment on a resource-constrained edge computer further achieves an approximately stable inference latency of $\sim$5.3 ms, avoiding the latency spikes and freezing events observed with planning baselines.

</details>


### [149] [Soft Eversion Robots for Colonoscopy: Challenges, Open Problems, and Emerging Solutions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10294)
*Cem Suulker,Thomas Mack,Qingzheng Cong,Neri Niccolò Dei,Reza Kashef,Mohammad Sheikh Sofla,Kaspar Althoefer*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper benchmarks four eversion robot architectures for colonoscopy against clinical requirements, identifying trade-offs in steering, payload delivery, and softness, and provides design guidance for clinical translation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional colonoscopy causes patient discomfort and procedural risks; eversion robots offer a low-friction alternative, but no existing design meets all clinical requirements.

Method: Four recent eversion robot architectures are benchmarked against key anatomical and clinical constraints (colon length, luminal diameter, bending angle, working-channel needs), analyzing trade-offs between steering, payload delivery, and low-friction behavior.

Result: The central trade-offs are identified, particularly the difficulty of integrating steering and payload delivery while maintaining soft, low-friction behavior.

Conclusion: Design guidance is provided across material selection, steering strategy, and payload delivery, with open problems highlighted for clinical translation.

Abstract: Conventional colonoscopy remains limited by patient discomfort and procedural risks, motivating research into compliant robotic alternatives. Eversion robots, which advance via pressure-driven tip growth, eliminate sliding friction against the colon wall and offer a less intrusive approach to traversal. However, no existing design simultaneously satisfies all clinical requirements. This paper benchmarks four recent eversion robot architectures against the key anatomical and clinical constraints of colonoscopy, including colon length, minimum luminal diameter, bending angle, and working-channel needs. We identify the central trade-offs each design reveals, particularly the difficulty of integrating steering and payload delivery without sacrificing the soft, low-friction behaviour that makes eversion robots attractive. We then provide design guidance across material selection, steering strategy, and payload delivery, and highlight open problems for clinical translation.

</details>


### [150] [PrismAD: Decoupled Planning via Semantic Mixture-of-Planners for End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10336)
*Kang Ding,Zhigui Lin,Hongsong Wang,Jie Gui,Qi Liu,Zhe Wang,Luqi Tang,Lei He*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PrismAD is a decoupled end-to-end autonomous driving framework using a Semantic Mixture-of-Planners that partitions scene tokens into interaction, geometry, and intent groups, assigns them to independent planning experts, and uses a semantics-aware router with sparse top-K activation to achieve competitive performance on nuScenes and NeuroNCAP benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing planners aggregate heterogeneous scene tokens into a coupled representation space, forcing a single planning branch to jointly model agent interaction, road geometry, and driving intention, which weakens factor-specific reasoning and obscures the contribution of different planning cues.
