Method: PrismAD partitions scene tokens into interaction, geometry, and intent groups, assigns them to independent planning experts (same architecture, separate parameters), and uses a semantics-aware router with separate routing weights for motion prediction and ego planning. Sparse top-K activation with noisy gating is introduced for robustness and efficiency.

Result: Competitive performance demonstrated on nuScenes open-loop dataset and NeuroNCAP closed-loop benchmark.

Conclusion: The decoupled, factor-specialized planning approach (PrismAD) effectively addresses the coupling limitation of existing planners and achieves competitive autonomous driving performance.

Abstract: This letter presents PrismAD, a decoupled end-to-end autonomous driving framework based on a Semantic Mixture-of-Planners. Existing planners usually aggregate heterogeneous scene tokens into a coupled representation space, forcing a single planning branch to jointly model agent interaction, road geometry, and driving intention. Such coupling may weaken factor-specific reasoning and obscure the contribution of different planning cues. To address this limitation, PrismAD partitions scene tokens into interaction, geometry, and intent groups, and assigns them to independent planning experts with the same architecture but separate parameters. Each expert learns a specialized motion-planning representation, while a semantics-aware router adaptively aggregates expert predictions with separate routing weights for motion prediction and ego planning. Sparse top-$K$ activation with noisy gating is further introduced to improve routing robustness and reduce unnecessary expert computation. Extensive experiments on the nuScenes open-loop dataset and NeuroNCAP closed-loop benchmark demonstrate that PrismAD exhibits competitive performance. Our code will be released soon.

</details>


### [151] [VINE: Taming Generative Control Policies for Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10369)
*Rushuai Yang,Zhuo Han,Houlin Li,Hecheng Wang,Zhichao Wu,Rui Zhang,Zhaowei Zhang,Zihong Chen,Xiaohan Yan,Chiming Liu,Yi Chen,Wei Shan,Maoqing Yao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Flow-matching policies suffer from training instability under value-gradient RL not due to iterative generation itself, but due to the vanilla sampling strategy from behavior cloning. The proposed VINE method uses a new interpolation-based sampling strategy that enables stable end-to-end value-gradient optimization while preserving expressiveness and iterative generation, outperforming SOTA RL methods on OGBench and real-world robotic tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work observed that scaling flow-matching policies with value-gradient RL leads to training instability, and existing methods attribute this to iterative generation—thus sacrificing either iterative generation, expressiveness, or value-gradient optimization. The authors aimed to identify the true source of instability and develop a method that preserves all three properties.

Method: VINE proposes an RL-oriented sampling method that, instead of following a single flow trajectory, reconstructs a new interpolation state at every denoising step, creating a stable differentiable path for value-gradient propagation while remaining compatible with the original flow-matching denoising process.

Result: Despite performing end-to-end backpropagation through all ten denoising steps, VINE achieves stable policy improvement and consistently outperforms state-of-the-art RL methods on the OGBench offline RL benchmark and real-world robotic manipulation task.

Conclusion: Instability in value-gradient RL for flow-matching policies does not stem from iterative generation but from the vanilla sampling strategy. VINE enables stable end-to-end value-gradient optimization for flow-matching policies without sacrificing expressiveness or iterative generation.

Abstract: Flow-matching policies have emerged as an effective policy parameterization for robot learning. They iteratively generate actions from noise, enabling highly expressive modeling of complex and multimodal action distributions. However, prior works observed that scaling these policies with value-gradient reinforcement learning (RL) often leads to training instability. Existing methods attribute this instability to iterative generation and therefore avoid end-to-end value-gradient optimization by sacrificing iterative generation, high expressiveness, or value-gradient optimization. Contrary to prior belief, we show the instability does not stem from iterative generation itself, but from the vanilla sampling strategy originally designed for behavior cloning, which becomes brittle under value-gradient RL. Motivated by this insight, we propose VINE, an RL-oriented sampling method that enables stable end-to-end value-gradient optimization for flow-matching policies. Instead of following a single flow trajectory, VINE reconstructs a new interpolation state at every denoising step, creating a stable differentiable path for value-gradient propagation while remaining compatible with the original flow-matching denoising process. As a result, VINE preserves the expressiveness and iterative generation of flow-matching without sacrificing end-to-end value-gradient optimization. Despite performing end-to-end backpropagation through all ten denoising steps, VINE achieves stable policy improvement and consistently outperforms state-of-the-art RL methods on the OGBench offline RL benchmark and real-world robotic manipulation task. Videos are available on our website: https://agibottech.github.io/vine.

</details>


### [152] [Robotic Contextual Awareness for Human-Robot Collaboration and Environmental Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10372)
*Federico Rollo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This thesis addresses the challenge of autonomous mobile robots operating in dynamic human-centric environments by developing two complementary solutions: (1) a human re-identification and tracking approach for targeted Human-Robot Collaboration, and (2) enhanced geometric and semantic environmental perception for improved contextual awareness, both aimed at enabling safer coexistence and more effective collaboration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous mobile robots transitioning from controlled industrial settings to dynamic human-centric environments (manufacturing, logistics, healthcare) face a critical obstacle: lack of comprehensive contextual awareness needed to perceive, understand, and interact with surroundings, plan adaptive behaviors, and engage in meaningful human interaction.

Method: Two complementary research directions: (1) Human re-identification and tracking approach enabling a mobile robot to recognize specific individuals for targeted collaboration; (2) Enhancement of perceptual capabilities for geometric understanding (motion planning, collision avoidance) and semantic understanding (richer environment knowledge for advanced interaction).

Result: The developed human re-identification approach enables a mobile robot to recognize a specific person while ignoring others, facilitating targeted collaboration. The environmental understanding approach improves both geometric and semantic perception of surroundings.

Conclusion: The contributions in human re-identification and environmental understanding represent a significant step toward more contextually aware robots, enabling safer coexistence and more effective collaboration between robots, humans, and environments.

Abstract: The transition of autonomous mobile robots from controlled industrial settings to dynamic, human-centric environments, such as manufacturing, logistics, and healthcare, has made their safe and autonomous operation a critical area of research. These sophisticated machines must be capable of perceiving, understanding, and interacting with their surroundings to navigate freely and perform complex tasks. A significant obstacle to achieving this is the lack of comprehensive contextual awareness, which requires a robot to recognize its spatial environment and identify the objects and actors within it. Without this perceptual knowledge, robots struggle to plan adaptive behaviors or engage in meaningful interaction with humans.
  This thesis presents novel solutions to this challenge by exploring two distinct but complementary research directions. The first direction involves human re-identification and tracking to improve Human-Robot Collaboration. Our developed approach enables a mobile robot to recognize a specific person, facilitating targeted collaboration while ignoring other individuals. The second direction focuses on enhancing the robot's overall perceptual capabilities to understand its environment geometrically and semantically. Geometric information is vital for motion planning and collision avoidance, while semantic knowledge provides the robot with a richer understanding for more advanced interaction. Both solutions are driven by the improvement of the semantical understanding of robots that enhance their knowledge of their surroundings, allowing a smoother and more natural interaction between robots, humans, and the environment. The contributions of this work in human re-identification and environmental understanding represent a significant step toward a future where robots are more contextually aware, enabling safer coexistence and more effective collaboration.

</details>


### [153] [Navigating the Crowd: Non-linear MPC with Social Forces Dynamics for Human-Aware Robot Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10374)
*Stefano Trepella,Andrea Ostuni,Mauro Martini,Pablo Pueyo,Noé Pérez-Higueras,Marcello Chiaberge,Fernando Caballero,Luis Merino*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SFM-NMPC is a Social Force Model-based Non-linear Model Predictive Control framework that embeds human motion prediction within the optimization loop, enabling socially compliant robot navigation in crowded environments at real-time 20 Hz performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous robots operating in human-populated environments need to go beyond collision avoidance and must anticipate human motion and respect personal space to ensure human comfort.

Method: The authors propose SFM-NMPC, which embeds the Social Force Model into the dynamic model of surrounding agents within a Non-linear MPC framework, allowing joint robot-human trajectory prediction over the prediction horizon, combined with tailored social cost functions to guide optimization toward human-compliant behaviors.

Result: SFM-NMPC runs in real-time at 20 Hz and outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in social compliance metrics with efficient and smooth navigation in crowded simulated environments.

Conclusion: The embedded SFM dynamics and social cost terms are effective for real-world social navigation, as confirmed by visual trajectory analysis and an ablation study.

Abstract: Safe and socially compliant navigation remains a fundamental challenge for autonomous robots operating in human-populated environments. Beyond collision avoidance, robots must anticipate human motion and respect personal space to ensure human comfort. Model Predictive Control (MPC) offers a robust alternative to classical and data-driven methods, although its effectiveness strongly depends on accurate human motion prediction and efficient computation. This paper introduces SFM-NMPC, a Social Force Model-based Non-linear Model Predictive Control framework that embeds human motion prediction directly within the optimization loop. By incorporating the Social Force Model into the dynamic model of surrounding agents, the controller jointly predicts the trajectories of humans and robots over the prediction horizon, thereby enabling socially-aware planning. A tailored set of social cost functions guides the optimization toward human-compliant behaviors. Despite the increased model complexity, the proposed formulation runs in real time at 20 Hz. Extensive simulated testing in crowded environments demonstrates that SFM-NMPC outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in social compliance metrics while maintaining efficient and smooth navigation. Visual trajectory analysis and an ablation study further highlight the contribution of the embedded SFM dynamics and social cost terms, confirming the effectiveness of the proposed approach for real-world social navigation.

</details>


### [154] [Interleaved POMDP Planning for Multi-Object Search in Unknown Multi-Room Household Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10437)
*Ruochu Yang,Ziyi Xia,Huibo Zhang,Yatong Han,Yiming Zhao,Yingke Li,Fumin Zhang,Yorai Wardi,Mengxue Hou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Inter-POMDP is an interleaved POMDP planning algorithm for multi-object search in unknown household environments, using LLM-informed high-level beliefs and obstacle-aware low-level motion planning, reducing collisions by 63%, navigation steps by 35%, and detection counts by 32%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-object search in unknown household environments involves extensive uncertainty from unknown object locations and cluttered obstacles. POMDPs offer a principled framework but remain intractable in large domains.

Method: Inter-POMDP decomposes planning into two interacting levels: a high-level POUCT planner using LLM-informed histogram beliefs for object distributions, and a low-level motion planner modeling navigation uncertainty with obstacle-aware particle beliefs as domain knowledge to guide high-level planning.

Result: In simulation and real-world experiments, Inter-POMDP reduces collision counts by up to 63%, navigation steps by up to 35%, and detection counts by up to 32% compared with baseline methods.

Conclusion: The interleaved design balances planning quality and efficiency despite large search spaces across unknown multi-room environments.

Abstract: Multi-object search in unknown household environments requires planning under extensive uncertainty - from unknown object locations to cluttered spaces with unobserved obstacles. POMDPs offer a principled framework for such problems but remain intractable in large domains. We propose Inter-POMDP, a novel interleaved POMDP planning algorithm that decomposes this challenge into two interacting levels: a high-level POUCT planner reasons over object distributions using LLM-informed histogram beliefs, while a low-level motion planner models navigation uncertainty with obstacle-aware particle beliefs as domain knowledge to guide high-level POUCT. This interleaved design balances planning quality and efficiency despite the large search space across unknown multi-room environments. Both simulation and real-world experiments show that our Inter-POMDP algorithm reduces collision counts by up to 63%, navigation steps by up to 35%, and detection counts by up to 32% compared with baseline methods. Full videos are https://sites.google.com/view/inter-pomdp

</details>


### [155] [Large Language Model Enhanced Differentiable Trajectory Planning for IoT-Enabled Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10438)
*Shihao Zhang,Jing Yang,Ziyu Song,Zheng Lin,Sunil Prajapat,Zhaochen Xia,Hemant Ghayvat,Haitao Ding,Lip Yee Por,Ashok Kumar Das*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes an LLM-enhanced differentiable trajectory planning framework for IoT-enabled autonomous driving, featuring a surrounding agent-centric data augmentation strategy, a complexity-aware asynchronous LLM-based semantic enhancement module, and a differentiable optimization module, achieving state-of-the-art performance on nuPlan benchmarks and verified through CARLA-ROS real-time closed-loop tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: IL-based planners suffer from limited coverage of complex long-tail interactions, weak consistency with downstream constrained refinement, and insufficient use of high-level scene semantics under real-time constraints.

Method: 1) Surrounding agent-centric data augmentation for training distribution improvement; 2) Complexity-aware asynchronous LLM-based semantic enhancement for high-level scene semantics; 3) Differentiable optimization module with explicit residual penalties and backpropagated gradients for trajectory refinement.

Result: Achieves best overall scores of 83.63 and 78.29 on nuPlan closed-loop nonreactive and reactive Hard20 benchmarks; CARLA-ROS tests confirm real-time closed-loop deployment capability.

Conclusion: The proposed LLM-enhanced differentiable framework effectively addresses key limitations of IL-based planners, improving trajectory safety, efficiency, and executability in complex urban IoT-enabled autonomous driving scenarios.

Abstract: Autonomous driving planning is a key component of IoT-enabled intelligent transportation systems, requiring vehicles to generate safe, efficient, and executable trajectories in complex urban environments from multi-source contextual information. While imitation learning (IL) has shown promise on large-scale datasets, IL-based planners still suffer from limited coverage of complex long-tail interactions, weak consistency with downstream constrained refinement, and insufficient use of high level scene semantics under real time constraints. To address these issues, this paper proposes a large language model (LLM) enhanced differentiable trajectory planning framework for IoT-enabled autonomous driving. Specifically, we introduce a surrounding agent centric data augmentation strategy to reorganize sur rounding agent trajectories as additional planning supervision, thereby improving the training distribution without collecting additional raw data. We further design a complexity-aware asyn chronous LLM-based semantic enhancement module to extract scene-related high-level semantic features with controlled online overhead. In addition, a differentiable optimization module is incorporated to refine generated trajectories with explicit residual penalties while backpropagating optimization gradients to the upstream planner. Experiments show that the proposed method achieves the best overall scores of 83.63 and 78.29 on the nuPlan closed-loop nonreactive and reactive Hard20 benchmarks, respectively, and CARLA-ROS tests further verify its online deployment and real time closed-loop execution capability.

</details>


### [156] [SUREFlow: State-space Uncertainty-aware REsidual Flow Matching for Robust Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10504)
*Md Tanvir Islam,Sai Navaneet Peddapalli,Sangmoon Lee,Sangtae Ahn*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SUREFlow is a state-space uncertainty-aware residual flow matching framework for robot manipulation that jointly predicts action velocities and input-dependent residual uncertainty, achieving 92.5% success rate on LIBERO and ~49% on LIBERO-PRO with only 179M parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion and flow-based policies exhibit instability under noise, partial observability, and stochastic conditions; small velocity errors accumulate during extended rollouts, and current methods lack explicit uncertainty modeling within action generation.

Method: SUREFlow uses a Mamba backbone with state-space uncertainty-aware residual flow matching; it jointly predicts action velocities and input-dependent residual uncertainty, enabling selective refinement of unreliable action dimensions without environment feedback.

Result: 92.5% average success rate on LIBERO (34.2% improvement over MaIL); ~49% SR on LIBERO-PRO with only 179M parameters, comparable to large VLAs with 3-7B parameters.

Conclusion: SUREFlow demonstrates that explicit uncertainty modeling in flow matching policies significantly improves robustness and efficiency, achieving strong performance with orders of magnitude fewer parameters than large vision-language-action models.

Abstract: Generative vision-language-action policies have advanced robot manipulation, but they often exhibit instability under noise, partial observability, and stochastic initial conditions. During extended rollouts, small velocity errors accumulate, degrading execution reliability. Existing diffusion and flow-based policies typically assume homoscedastic residuals and lack explicit uncertainty modeling within action generation, limiting robustness during iterative rollout. We propose SUREFlow, a state-space uncertainty-aware residual flow matching framework built on a Mamba backbone. The method jointly predicts action velocities and input-dependent residual uncertainty, enabling selective refinement of unreliable action dimensions without environment feedback while preserving computational efficiency. On LIBERO, SUREFlow achieves 92.5% average success rate (SR), outperforming the Mamba-based MaIL by 34.2%. On LIBERO-PRO, it attains around 49% SR using only 179M parameters, achieving performance comparable to large VLAs with 3-7B parameters. SUREFlow source code is available on: https://github.com/tanvirnwu/SUREFlow

</details>


### [157] [SLIDER: Sparse History-Guided Aerial Robot Target Search using Sliding Local Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10553)
*Xiaolei Hou,Zheng Pan,Hua Lan,Zhenghao Zou,Yinhong Chen,Chenxi Zhu,Yang Lyu,Jinwen Hu,Chunhui Zhao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SLIDER is a lightweight, memory-efficient framework for aerial robot exploration that uses a local sliding map and sparse global history to achieve efficient frontier detection, incremental viewpoint clustering, and sparse global topological mapping, outperforming state-of-the-art methods in memory usage, decision latency, and search efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficient exploration and target search in large-scale unknown environments remain challenging for aerial robots due to the demands of broad spatial coverage, fine-grained perception, and real-time decision-making, and existing methods relying on globally dense maps are memory-intensive and computationally heavy.

Method: SLIDER combines a local sliding map with sparse global history information, proposes a novel observation quality evaluation method using historical poses and sensor models for real-time point cloud assessment and efficient frontier detection, employs an incremental viewpoint clustering strategy that dynamically adapts to local updates to reduce candidate targets and computational load, and incrementally maintains a sparse global topological map for planning and cost evaluation.

Result: Extensive simulations and real-world experiments demonstrate that the proposed system outperforms state-of-the-art methods in memory usage, decision latency, and search efficiency.

Conclusion: SLIDER provides a lightweight and memory-efficient solution for aerial robot exploration that avoids globally dense maps while maintaining competitive search performance.

Abstract: Efficient exploration and target search in large-scale unknown environments remain challenging for aerial robots due to the demands of broad spatial coverage, fine-grained perception, and real-time decision-making. This paper presents SLIDER, a lightweight and memory-efficient framework that avoids reliance on globally dense maps by combining a local sliding map with sparse global history information. A novel observation quality evaluation method is proposed, leveraging historical poses and sensor models to assess point cloud data in real-time, enabling efficient frontier detection. To support scalable and responsive planning, an incremental viewpoint clustering strategy dynamically adapts to local updates, significantly reducing the number of candidate targets and decreasing computational load. A sparse global topological map is incrementally maintained to assist global planning and cost evaluation. Extensive simulations and real-world experiments demonstrate that the proposed system outperforms state-of-the-art methods in memory usage, decision latency, and search efficiency.

</details>


### [158] [BucketKD: A Safety-Aware Bucket-Based Knowledge Distillation Framework for End-to-End Motion Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10565)
*Md Nahidul Islam,Mohd Hasan Ali,Dipankar Dasgupta,Myounggyu Won*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: BucketKD is a bucket-based knowledge distillation framework for end-to-end motion planning in autonomous driving that discretizes environmental variables into adaptive buckets and uses a safety-aware waypoint attention mechanism with time-to-collision formulation to produce compact, safety-aware planners, outperforming state-of-the-art in planning accuracy and safety on CARLA/Bench2Drive.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: End-to-end motion planners suffer from large model sizes that hinder deployment on resource-constrained platforms; existing distillation approaches rely on simplified planning state representations that lack rich scene semantics.

Method: (1) Bucket-based knowledge distillation framework that discretizes critical environmental variables into adaptive buckets for richer scene semantics while preserving efficiency; (2) Safety-aware waypoint attention mechanism that evaluates each waypoint's risk level using obstacle proximity and relative motion via time-to-collision (TTC) formulation.

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art in both planning accuracy and safety while maintaining strong compression ratios, validated extensively in CARLA using the Bench2Drive dataset.

Conclusion: BucketKD effectively yields compact and safety-aware end-to-end planners, demonstrating that bucket-based discretization and safety-aware attention can bridge the gap between model compression and safety-critical behavior retention.

Abstract: End-to-end motion planning has emerged as a promising paradigm in autonomous driving, directly mapping raw sensor data to control commands via deep neural networks. Despite its advantages, its large model size hinders deployment in resource-constrained platforms. In this paper, we present BucketKD, a bucket-based knowledge distillation framework that yields compact and safety-aware end-to-end planners. Compared to the state-of-the-art approach, which relies on simplified planning state representations, BucketKD discretizes critical environmental variables into adaptive buckets that capture richer scene semantics while preserving efficiency. In addition, we design a safety-aware waypoint attention mechanism that evaluates each waypoint's risk level by accounting for both obstacle proximity and relative motion through a time-to-collision (TTC) formulation widely used in transportation research. This enables the student model to better retain safety-critical behaviors during distillation. Extensive experiments in CARLA using the Bench2Drive dataset show that BucketKD significantly outperforms the state-of-the-art in both planning accuracy and safety while maintaining strong compression ratios.

</details>


### [159] [Underwater Dead Reckoning with Deployable Situation-Triggered Covariance Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10597)
*Akshay Naik,Ramavarapu S. Sreenivas,Dustin Nottage,Ahmet Soylemezoglu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a Situation-Triggered Calibrated Adaptive Robust Extended Kalman Filter for BlueROV2 underwater vehicle dead reckoning. An onboard probabilistic trigger identifies motion situations and switches between pre-calibrated noise matrices without resetting the filter. Validation shows modest but consistent improvement (0.488m to 0.471m RMSE) over fixed-tuning approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed filter parameters for underwater dead reckoning work well in normal conditions but are poorly matched during turns, motion transitions, or when sensor measurements are less reliable, motivating the need for situation-aware adaptive filtering.

Method: A probabilistic trigger identifies the current motion situation; when confident, the filter switches to pre-calibrated process/measurement noise matrices without resetting state estimates, covariance, or dynamics. Calibration uses sparse AprilTag-supervised pool runs with offline optimization of trigger thresholds, noise profiles, and DVL yaw-alignment correction. A separate validation set selects the scheduling policy, which is fixed before held-out testing.

Result: Across four held-out pool runs, label-weighted mean per-run translation RMSE reduced from 0.488m to 0.471m. Every held-out run favored the scheduled method. Paired bootstrap over 10-second segments yielded -0.017m difference (95% CI: [-0.024, -0.008]m). Orientation error remained essentially unchanged.

Conclusion: Situation-aware covariance scheduling provides a modest but consistent vision-free dead-reckoning improvement without switching estimators or resetting the filter, demonstrating the viability of selective noise matrix adaptation for underwater vehicles.

Abstract: Underwater dead reckoning estimates vehicle position when vision is unavailable and external positioning cannot be assumed. A single set of filter parameters can work well in many situations, but fixed tuning may be poorly matched during turns, motion transitions, or periods when sensor measurements are less reliable. This paper presents the Situation-Triggered Calibrated Adaptive Robust Extended Kalman Filter for a BlueROV2. An onboard probabilistic trigger identifies the current motion situation while one error-state filter runs continuously. When the trigger is confident, the filter changes only to the corresponding pre-calibrated process- and measurement-noise matrices; the state estimate, covariance history, dynamics, and measurement models are not reset or replaced. The trigger, noise profiles, and a one-time Doppler velocity log yaw-alignment correction are calibrated offline using sparse AprilTag-supervised pool runs. A separate validation set selects the scheduling policy, which is then fixed before held-out testing. Across four held-out pool runs, the method reduces label-weighted mean per-run translation root-mean-square error from 0.488 m to 0.471 m relative to the same filter backbone with one global noise profile, and every held-out run favors the scheduled method. A paired bootstrap over 10-second segments gives a candidate-minus-baseline difference of -0.017 m with a 95% confidence interval of [-0.024, -0.008] m, while orientation error remains essentially unchanged. These results indicate that situation-aware covariance scheduling provides a modest but consistent vision-free dead-reckoning improvement without switching estimators or resetting the filter.

</details>


### [160] [Dual-Process Atomic Skill Learning: Decoupling Semantic Reasoning and Real-Time Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10625)
*Jun Chen,Erdent Bao,Wenlong Dong,Jierui Liu,Qi Cai,Hao Wan,Shaopeng Li,Weijun Qin,Jing Liang,Huiping Zhuang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DASL proposes a dual-process asynchronous hierarchical imitation learning framework that decouples slow semantic reasoning (Slow-Frequency Policy with VQ) from fast motion control (High-Frequency Policy with latent diffusion + Decision Transformer), mitigating codebook collapse and achieving superior compositional generalization on multi-step instruction following tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing hierarchical IL methods for language-conditioned tasks suffer from training instability and codebook collapse due to tight coupling between high-level skill reasoning and low-level action generation in joint training paradigms.

Method: DASL decouples skill reasoning and action generation into two asynchronous policies: (1) Slow-Frequency Policy uses Vector Quantization to predict discrete, interpretable skills; (2) High-Frequency Policy employs a latent diffusion model + Decision Transformer to generate precise actions conditioned on those latent skills.

Result: DASL significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on simulation benchmarks and real-world experiments, excelling in skill acquisition and compositional generalization to unseen instructions.

Conclusion: Asynchronously coordinating the two modules and using diffusion to structure the latent space mitigates skill codebook interference, enabling more stable training and better generalization for multi-step compositional tasks.

Abstract: Language-conditioned Imitation Learning (IL) is essential for enabling robots to perform complex tasks following natural language instructions. However, generalizing to multi-step compositional tasks remains a significant challenge. While hierarchical approaches attempt to address this by decomposing tasks into atomic skills, existing methods often suffer from training instability and codebook collapse due to the tight coupling between high-level skill reasoning and low-level action generation in joint training paradigms. Inspired by the Dual-Process Theory of cognition, we propose Dual-Process Atomic Skill Learning (DASL), a novel asynchronous hierarchical imitation learning framework that decouples slow semantic reasoning from fast, real-time motion control. DASL comprises a Slow-Frequency Policy that predicts interpretable, discrete skills via Vector Quantization, and a High-Frequency Policy that leverages a latent diffusion model and a Decision Transformer to generate precise actions conditioned on these latent skills. By asynchronously coordinating these modules and utilizing diffusion to structure the latent space, our framework mitigates the skill codebook interference problem common in joint training paradigms. Evaluations across simulation benchmarks and experiment demonstrate that DASL significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, excelling in skill acquisition and compositional generalization to unseen instructions. GitHub page: https://github.com/Hatakekaka/DASL

</details>


### [161] [World Models as Adversaries: Multi-Agent Self-Play Fine-Tuning for Robust Motion Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10630)
*Tong Nie,Yuewen Mei,Junlin He,Yihong Tang,Jian Sun,Wei Ma*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AWM is a self-play fine-tuning framework for robust motion planning that casts adversarial training as a constrained min-max game, using a decoupled solver with a role-conditioned adversary and regret-aware robust best response, achieving competitive closed-loop performance in both nominal and long-tail traffic scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robust motion planning in dense traffic requires handling rare, safety-critical scenarios underrepresented in naturalistic data; existing adversarial training methods rely on external generators or heuristic perturbations, making them hard to integrate with modern autoregressive planners.

Method: AWM formulates adversarial planner learning as a constrained min-max game with a decoupled solver: (inner) converts the planner's world model into a role-conditioned adversary using counterfactual credit assignment for sparse scene-adaptive attacks; (outer) optimizes a regret-aware robust best response with tail-risk weighting and reference-anchored trust regions.

Result: Experiments on nuPlan and InterPlan benchmarks show AWM generates transferable adversarial interactions and achieves competitive closed-loop performance in both nominal and highly interactive long-tail scenarios; theoretical analysis justifies the decoupled solver and optimization components.

Conclusion: AWM provides a theoretically grounded, integrable framework for adversarial robust planning via self-play fine-tuning, effectively bridging the gap between adversarial training and autoregressive planners.

Abstract: Robust motion planning in dense traffic requires autonomous vehicles to interact in rare and safety-critical scenarios that are underrepresented in naturalistic driving data. Although adversarial training offers a feasible solution, existing methods often rely on external scenario generators, heuristic perturbations, or simulator-heavy rollouts, which makes them difficult to integrate with modern autoregressive planners. Here, we cast adversarially robust planner learning as a constrained min-max game and propose Adversarial World Modeling (AWM), a theoretically grounded multi-agent self-play fine-tuning framework. Since solving the exact game is intractable, AWM introduces a principled decoupled solver. In the inner minimization, the planner's predictive world model is converted into a role-conditioned adversary that learns sparse, scene-adaptive attack coalitions via counterfactual credit assignment. In the outer maximization, the ego planner optimizes a regret-aware robust best response against the frozen AWM, utilizing tail-risk weighting and reference-anchored trust regions to improve hard-case recovery while preserving nominal driving behavior. Experiments on the nuPlan and InterPlan benchmarks demonstrate that our method generates transferable adversarial interactions and yields a robust planner that achieves competitive closed-loop performance in both nominal and highly interactive long-tail scenarios. Theoretical analysis justifies the decoupled solver and the main optimization components.

</details>


### [162] [Coverage Path Planning: Classical Foundations, Recent Advances, and Future Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10649)
*Zongyuan Shen,Shalabh Gupta,Shancheng Zhao,Dehua Zhou,Gao Wang,Zhongqiang Ren,Yaming Ou,Yikui Zhai,C. L. Philip Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A comprehensive survey of 125 coverage path planning works (2015–2026), organized into six categories: single-robot, multi-robot, 3D, constrained, learning-based, and visual CPP, identifying open challenges in scalable online planning, coordination, and learning-enhanced coverage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CPP is fundamental in robotics with widespread applications; recent advances have extended beyond classical single-robot planar settings, necessitating a structured survey to organize and compare these emerging methods.

Method: Reviewed 125 representative works (2015–2026) against classical CPP methods (<2015), categorized into six groups: single-robot, multi-robot, 3D, constrained, learning-based, and visual CPP; analyzed formulations, algorithms, strengths/limitations per category.

Result: Six-category taxonomy; analysis of how environmental knowledge, workspace geometry, robot constraints, sensing objectives, and coordination requirements shape CPP; identification of open challenges.

Conclusion: Structured overview of recent CPP developments and future research directions in scalable online planning, multi-robot coordination, 3D/visual coverage, resource-aware coverage, and learning-enhanced coverage.

Abstract: Coverage path planning (CPP) is a fundamental problem in robot motion planning, whose aim is to produce robot trajectories that provide complete coverage of target workspaces while minimizing task-specific objectives such as path length, overlap, number of turns, and energy consumption. CPP has widespread applications in cleaning, inspection, mapping, agriculture, manufacturing, surveillance, demining, and environmental monitoring. Although classical CPP has been extensively studied, recent advances have extended CPP beyond single-robot settings to multi-robot systems, complex 3D environments, constrained platforms, learning-based coverage planning, and visual coverage tasks. This paper presents a comprehensive survey of 125 representative works published primarily between 2015 and 2026, while presenting the evolution of recent developments in light of the classical CPP methods published before 2015. The CPP methods are organized into six main categories: single-robot CPP, multi-robot CPP, 3D CPP, constrained CPP, learning-based CPP, and visual CPP. For each category, the review summarizes the main planning formulations, representative algorithms, strengths, and limitations. In addition, the review analyzes how environmental knowledge, workspace geometry, robot constraints, sensing objectives, and coordination requirements shape the CPP problem. The survey further discusses open challenges in scalable online planning, multi-robot coordination, 3D and visual coverage, unified platform-constrained and resource-aware coverage, and learning-enhanced coverage. Thus, the survey provides a structured overview of recent CPP developments and future research directions.

</details>


### [163] [Artificial Foveated Perception for Mitigating Shortcut Learning in Robotic Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10655)
*Xiatao Sun,Yuan Zhuang,Mateo Sanchez Lopez Negrete,Matei-Victor Coldea,Chen Liang,Haoyang Zhang,Che Liu,Ziyao Zeng,Shawn Li,Qian Wang,Fei Miao,Daniel Rakita*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper identifies shortcut learning (exploiting spurious correlations) as a key failure mode in robotic foundation models, and proposes Artificial Foveated Perception (AFP)—a lightweight module that generates task-conditioned masks to guide policy attention toward task-relevant regions during fine-tuning. AFP reduces overfitting, speeds up fine-tuning, and improves generalization across state-of-the-art robotic foundation models without altering the core architecture or requiring runtime mask inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robotic foundation models struggle to generalize across diverse real-world settings due to shortcut learning—policies often rely on spurious scene-level correlations instead of task-relevant visual evidence. While shortcut learning is well-studied in vision and ML, its role in robotic foundation models remains underexplored.

Method: Proposes Artificial Foveated Perception (AFP), a lightweight, policy-agnostic module that takes vision and language inputs and predicts task-conditioned masks over relevant objects, the robot, and action-critical regions. AFP is used as an auxiliary grounding signal during fine-tuning to align policy attention, without requiring runtime inference or architectural changes.

Result: AFP reduces fine-tuning time, suppresses overfitting, and improves generalization under environmental perturbations across state-of-the-art robotic foundation models. Ablations confirm that gains come from directing policy learning toward task-relevant visual evidence.

Conclusion: Task-conditioned foveated perception is a practical, lightweight mechanism for making robotic foundation models more robust, data-efficient, and scalable.

Abstract: Robotic foundation models have recently made substantial progress in multi-task capability, cross-embodiment transfer, and language-conditioned control. Yet robust deployment across diverse real-world settings remains difficult, in part because policies often fail to distinguish causally relevant visual structure from spurious scene-level correlations. We identify this failure mode as shortcut learning: the tendency to exploit predictive but non-causal correlations in the training distribution rather than the task-relevant visual evidence that determines successful action. Although shortcut learning has been extensively studied in computer vision and broader machine learning, its role in robotic foundation models remains comparatively underexplored. We propose Artificial Foveated Perception (AFP), a lightweight, policy-agnostic module that takes the same vision and language inputs as Vision-Language-Action and World Action Model pipelines and predicts task-conditioned masks over relevant objects, the robot, and other action-critical regions. We use these masks primarily as an auxiliary grounding signal during fine-tuning, aligning policy attention with task-relevant regions while leaving the core architecture unchanged. After fine-tuning, the policy executes on the original observation stream without requiring AFP in the control loop. We evaluate AFP across state-of-the-art robotic foundation models and show that foveated perception reduces fine-tuning time, suppresses overfitting, and improves generalization under environmental perturbations. Ablations over mask quality and grounding-loss design further show that these gains arise from directing policy learning toward task-relevant visual evidence. These results suggest that task-conditioned foveated perception is a practical mechanism for making robotic foundation models more robust, data-efficient, and scalable.

</details>


### [164] [SensorPerch: Sense Wherever and Whenever it Matters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10682)
*Zhanxin Wu,Ruofei Tong,Tapomayukh Bhattacharjee*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SensorPerch is a novel active perception system where sensors are decoupled from robots and fixed infrastructure, allowing robots to autonomously place wireless sensor platforms on diverse surfaces to create task-optimal viewpoints on demand.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing robotic perception is constrained by robot-mounted or fixed environmental sensors, limiting viewpoints. As tasks diversify, the optimal viewpoint varies across tasks, and neither onboard sensors nor static infrastructure can satisfy these shifting requirements.

Method: SensorPerch consists of a lightweight, wireless, reconfigurable sensor platform that can perch on diverse surfaces, paired with a viewpoint-selection framework that determines task-optimal sensor placements.

Result: Demonstrated on two task classes: (i) object-coupled perception—enables persistent object-state detection beyond the robot's current position, achieving successful event detection even when the robot is not nearby; (ii) policy-coupled perception—allows robots to construct diverse policy-specific viewpoints, achieving success rates comparable to oracle viewpoints.

Conclusion: Decoupling sensing from robot embodiment and fixed infrastructure enables robots to construct task-relevant viewpoints on demand, improving perception flexibility and performance across diverse tasks.

Abstract: Existing robotic perception is constrained by sensors that are either robot-mounted or permanently fixed in the environment, locking perception to a limited set of viewpoints. Yet as robots perform increasingly diverse tasks, the most informative viewpoint shifts from one task to the next-often somewhere onboard sensor and static infrastructure can not readily satisfy. To address this gap, we propose SensorPerch, a novel realization of active perception that decouples sensing from both the robot embodiment and the environment by treating sensors as independent physical entities that the robot can autonomously detach and re-attach within the environment. SensorPerch presents one realization of this paradigm: a lightweight, wireless, reconfigurable sensor platform that can perch on diverse surfaces, paired with a viewpoint-selection framework that determines task-optimal sensor placements. Together, these enable robots to construct task-relevant viewpoints on demand, independent of the robot's current position and available fixed infrastructure. We demonstrate the paradigm on two task classes: (i) object-coupled perception, where SensorPerch enables persistent object-state detection beyond the robot's current position, achieving successful event detection even when the robot is not nearby; and (ii) policy-coupled perception, where SensorPerch allows robots to construct diverse, policy-specific viewpoints for various policies, achieving success rates comparable to those obtained using oracle viewpoints.

</details>


### [165] [Action Map Policy: Learning 3D Closed-loop Manipulation via Pixel Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10706)
*Haojie Huang,Zhang Ye,Linfeng Zhao,Boce Hu,Mingxi Jia,Yu Qi,Ahmed Agha,Dian Wang,Robert Platt,Robin Walters*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AMP projects 3D robot actions onto 2D camera image planes, treating pixel locations as discrete classes for action classification, achieving efficient, multi-modal, high-precision manipulation policy learning without complex diffusion processes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The action space in robot learning is high-dimensional, spans long horizons, and admits multi-modal optimal solutions, making representation and loss function design challenging with existing trade-offs.

Method: AMP casts 3D manipulation policy learning as a classification problem in image space by projecting 3D actions onto camera image planes and treating each pixel location as a discrete class, controlling vocabulary size while preserving multi-modality and precision. The entire action chunk is predicted in a single forward pass.

Result: AMP outperforms strong baselines across various manipulation tasks with higher success rates, faster inference, and enhanced spatial reasoning, avoiding complex noise scheduling and iterative denoising used in diffusion policies.

Conclusion: Projecting actions into pixel space for classification is an effective alternative to diffusion-based policies, offering a simpler, faster, and more precise paradigm for closed-loop manipulation learning.

Abstract: The action space poses a major challenge in robot learning, since it is often high-dimensional, can span long time horizons, and frequently admits multi-modal optimal solutions. A good choice of action representation and loss function can help to address these concerns, but there are often trade offs. We propose Action Map Policy (AMP), which casts 3D closed-loop manipulation policy learning as a classification problem in image space. While classification has been an effective formulation in generative language models, applying it to robot action learning is difficult because naively discretizing high-dimensional continuous actions explodes the token vocabulary. Our key idea is to project 3D actions onto the camera image planes and treat each pixel location as a discrete class, thus controlling dimensionality while retaining multi-modality. This method supports millimeter-level precision for high-dimensional actions without requiring a prohibitively large vocabulary, while preserving fine-grained pixel-wise visual signals. Furthermore, it can predict the entire action chunk in a single forward pass, avoiding complex noise scheduling and iterative denoising while achieving substantially faster inference than diffusion policies. Experiments on various manipulation tasks show that AMP outperforms strong baselines, achieving higher success rates, faster inference, and enhanced spatial reasoning.

</details>


### [166] [Learning Roller-Skating Motions of Humanoid Robots Based on Adversarial Motion Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10815)
*Yunkang Cheng,Yutong Wu,Menghan Li,Shihe Zhou,Mingguo Zhao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents an adversarial motion prior (AMP) based reinforcement learning framework for two humanoid roller-skating gaits (Pump Glide and Push Glide), using motion capture data retargeted to a humanoid robot, trained via separate AMP pipelines with independent datasets, policies, and rewards, and evaluated on gait quality, velocity tracking, turning, and reward ablations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humanoid roller-skating is challenging due to the need for whole-body balance, rolling contacts, and velocity-dependent posture regulation, which existing methods struggle to handle.

Method: The authors propose an AMP-based RL framework. Two skating gait datasets (Pump Glide and Push Glide) are collected via motion capture, retargeted to a humanoid robot, then smoothed and resampled into reference motion states. Each gait is learned independently via separate AMP pipelines with distinct reference datasets, policies, and reward architectures.

Result: Simulation experiments evaluate gait quality, velocity tracking, turning performance, and gait-specific reward ablations for both skating gaits.

Conclusion: The AMP-based framework enables learning of two distinct humanoid roller-skating gaits with independent training pipelines, demonstrating effective gait synthesis and control in simulation.

Abstract: Humanoid roller-skating is difficult because the robot must coordinate whole-body balance, rolling contacts, and velocity-dependent posture regulation. This paper presents an adversarial motion prior based reinforcement learning framework for two humanoid roller-skating gaits: Pump Glide skating and Push Glide skating. The two gait datasets are collected independently through motion capture and retargeted to the humanoid robot separately. The retargeted data are then smoothed and resampled into reference motion states for AMP training. The two gaits are learned by independent AMP training pipelines with separate reference datasets, separate policies, and independent reward architectures. Simulation experiments are designed to evaluate gait quality, velocity tracking, turning, and gait-specific reward ablations.

</details>


### [167] [D-SafeMPC: Diffusion-Driven Safe Model Predictive Control with Discrete-Time Control Barrier Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10842)
*Erdi Sayar,Ersin Daş,Joel W. Burdick,Alois Knoll,Erdal Kayacan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: D-SafeMPC integrates control barrier functions and control Lyapunov functions into the diffusion model's reverse denoising process with iterative MPC refinement, enabling safe, dynamically feasible robotic planning that outperforms baselines in simulation and real-world experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion models used in robotic planning cannot inherently enforce safety or dynamical constraints, leading to physically infeasible or unsafe outputs. Hybrid approaches using MPC for correction are unstable because poor trajectory initializations from diffusion prevent MPC convergence.

Method: D-SafeMPC guides the reverse diffusion process with CBFs and CLFs, and employs an iterative-projection scheme where MPC refines the trajectory at each denoising step, steering sampling toward safe, goal-directed regions and providing reliable MPC warm starts.

Result: In simulations on a Franka manipulator across four scenarios (one static-obstacle, three dynamic-obstacle) and a sim-to-real experiment, D-SafeMPC improves safety, task success rates, and planning efficiency over state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: D-SafeMPC effectively addresses the safety and feasibility limitations of diffusion models for robotic planning by tightly coupling diffusion sampling with control-theoretic guarantees, achieving superior performance in both simulated and real-world settings.

Abstract: A key limitation on the use of diffusion models in robotic planning is their inability to inherently enforce safety or dynamical constraints, which often results in physically infeasible or unsafe outputs. Hybrid approaches that employ model predictive control (MPC) to address this problem can be unstable, as poor trajectory initializations from the diffusion model prevent the MPC from converging to a safe and feasible solution. To overcome these challenges, we propose D-SafeMPC, which enhances the interaction between diffusion and control. Our method guides the reverse diffusion process with control barrier functions (CBFs) and control Lyapunov functions (CLFs) and employs an iterative-projection scheme where an MPC refines the trajectory at each denoising step. This steers sampling toward safe, goal-directed regions and provides reliable MPC warm starts. In simulations on a Franka manipulator across four scenarios (one static-obstacle and three dynamic-obstacle settings) and in a sim-to-real experiment on a physical Franka robot, D-SafeMPC improves safety, task success rates, and planning efficiency over state-of-the-art baselines. To facilitate reproducibility, our source code and experimental configurations are available in a repository at https://github.com/erdiphd/D-SafeMPC

</details>


### [168] [3D Scene Graph Prediction: Generating Hierarchical Models from Partially Observed Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10879)
*Siyi H,Jared Strade,Hyungtae Lim,Luca Carlone*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a top-down framework for generating hierarchical 3D scene graphs (room-level floor plans and object layouts) using a mixed-domain graph diffusion model for rooms and an existing diffusion model for objects, enabling prediction of unexplored areas for robotics applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots exploring partially known environments need to predict unexplored regions for downstream tasks like exploration and object search. Existing methods focus on single-room object layout generation and do not address high-level scene structure (room layout, traversability) which is crucial for robotics.

Method: A top-down framework with two layers: (1) Room layer: a novel mixed-domain graph diffusion model that jointly predicts room categories, floor boundaries, and traversability, supporting partial constraints via corruption and masking. (2) Object layer: an existing mixed discrete-continuous diffusion model predicting object categories, locations, sizes, and orientations given floor plans.

Result: The method outperforms occupancy-based and LLM-based floor plan generation baselines on a standard benchmark, generalizes substantially better to out-of-distribution partial floor plans, and demonstrates integrated prediction on real-world robot-collected data.

Conclusion: The proposed hierarchical scene graph generation approach effectively predicts unexplored areas from partial observations, with strong generalization to out-of-distribution inputs, supporting practical robotics applications.

Abstract: Generating realistic 3D indoor scenes is an area of growing interest in computer vision and robotics. Existing methods, often motivated by applications such as interior design, generally focus on object layout generation within a single room. The generation of high-level scene structure, such as room-level layout and traversability, remains underexplored despite its importance for robotics applications. In this paper, we consider the case where a robot has explored part of an environment and needs to predict the unexplored parts to support downstream tasks such as exploration or object search. We propose a top-down framework for synthesizing hierarchical 3D scene graphs, including a room layer -- describing the floor plan and traversability -- and an object layer modeling object layouts within each room. For the room layer, we propose a novel mixed-domain graph diffusion model jointly predicting room categories, floor boundaries, and traversability between rooms. Via corruption and masking, this model supports partial constraints such as incomplete floor plans, avoiding the need for partially observed training data. For the object layer, we integrate an existing mixed discrete-continuous diffusion model for joint prediction of object categories, locations, sizes, and orientations within each room given the floor plan. We compare our method with state-of-the-art occupancy-based and LLM-based floor plan generation methods on a standard benchmark. Compared with an occupancy-based learning baseline, our method generalizes substantially better to out-of-distribution partial floor plans. We also demonstrate our integrated prediction pipeline on real-world scenes from robot-collected data, enabling prediction beyond explored areas.

</details>


### [169] [A Single Diffusion-Policy Controller for Multi-Task Block Pushing with Zero-Shot Sim-to-Real Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10892)
*Haitong Ma,Haldun Balim,Yang Hu,Bo Dai,Na Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper trains diffusion policies from scratch using reinforcement learning (RL) for multi-task robotic block-pushing, employing a reweighted evidence lower bound as the policy loss, reverse curriculum generation, and objective-centric representations to enable learning without demonstrations, with zero-shot transfer to real-world tasks under varying conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion policies for robot learning rely on behavior cloning (BC) from demonstrations, which limits their scalability due to the need for large demonstration datasets. The authors aim to train diffusion policies from scratch using RL to eliminate the dependency on demonstrations while leveraging the expressiveness of diffusion models for complex multi-task manipulation.

Method: The framework uses a reweighted evidence lower bound (ELBO) as the policy loss function, adapted from BC-based diffusion policy training, which integrates seamlessly as the policy learning module in RL algorithms. To address exploration without demonstrations, they incorporate reverse curriculum generation and objective-centric representations. A single diffusion policy is trained for multi-task block-pushing tasks with varying shapes in a sparse-reward simulation setting.

Result: The proposed approach successfully learns multi-task block-pushing policies in a sparse-reward simulation environment. The trained diffusion policy transfers zero-shot to real-world tasks under varying environmental conditions including goal positions, block shapes, block weights, and surface friction, demonstrating robustness across tested variations.

Conclusion: Training diffusion policies from scratch using RL is feasible for multi-task robotic manipulation, overcoming the demonstration bottleneck. The combination of diffusion policy expressiveness with RL and targeted exploration strategies enables effective policy learning and zero-shot sim-to-real transfer, suggesting broader applicability for complex robotic tasks.

Abstract: Diffusion policies have shown promising empirical performance in representing and learning complex maneuvers for robots using behavior cloning (BC). In this paper, we explore training diffusion policies from scratch using reinforcement learning (RL) for multi-task robotic manipulation. Specifically, we aim to train a single diffusion policy for block-pushing tasks with multiple shapes. The proposed framework features a simple policy loss function, which is a reweighted evidence lower bound used in BC-based diffusion policy training and can seamlessly serve as the policy learning module in RL algorithms. To address the exploration challenges arising from the absence of demonstrations, we incorporate reverse curriculum generation and objective-centric representations. Combined with the expressiveness of diffusion policies, our design supports learning of multi-task block-pushing policies in our sparse-reward simulation setting. We further evaluate whether the trained diffusion policy transfers in zero-shot to real-world tasks under varying environmental conditions including goal positions, block shapes, block weights and surface friction, providing evidence that this pipeline can transfer to our real-world block-pushing setup under the tested variations.

</details>


### [170] [Mapping Pamir: Multi-Session Visual-Inertial SLAM and 3D Reconstruction of an Underwater Shipwreck](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10925)
*Michalis Chatzispyrou,Luke Horgan,Hyunkil Hwang,Harish Sathishchandra,Chinmay Burgul,Monika Roznere,Alberto Quattrini Li,Philippos Mordohai,Ioannis Rekleitis*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a multi-session underwater mapping framework using an affordable action camera, dive computer depth data, SVIn2 for sparse reconstruction, and COLMAP for dense reconstruction. It demonstrates mapping both exterior and interior parts of a shipwreck off Barbados across multiple sessions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable affordable, multi-session 3D mapping of underwater environments (specifically shipwrecks) using low-cost consumer hardware, addressing the challenge of aligning data collected across different dives with different trajectories and viewpoints.

Method: 1) Collect Visual-Inertial data with an action camera and depth data from a dive computer. 2) Use SVIn2 (VI-SLAM) for trajectory and sparse reconstruction per session. 3) Extract keyframes and use COLMAP (SfM) for global optimization and dense reconstruction. 4) Use calibration targets at fixed locations to estimate coordinate transformations between sessions. 5) Apply pipeline to map a shipwreck in three sessions (exterior, interior, and dual-camera setup).

Result: Successfully mapped both exterior and accessible interior parts of a shipwreck off Barbados across two sessions, with a third session using two cameras of different fields of view.

Conclusion: The proposed low-cost, multi-session mapping pipeline is effective for underwater archaeological documentation, enabling comprehensive reconstruction of complex structures like shipwrecks by combining data from multiple dives.

Abstract: This paper presents a framework for multi-session mapping of underwater environments utilizing an affordable action camera. The Visual-Inertial data are augmented by water depth recordings from a dive computer. SVIn2, an open-source VI-SLAM framework, is utilized to generate a trajectory and a sparse reconstruction for each session. Utilizing the keyframes extracted from SVIn2 and the estimated camera poses, a Structure-from-Motion (SfM) framework, COLMAP, is employed for global optimization and to produce a dense reconstruction of the target environment. The presence of calibration targets at fixed locations, when available, is used to estimate the coordinate transformation between different data collection sessions, thus transforming the different sessions into the same coordinate frame. The proposed pipeline is employed for the mapping of a shipwreck off the coast of Barbados. For the first time, both the exterior and the accessible interior parts of the wreck were mapped in two sessions, while a third session employed two cameras with different fields of view.

</details>


### [171] [Real-Time Rulebook-Aware Nonlinear MPC for Autonomous Driving with Priority-Biased Tiered Slacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10975)
*Hadi Hajieghrary,Benedikt Walter,Chaitanya Shinde,Paul Schmitt,Miguel Hurtado*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: W-SQP is a weighted tiered-slack nonlinear model predictive controller for autonomous vehicles that compiles nine driving-rule families into a four-tier shared-slack nonlinear program, enabling priority-biased rule compliance with anytime capability at 10 Hz replanning, evaluated on 150 Waymo scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous-vehicle motion planners must resolve conflicts among safety, regulation, comfort, and efficiency in real time while exposing those decisions for audit.

Method: Proposes W-SQP, a weighted tiered-slack NMPC that compiles nine driving-rule families into a four-tier shared-slack nonlinear program solved with CasADi and IPOPT. Uses strongly separated tier penalties to bias residual violations toward lower-priority rules while keeping actuation bounds hard. Replans at 10 Hz with a 90 ms solver-time limit, returning anytime iterates projected through vehicle dynamics.

Result: Median and maximum observed wall-clock solve times were 28 ms and 104 ms. Evaluated on 150 Waymo Open Motion Dataset scenarios in Waymax against reactive and proposal-and-select baselines. Under a log-independent protocol, W-SQP shows no systematic group-level deficit relative to expert replay on safety and regulatory rules, with localized regressions in the hardest scenarios.

Conclusion: W-SQP is characterized as an auditable, priority-biased, anytime-capable NMPC prototype rather than a hard-real-time or formally safe controller.

Abstract: Autonomous-vehicle motion planners must resolve conflicts among safety, regulation, comfort, and efficiency in real time while exposing those decisions for audit. We present W-SQP, a weighted tiered-slack nonlinear model predictive controller (NMPC) that compiles nine driving-rule families into a four-tier shared-slack nonlinear program solved online with CasADi and IPOPT; the name denotes the weighted quadratic slack penalty, not a sequential-quadratic-programming solver. Strongly separated tier penalties bias residual violations toward lower-priority rules while leaving actuation bounds hard. The controller replans from its executed state at $10$\,Hz and records per-rule residuals on every cycle. A $90$\,ms solver-time limit returns an anytime iterate that is projected through the vehicle dynamics before execution; median and maximum observed wall-clock solve times were $28$ and $104$\,ms. We evaluate W-SQP in closed loop on 150 Waymo Open Motion Dataset scenarios in Waymax against reactive and proposal-and-select baselines, and introduce a log-independent protocol that separates safety and regulatory compliance from resemblance to the recorded human trajectory. Under this protocol, W-SQP shows no systematic group-level deficit relative to expert replay on the log-independent safety and regulatory rules, with several localized regressions in the hardest, highest-divergence scenarios. The results characterize W-SQP as an auditable, priority-biased, anytime-capable NMPC prototype rather than a hard-real-time or formally safe controller.

</details>


### [172] [Think When It Matters: Conditional VLM Reasoning for Social Navigation with RL Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10991)
*Ali Ahmadi,Hamed Rahimi,Adrien Jacquet Cretides,Marie Samson,Mahdi Khoramshahi,Mohamed Chetouani*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HUMA is a hybrid architecture combining RL policies (for efficient routine navigation) with VLMs (for semantic reasoning in sensitive social situations), achieving improved task success and reduced human collisions on Social-MP3D and Social-HM3D benchmarks while demonstrating real-world viability on a Mirokaï robot.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mobile robots need social navigation that ensures human comfort and safety, but RL policies lack semantic reasoning and VLMs suffer from high computational cost and slow inference, preventing real-time deployment.

Method: HUMA uses a reactive RL policy for routine low-density navigation tasks and conditions it on a post-trained high-level VLM only when humans enter the robot's proximity zone, dynamically balancing efficiency and semantic understanding.

Result: HUMA achieves 20% and 3% task success improvements on Social-MP3D and Social-HM3D respectively, significantly reduces personal space violations and human collisions, and validates components via ablations and real-world deployment on Mirokaï.

Conclusion: Hybrid architectures that dynamically allocate RL and VLM-based reasoning based on social context can overcome the efficiency-semantics trade-off, making socially-aware navigation practically viable for real-time deployment.

Abstract: As mobile robots become more integrated into everyday human environments, social robot navigation is becoming essential for ensuring human comfort, safety, and trust. While reinforcement learning (RL) navigation policies provide the fast inference and reactive behavior necessary for real-time deployment, they still lack flexible semantic reasoning capabilities and often fail to generalize to complex social scenarios. Recent approaches have increasingly turned to vision-language models (VLMs) in place of RL policies to improve semantic and social reasoning in robot navigation. Nevertheless, their high computational cost and slow inference remain major barriers to real-time deployment. To overcome these limitations, we introduce HUMA (Hybrid Understanding for Multi-modal social Navigation), a hybrid architecture that dynamically balances the computational efficiency of RL policies with the deep semantic understanding of VLMs. Our approach uses a reactive RL policy to handle low-density, routine navigation tasks, while conditioning it on a post-trained high-level VLM when a human enters sensitive situations, such as the robot's proximity zone. We evaluate HUMA on the Social-MP3D and Social-HM3D benchmarks, where it achieves task success improvements of 20% and 3%, respectively, while significantly reducing personal space violations and human collisions against state-of-the-art baselines. Extensive ablation studies validate each architectural component, and real-world deployment on the Mirokaï mobile robot further demonstrates the practical viability of our approach.

</details>


### [173] [Wearing A Coat: Dual-Arm Robot-Assisted Dressing with Differentiable Clothing Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10999)
*Yiming Liu,Lijun Han,Hesheng Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a robotic dressing control algorithm that integrates real-time differentiable clothing simulation with higher-order perturbations for stability, a multi-phase control strategy, and a constrained local model for high-frequency compensation, validated in simulated and physical dressing scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing robot-assisted dressing algorithms treat clothing as discrete segments and struggle with managing partially worn garments under contact constraints due to intricate garment-limb interactions.

Method: (1) Real-time differentiable clothing simulation using explicit iterative scheme with higher-order perturbations for stability under large time-steps; (2) Multi-phase control strategy leveraging simulation-resolved garment states under contact constraints; (3) Constrained local model with optimization solver for high-frequency local compensation to the differentiable simulation-based global controller.

Result: The approach was experimentally validated through both simulated and physical dressing scenarios, demonstrating feasibility and efficacy.

Conclusion: Integrating differentiable simulation with a hierarchical control framework effectively addresses the challenge of partial garment manipulation under contact constraints in robotic dressing.

Abstract: The development of assistive robots for dressing tasks serves to augment human convenience and improve the quality of life for individuals with physical impairments. However, due to the intricate contact interactions between garments and the human limbs during dressing, most robot-assisted dressing algorithms treat clothing as an assembly of discrete segments, thereby struggling to manage the partial worn garments under contact constraints. To overcome this challenge, we propose a novel robotic dressing control algorithm that integrates realtime differentiable clothing simulation. The simulation algorithm employs explicit iterative scheme with intentionally introduced higher-order perturbations to enhance computational efficiency while maintaining stability under large time-step conditions. Through simulation, we resolve the garment state under contact constraints, which then enables a multi-phase control strategy for successful coat dressing assistance. To further improve real-time performance, we introduce a constrained local model along with its corresponding optimization solver, permitting high-frequency local compensation for the differentiable simulation based global controller. Finally, we experimentally validate our approach through both simulated and physical dressing scenarios, conclusively demonstrating its feasibility and efficacy

</details>


### [174] [Affordance-Based Manipulation Planning with Text Goals and Sim-to-Real Generalisation via Real-to-Sim Image Conversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11004)
*Solvi Arnold,Rin Karashima,Tadashi Adachi,Takafumi Mochizuki,Kimitoshi Yamazaki*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A manipulation planning system that uses affordance recognition and action effect prediction, reasoning through visual futures and matching predicted outcomes with text-based goals, including occlusion handling and image conversion for real-world robot deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable robots to generate manipulation plans by reasoning about possible future visual states, match predicted outcomes to text-based goals, handle occluded objects during planning, and bridge the gap between simulation and real-world visual appearances.

Method: The system consists of: (1) affordance recognition and action effect prediction for reasoning through visual futures, (2) a multi-modal goal-matching module to evaluate candidate plans against run-time text goals, (3) occlusion-aware tracking of goal-named objects through predictions, and (4) an image conversion module that translates real-world state images into a consistent visual appearance for planning.

Result: Modules evaluated in isolation; integrated system demonstrated manipulation planning capabilities on challenging tasks both in simulation and on physical hardware.

Conclusion: The proposed system can generate action plans even under occlusion and across varied visual appearances, enabling effective manipulation planning in both simulated and real-robot settings.

Abstract: We present a manipulation planning system based on affordance recognition and action effect prediction. The system reasons through possible futures in visual form, and evaluates candidate plans by agreement of predicted outcomes with text-based goals set at run-time, using a multi-modal goal-matching module. Positions of objects named in the goal text are tracked through predictions even when occluded, making it possible to generate action plans even when objects become occluded, or when their initial descriptors cease to identify them in future states. We further expand the system with an image conversion module for translating real-world state images with objects of varied shapes and visual appearances into a consistent visual appearance, to facilitate manipulation planning in a physical robot setup. We evaluate performance of the system's modules in isolation and demonstrate the integrated system's manipulation planning capabilities on a set of challenging tasks in both simulation and on hardware.

</details>


### [175] [Whole-Body Semantic-to-Actuation Grounding of Elephant-Inspired Soft-Trunk Motion via Lightweight Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11018)
*Tingcong Liu,Tongshun Chen,Siyi Ma,Yuhao Wang,Aye Phyu Phyu Aung,Ibrahim Alsarraj,J. Senthilnath,Bo An,Ke Wu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A whole-body semantic-to-actuation grounding framework for elephant-inspired soft-trunk HRI using lightweight flow matching converts multimodal LLM responses into feasible trunk motions, improving grounding correctness from 25.0% to 77.2% over dense regression and from 71.9% to 77.2% over diffusion baselines, while reducing inference time to 4.87 ms; a 100-participant study shows adding the motion channel boosts satisfaction from 46% to 82% over audiovisual-only.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Trunk-like continuum manipulators enable diverse whole-body expression in close-contact HRI, but existing methods struggle because end-effector motion underspecifies body shape and direct whole-body commands are high-dimensional and hard to keep feasible. The authors aim to ground open-vocabulary responses into feasible whole-body trunk motions.

Method: The framework consists of: (1) converting multimodal LLM responses into bounded morphology-aligned intent-intensity tuples; (2) parameterizing tendon-actuation trajectories with compact Catmull-Rom spline controls; (3) using a rectified-flow generator to sample feasible whole-body trunk motions.
