Result: Held-out grounding correctness improved from 25.0% to 77.2% over raw-response dense-regression baseline, and from 71.9% to 77.2% over denoising-diffusion baseline; inference time reduced from 7.86 ms to 4.87 ms while preserving motion diversity. In a 100-participant physical HRI study, adding the soft-trunk motion channel increased positive overall-satisfaction from 46% to 82% over audiovisual-only baseline.

Conclusion: The proposed whole-body semantic-to-actuation grounding framework effectively enables expressive, feasible trunk motions for close-contact HRI, significantly outperforming baselines in correctness and efficiency, and substantially improving user satisfaction in physical interaction.

Abstract: For close-contact human-robot interaction (HRI), trunk-like continuum manipulators provide a physical channel for diverse whole-body expression, but grounding open-vocabulary responses into such robots is difficult: end-effector motion underspecifies body shape, whereas direct whole-body commands are high-dimensional and hard to keep feasible. We propose a whole-body semantic-to-actuation grounding framework for elephant-inspired soft-trunk HRI based on lightweight flow matching. The framework converts responses from a multimodal large language model into bounded, morphology-aligned intent-intensity tuples, parameterizes tendon-actuation trajectories with compact Catmull-Rom spline controls, and uses a rectified-flow generator to sample feasible whole-body trunk motions. Experiments show that the proposed framework improves held-out grounding correctness from 25.0% to 77.2% over a raw-response dense-regression baseline. Compared with a denoising-diffusion baseline, it improves correctness from 71.9% to 77.2% and reduces inference time from 7.86 ms to 4.87 ms while preserving motion diversity. A 100-participant physical HRI study further shows that adding the generated soft-trunk motion channel increases the positive overall-satisfaction rating from 46% to 82% over the audiovisual-only baseline.

</details>


### [176] [SegDiff: Segmented Trajectory Diffusion for Consistent and Adaptive Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11027)
*Haidong Cao,Wenjun Cao,Quanhao Li,Sicheng Xie,Zhiying Du,Jiaqi Leng,Zuxuan Wu,Yu-Gang Jiang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SegDiff is a closed-loop visuomotor policy that combines keypose-based and continuous trajectory prediction paradigms using diffusion models and DDIM inversion, with a Dynamic Temporal Ensembling mechanism for long-horizon, real-time robotic manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing imitation learning methods either predict continuous action sequences (suffer from compounding errors and struggle with multi-modal distributions) or discrete keyposes (require external planner, limiting real-time use). SegDiff aims to combine both paradigms' strengths while overcoming their limitations.

Method: SegDiff decomposes demonstrations into motion segments between keyposes, learns to predict continuous trajectories from current state to next keypose, and employs Dynamic Temporal Ensembling via diffusion models and DDIM inversion for efficient response to dynamic environments and mitigation of discontinuities from multi-modal sampling.

Result: Significant performance gains over existing approaches across various simulated and real-world scenarios, demonstrating strong long-horizon temporal reasoning while maintaining real-time adaptability and control stability.

Conclusion: SegDiff effectively integrates keypose-based and continuous prediction paradigms for closed-loop visuomotor control, achieving both long-horizon prediction capability and real-time refinement in robotic manipulation tasks.

Abstract: Imitation learning enables robots to acquire manipulation skills from demonstrations by mapping observations to actions. Existing approaches predict either short-horizon continuous action sequences or discrete keyposes. However, continuous prediction methods suffer from compounding errors due to short prediction horizons and struggle with multi-modal action distributions, whereas keypose-based methods necessitate an external planner, constraining real-time applicability. To address these challenges, we introduce SegDiff, a closed-loop visuomotor policy that integrates the strengths of both paradigms. SegDiff decomposes demonstrations into motion segments between keyposes and learns to predict the continuous trajectory from the current state to the next keypose, enabling long-horizon prediction with real-time refinement. Furthermore, we leverage the capability of diffusion models and DDIM inversion to propose a Dynamic Temporal Ensembling mechanism, which allows the policy to efficiently respond to dynamic environments and mitigate discontinuities caused by inconsistent multi-modal sampling. SegDiff demonstrates significant performance gains over existing approaches across various simulated and real-world scenarios, indicating its strong ability to reason over extended temporal dependencies while maintaining real-time adaptability and control stability.

</details>


### [177] [Learning to Navigate Efficiently with Only 0.58M Trainable Parameters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11029)
*Edward Beng Wai Tan,Siew-Kei Lam*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: By decomposing visual navigation into analytical geometric operations and three small learned modules, the model achieves near-SOTA performance with only 0.58M trainable parameters (233x fewer than typical), trained on 44k frames in under one GPU-hour, with lowest collision rates and transparent failure modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate how much scale (parameters, data, compute) a single visual navigation task family actually requires, and what structural priors can substitute for large-scale end-to-end training.

Method: A decomposed navigation model where closed-form analytical operations (projective geometry, occupancy, coordinate transforms) serve as interfaces between three small learned modules: (1) an egress predictor for grounding episode goals as local subgoals, (2) a navigation predictor for goal-conditioned posterior over trajectory distributions, and (3) an endpoint-pinned residual diffusion generator for sampling trajectory shapes from the posterior.

Result: 0.58M trainable parameters (out of 22.7M total) trained on 44k frames in under 1 GPU-hour; approaches SOTA on 6060 point-goal episodes across 60 environments; 233x fewer trainable parameters; lowest collision rate; 50 Hz inference; transfers to no-goal exploration by retraining only the 123k-parameter egress head.

Conclusion: Decomposed navigation models with analytical geometric operations can approach state-of-the-art performance using 233x fewer trainable parameters (0.58M), 44k frames of training data, and under one GPU-hour, while achieving the lowest collision rates, 50 Hz inference, and transparent failure modes that are analytically correctable under sensor corruption.

Abstract: Recent progress in visual navigation has largely been driven by scale: end-to-end policies with hundreds of millions of parameters trained on billions of frames or large-scale simulated data. We ask how much of this scale a single task family actually requires, and what structure can substitute for it. We propose a decomposed navigation model in which operations with known closed-form structure, such as projective geometry, occupancy, and coordinate transforms, are computed analytically and serve as interfaces between three small learned modules: an egress predictor that grounds the episode goal as a local subgoal in the current view, a navigation predictor that estimates a goal-conditioned posterior over where trajectories travel, and an endpoint-pinned residual diffusion generator that samples trajectory shapes from this posterior. The system trains only 0.58M out of a total of 22.7M parameters, on 44k frames in under one GPU-hour, yet approaches the performance of state-of-the-art models on navigation tasks across 6060 point-goal episodes and 60 environments, while having 233x fewer trainable parameters, the lowest collision rate among all evaluated methods, and 50 Hz inference speed. The decomposition further transfers to no-goal exploration by retraining only the 123k-parameter egress head, and its failure modes under sensor corruption are transparent and analytically correctable.

</details>


### [178] [GraspGraphNet: Graph-Structured Multi-Embodiment Dexterous Grasp Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11031)
*Yeonseo Lee,Taeyeop Lee,Hyosup Shin,Guebin Hwang,Sungho Jo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GraspGraphNet is a topology-aware framework that represents robot hands as URDF-derived kinematic graphs and directly generates executable grasps (palm poses + joint configurations) across different hand topologies using conditional flow matching, achieving 83.48% success rate at 40ms per grasp on a 40-object benchmark across three distinct hands without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dexterous grasp generation across robot hands is challenging because hands differ in kinematic topology, actuation dimensions, and native command spaces, making cross-hand generalization difficult.

Method: GraspGraphNet represents each hand as a URDF-derived kinematic graph, combines hierarchical object surface encoding, differentiable forward kinematics, and dynamic world-edge message passing to model robot-object interactions, and applies conditional flow matching directly in executable palm-pose and joint-state space without post-processing optimization, inverse kinematics, or retargeting.

Result: Achieves 83.48% average success rate across Barrett Hand, Allegro Hand, and Shadow Hand with 40ms inference per grasp on a 40-object benchmark; without retraining achieves 72.70% success on controlled finger-removal variants, demonstrating robustness to hand-topology variations.

Conclusion: Graph-structured hand representations can effectively support dexterous grasp generation across robot hands with different kinematic structures, enabling cross-hand generalization without per-hand retraining or post-processing.

Abstract: Dexterous grasp generation across robot hands is challenging because hands differ in kinematic topology, actuation dimensions, and native command spaces. We introduce GraspGraphNet, a topology-aware grasp generation framework that represents each hand as a URDF-derived kinematic graph and directly generates executable palm poses and joint configurations. GraspGraphNet combines hierarchical object surface encoding, differentiable forward kinematics, and dynamic world-edge message passing to model evolving robot-object interactions. It applies conditional flow matching directly in executable palm-pose and joint-state space, avoiding post-processing optimization, inverse kinematics, and retargeting. Using a shared model trained on Barrett Hand, Allegro Hand, and Shadow Hand, GraspGraphNet achieves an average success rate of 83.48% with 40ms inference time per grasp on a 40-object benchmark. Without retraining, the same model achieves 72.70% success on controlled finger-removal variants, demonstrating robustness to hand-topology variations. These results suggest that graph-structured hand representations can effectively support dexterous grasp generation across robot hands with different kinematic structures. Project: https://lysees.github.io/graspgraphnet-page

</details>


### [179] [PAKE: Learning Whole-Body Loco-Manipulation with Partial Kinematic Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11041)
*Zhengmao He,Moonkyu Jung,Hyeongjun Kim,Jiseong Lee,Hui Zhang,Jemin Hwangbo,Jie Song*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a whole-body control framework for loco-manipulation that uses a Kinematic Normalizing Flow (KNF) model to generate partial reference motions, with a high-level controller navigating the KNF's latent space for redundancy exploitation and a low-level controller for accurate execution. The approach achieves state-of-the-art performance on a quadrupedal robot with a 6-DoF arm, with end-effector pose errors of 4.5 cm / 0.14 rad and locomotion tracking errors of 0.1 m/s / 0.01 rad/s across 24 episodes of 8 tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Loco-manipulation faces challenges in high-precision control, managing high-dimensional action spaces from many DoFs, and fully exploiting whole-body system redundancy.

Method: The problem is decomposed into partial reference motion generation and low-level imitation control. A Kinematic Normalizing Flow (KNF) model is trained on large-scale kinematic data to generate diverse feasible partial reference motions. A high-level controller navigates the KNF's latent space to exploit redundant solutions, while a low-level controller ensures physically feasible and accurate motion execution.

Result: In simulation, the approach outperforms SOTA methods in tracking accuracy and feasible workspace coverage. In hardware deployment (24 episodes, 8 mobile loco-manipulation tasks), it achieves end-effector pose errors of 4.5 cm and 0.14 rad, and locomotion tracking errors of 0.1 m/s and 0.01 rad/s, outperforming competitive baselines.

Conclusion: The method provides a practical and powerful solution for accurate and generalized whole-body loco-manipulation in high-DoF robotic systems, with potential for diverse downstream tasks.

Abstract: Loco-manipulation has recently shown promising capabilities; however, achieving high-precision control, managing the high-dimensional action space induced by many degrees of freedom (DoFs), and fully exploiting the inherent redundancy of whole-body systems remain challenging. In this paper, we propose a novel whole-body control framework that effectively addresses these challenges by decomposing the complex loco-manipulation problem into partial reference motion generation and low-level imitation control. We introduce a new Kinematic Normalizing Flow (KNF) model, trained on a large-scale kinematic dataset, that generates diverse yet feasible partial reference motions. A high-level controller is then trained to navigate the KNF's latent space to exploit redundant solutions, while a low-level controller ensures physically feasible and accurate motion execution. We validate our approach on the quadrupedal robot equipped with a six-DoF robotic arm. In simulation, experimental results show that our approach significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods in terms of tracking accuracy and feasible workspace coverage. For hardware deployment, we evaluate the system over 24 episodes across 8 different mobile loco-manipulation tasks. The system achieves end-effector pose-tracking errors of 4.5 cm and 0.14 rad, while maintaining accurate locomotion tracking with linear and angular velocity errors of 0.1 m/s and 0.01 rad/s, respectively, outperforming competitive baselines. Our method represents a practical and powerful solution for accurate and generalized whole-body loco-manipulation in high-DoF robotic systems, with promising potential for diverse downstream robotic tasks.

</details>


### [180] [Desc++: Efficient Descriptor Enhancement for Data Association in Existing Visual SLAM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11099)
*Ting-Wei Ou,Huang-Ting Lin,Kuu-Young Young*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Desc++ is a lightweight descriptor enhancement module for V-SLAM that jointly encodes descriptor representations and keypoint geometry using a hybrid architecture (global attention + geometry-aware sequential modeling) to improve matching accuracy and trajectory estimation without modifying existing pipelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Handcrafted descriptors in V-SLAM degrade under illumination and viewpoint changes; learning-based front-ends improve accuracy but introduce computational overhead; existing descriptor enhancement methods rely on simplified attention mechanisms with limited contextual modeling, constraining matching quality.

Method: Desc++ uses a hybrid architecture combining order-agnostic global attention with geometry-aware sequential modeling in linear time to jointly encode descriptor representations and keypoint geometry, enhancing existing descriptors while preserving their original dimensionality and matching interface.

Result: Desc++ improves matching accuracy over state-of-the-art enhancement methods, translates gains into more accurate and stable trajectory estimation across four V-SLAM systems, and achieves a favorable accuracy-efficiency balance for real-time integration.

Conclusion: Desc++ resolves the trade-off between contextual expressiveness and efficiency for descriptor enhancement in V-SLAM, enabling practical integration into deployed real-time systems without pipeline modification.

Abstract: Reliable visual data association is fundamental to visual SLAM (V-SLAM), as it directly determines the quality of the camera pose estimation and map consistency. However, the handcrafted descriptors used by most mature real-time systems degrade under illumination and viewpoint changes, while learning-based front-ends that address this weakness typically require replacing the extraction-and-matching pipeline and introduce substantial computational overhead. Descriptor enhancement offers a compromise by refining existing descriptors within their original format, yet current methods rely on simplified attention mechanisms whose limited contextual modeling constrains the achievable matching quality. To resolve this trade-off between contextual expressiveness and efficiency, we propose Desc++, a lightweight enhancement module that jointly encodes descriptor representations and keypoint geometry and aggregates spatial context through a hybrid architecture that combines order-agnostic global attention with geometry-aware sequential modeling in linear time. The enhanced descriptors retain their original dimensionality and matching interface, enabling integration into deployed V-SLAM systems without modifying the pipeline. Experiments across descriptor matching, correspondence analysis, and system-level benchmarks with four different V-SLAM systems demonstrate that Desc++ improves matching accuracy over the state-of-the-art enhancement method, translates these gains into more accurate and stable trajectory estimation, and achieves a favorable balance between accuracy and efficiency for practical integration into existing real-time V-SLAM pipelines.

</details>


### [181] [VIA: Visual Interface Agent for Robot Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11119)
*Hengyuan Hu,Priya Sundaresan,Jensen Gao,Dorsa Sadigh*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VIA (Visual Interface Agent) is a framework that controls robots using off-the-shelf foundation model agents through a browser-based 3D interface, without any robot-specific fine-tuning. It achieves strong zero-shot performance on tabletop manipulation tasks, with 96.7% success on LIBERO-Goal and 100% on long-horizon assembly using the strongest model (Fable 5).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current VLA models are limited because they require fine-tuning on robot data, resulting in much smaller models than frontier FMs, which constrains their general capability. The authors explore whether frontier FMs' existing ability to operate software through visual interfaces can directly transfer to robot control.

Method: VIA recasts robot control as an agentic task: an off-the-shelf FM-powered agent drives a manipulator through a browser-based 3D interface, taking screenshots, issuing intuitive commands, observing outcomes, and adjusting. The agent receives no robot-specific fine-tuning and no privileged state information.

Result: VIA solves diverse tabletop manipulation tasks zero-shot with Claude Code and Codex. With Fable 5, it achieves 96.7% success on three LIBERO-Goal tasks and 100% on a long-horizon rainbow assembly task. Performance scales with model strength.

Conclusion: Frontier agents already possess skills that transfer directly to robot control given the right interface—coding or computer-use agents are, in a sense, secretly robot-control agents.

Abstract: Robot manipulation is a complex task that requires visual understanding, physical reasoning, planning, and closed-loop control. General-purpose foundation models (FMs) have grown remarkably capable of some of these, especially vision and reasoning. To leverage this for generalist robot policies, current methods typically involve converting existing FMs into vision-language-action (VLA) models by fine-tuning on robot data to output low-level actions. However, VLAs are often orders of magnitude smaller than frontier FMs given the limited data and compute available for fine-tuning, which in turn limits their general capability. Inspired by the growing ability of FMs to operate software through visual interfaces, we ask whether that same competence suffices to control a robot. We present VIA (Visual Interface Agent for robot control), a framework that recasts robot control as an agentic task: an off-the-shelf FM-powered agent drives a manipulator through a browser-based 3D interface by taking screenshots, issuing intuitive commands, observing the outcome, and adjusting. The agent receives no robot-specific fine-tuning and no access to privileged state information: it perceives visual input and acts through a small set of general tools. VIA inherits the agent's general reasoning, closed-loop error recovery, and ability to plan and re-plan from what it observes. It solves a diverse suite of tabletop manipulation tasks zero-shot with both Claude Code and Codex. With the strongest model (Fable 5) it achieves 96.7% success on three LIBERO-Goal tasks and 100% on a long-horizon rainbow assembly task. Performance improves with the scale and strength of the underlying model. These results suggest that frontier agents already possess skills that transfer directly to robot control given the right interface: your coding or computer-use agent is, in a sense, secretly a robot-control agent.

</details>


### [182] [Comparison-Based Ordinal Learning for Proactive Driving Risk Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11128)
*Zhuoren Li,Yi Zhong,Weiqi Zhang,Xinrui Zhang,Lu Xiong,Chongfeng Wei,Bo Leng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes a comparison-based ordinal risk learning framework that learns collision-relevant risk scores from pairwise supervision, improving proactive collision warning precision and lead time over surrogate-based methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing driving risk assessment methods rely on surrogate objectives due to scarcity of collision data and frame-level risk labels, which may imperfectly align with true collision risk and fail to faithfully reflect relative danger of driving interactions.

Method: Ordinal risk learning framework using pairwise comparisons from three sources: temporal progression within safety-critical sequences, event-level contrast between dangerous and normal interactions, and physics-based counterfactual perturbations. Three risk-scoring function instantiations: direct learning from comparisons, aligning single surrogate model, and aligning multiple surrogate models.

Result: Evaluated on 100-Car and SHRP2 datasets demonstrated improved high-recall risk discrimination, warning precision, and warning lead time over surrogate-based baselines in both in-distribution and out-of-distribution settings.

Conclusion: The framework provides more reliable risk assessment for automated driving systems and safety-critical driving interactions, contributing to proactive safety research.

Abstract: Real-time driving risk assessment provides an essential basis for proactive safety by identifying and quantifying the danger of ongoing road interactions before adverse outcomes occur. However, due to the scarcity of collision data and frame-level risk labels, existing driving risk assessment methods often rely on surrogate objectives, which may imperfectly align with true collision risk and not faithfully reflect the relative danger of driving interaction. This paper proposes a comparison-based ordinal risk learning framework that learns collision-relevant risk scores from pairwise supervision in driving data, directly modeling relative risk ordering without requiring numerical frame-level risk labels. We derive pairwise comparisons from three sources of event-structured driving data for such ordinal risk learning: temporal progression within safety-critical sequences, event-level contrast between dangerous and normal interactions, and physics-based counterfactual perturbations. On this basis, instantiations with three risk-scoring function parameterizations are implemented, including directly learning risk scores from comparison data, and aligning existing single or multiple surrogate-based risk models. The proposed framework is evaluated on the 100-Car and SHRP2 naturalistic driving datasets using a proactive collision warning task. Results show that the proposed framework improves high-recall risk discrimination, warning precision, and warning lead time over representative surrogate-based baselines across both in-distribution and out-of-distribution evaluations. These results suggest that the proposed framework can contribute to proactive safety research by providing more reliable risk assessment for automated driving systems and safety-critical driving interactions.

</details>


### [183] [Pix2Act: Image-Space Manipulation Policies with Equivariant Augmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11167)
*Haojie Huang,Linfeng Zhao,Haotian Liu,Zhang Ye,Si-Yuan Huang,Mingxi Jia,Boce Hu,Fangzhou Lin,Yu Qi,Dian Wang,Robin Walters,Robert Platt*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Pix2Act reformulates 3D manipulation as 2D keypoint trajectory prediction in camera planes, using triangulation for pose recovery and equivariant data augmentation across multiple views, outperforming baselines on diverse tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Representing manipulation actions as 2D trajectories in the camera plane is compact and interpretable but suffers from out-of-frame trajectories and limited precision. The authors aim to address these challenges while leveraging the benefits of 2D action representations.

Method: Pix2Act generates continuous image-space keypoint trajectories in each camera plane, losslessly recovers end-effector poses via triangulation, aligns observations and actions in the same coordinate space, and uses equivariant transformations to rotate camera images jointly with their image-space actions. A multi-view fusion network architecture respects per-view rotations.

Result: Pix2Act outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across diverse simulated and real-world manipulation tasks and remains robust under camera perturbations.

Conclusion: By implicitly enlarging the support of the data distribution and learning invariant action structures across transformations, Pix2Act achieves improved generalization and overall performance for 3D manipulation policies.

Abstract: Representing manipulation actions as 2D trajectories in the camera plane provides a compact and interpretable basis for learning complex 3D manipulation policies. However, it also creates challenges from out-of-frame trajectories and limited precision. We propose Pix2Act, an imitation learning method that addresses these challenges by generating continuous image-space keypoint trajectories in each camera plane and losslessly recovering end-effector poses via triangulation. This reformulates high-dimensional 3D control as a simpler, more learnable 2D prediction problem. Crucially, it aligns observations and actions in the same coordinate space, enabling equivariant transformations to jointly rotate individual camera images together with their image-space actions. We analyze the symmetry properties of this augmentation and design a network architecture that can fuse multiple camera views while respecting their per-view rotations. As a result, Pix2Act implicitly enlarges the support of the data distribution and learns invariant action structures across transformations, yielding improved generalization and overall performance. Across diverse simulated and real-world manipulation tasks, Pix2Act outperforms state-of-the-art baselines and remains robust under camera perturbations.

</details>


### [184] [GeoGS-SLAM: Online Monocular Reconstruction Using Gaussian Splatting with Geometric Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11184)
*Ruilan Gao,Letian Jin,Yu Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GeoGS-SLAM is an online monocular dense reconstruction system that combines 3D Gaussian Splatting with learned geometric priors from feed-forward models, jointly optimizing camera poses and scene maps via coarse-to-fine photometric and geometric losses with loop closure, achieving state-of-the-art rendering quality and tracking accuracy in real-time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3DGS-based SLAM methods need external depth sensors for geometric information, while geometry-prior-based systems discard RGB data during optimization, degrading reconstruction quality. GeoGS-SLAM aims to combine both 3DGS representation and learned geometric priors for better dense reconstruction without external sensors.

Method: (1) Feed-forward visual geometry model predicts camera/scene priors from uncalibrated RGB. (2) Gaussian scene map expanded by sampling primitives from both RGB input and geometric priors. (3) Coarse-to-fine joint optimization of camera poses and scene map minimizing photometric + geometric losses. (4) Online loop closure detection and pose graph optimization for global consistency.

Result: Superior rendering quality and tracking accuracy compared to state-of-the-art methods across indoor and outdoor benchmarks, while maintaining online real-time performance.

Conclusion: GeoGS-SLAM effectively integrates 3DGS map representation with learned geometric priors for monocular dense reconstruction, eliminating the need for external depth sensors while achieving high-quality results in real-time.

Abstract: SLAM methods based on 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) have demonstrated impressive tracking and mapping performance, but typically require additional geometric information from external depth sensors. Meanwhile, recent SLAM systems that leverage geometric priors from pre-trained feed-forward models enable real-time dense reconstruction, yet often discard original RGB information during optimization, thus degrading overall reconstruction quality. We present GeoGS-SLAM, an online monocular dense reconstruction system that combines the 3DGS-based map representation with learned geometric priors. Given uncalibrated RGB input, we first employ a feed-forward visual geometry model to predict camera and scene priors. The Gaussian scene map is then expanded by directly sampling Gaussian primitives from both RGB input and geometric priors. Camera poses and the scene map are jointly optimized through a coarse-to-fine strategy that minimizes both photometric and geometric losses. To ensure global consistency, we further incorporate online loop closure detection and pose graph optimization. Extensive experiments across indoor and outdoor benchmarks demonstrate that GeoGS-SLAM achieves superior rendering quality and tracking accuracy compared to state-of-the-art methods while maintaining online real-time performance. Project page: https://rlgao.github.io/geogs_slam.

</details>


### [185] [Stop to Decide: Latency-Aware Proprioceptive Navigation Primitives for Mapping-Free Quadruped Inspection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11204)
*Hanting Suo,Haonan Yan,Liang Wang,Aiguo Song*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A compute-constrained quadruped runs navigation at ~15Hz (below controller design rate), causing stair-summit arrival detection failures. A climb-settle cadence eliminates overshoot (22/45 vs 1/45; p≈2.4e-7). A logistic v/f model predicts failure; a 40Hz out-of-sample test favors the protocol (33% vs 43%/22%). Deployment rule: critical loop rate ~19Hz at 0.30m/s. The fully onboard, mapping-free, learning-free stack (IMU, foot force, range sensors, line camera) achieves 90% trial success on a single course.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Compute-constrained quadrupeds run navigation loops below controller design rate due to onboard vision pipeline sharing (e.g., 15Hz on Jetson Orin), which breaks standard proprioceptive stair-summit arrival detection and causes overshoot on short stepped platforms.

Method: Proposes a climb-settle cadence as an alternative to in-motion detection; uses a logistic dose-response model in v/f to characterize failure; stacks fully onboard, mapping-free, learning-free sensors (IMU, 4 foot-force channels, 3 1-D range sensors, 1 line camera) with line-following, a 3-segment 90° turn maneuver, and stair traversal.

Result: Climb-settle cadence holds overshoot near zero across loop rates (22/45 vs 1/45; Fisher p≈2.4e-7); at deployed ~15Hz: 7/15 vs 0/15. Logistic model fits failure; 40Hz out-of-sample test: 33% observed vs 43%/22% predicted. Critical loop rate ~19Hz at 0.30m/s. Overall inspection course success: 18/20 (90%).

Conclusion: The climb-settle cadence reliably eliminates stair-summit overshoot under slow loop rates, and the proposed fully onboard, mapping/learning-free stack achieves high success on a single inspection course geometry.

Abstract: Compute-constrained quadrupeds often run their navigation loop far below the controller's design rate: sharing the onboard Jetson Orin with the vision pipeline slows our stair loop to about 15 Hz. This latency breaks a standard proprioceptive pattern: declaring stair-summit arrival from the body-pitch signal while still climbing. On a stepped platform whose 50 cm top is shorter than the robot (Unitree Go2, about 75 cm), in-motion detection overshoots the top edge with probability rising with the per-period advance v/f (the slowest about 15 Hz cell partly diluted by a separate non-arrival mode), whereas a climb-settle cadence holds overshoot near zero at every loop rate (pooled 22/45 vs 1/45 over about 30/20/15 Hz; Fisher p about 2.4e-7; 7/15 vs 0/15 at the deployed about 15 Hz). A logistic dose-response model in v/f captures the failure; a pre-specified 40 Hz out-of-sample test favours the protocol-clean fit (33% observed vs 43%/22% predicted), giving a deployment rule (critical loop rate about 19 Hz at 0.30 m/s). The detector sits in a fully onboard, mapping-free and learning-free stack: built-in inertial measurement unit, four foot-force channels, three 1-D ranges, one line camera, chaining line-following, a three-segment maneuver for 90-degree corners in a 55 cm corridor (20/20 contact-free vs 14/20 with 12 wall contacts for in-place yaw; exit-heading error 1.56 degrees vs 5.64 degrees), and stair traversal, completing the inspection course in 18/20 trials (90%). Results are from a single course geometry, platform, and operator.

</details>


### [186] [Towards Predictive, Aligned, and Scalable Robot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11270)
*Peijun Tang,Shangjin Xie,Baifu Huang,Binyan Sun,Haotian Yang,Kuncheng Luo,Weiqi Jin,Shilin Fang,Jianan Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Lumo-2 introduces a latent world-action model that generates actions by reasoning over world dynamics in latent space, using multi-stage modality pre-alignment to create structured representations for predictive reasoning, consistently outperforming VLA and WAM baselines on challenging real-world tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard reconstruction-based action tokenization objectives induce representations biased toward low-level signal fidelity, misaligning reconstruction quality with downstream control performance. The authors hypothesize that action generation quality is governed by the geometry of the latent space.

Method: A latent world-action model with: (1) learned latent world dynamics capturing physically grounded visual transitions, (2) multi-stage modality pre-alignment strategy that progressively aligns action representations with latent world dynamics, vision, and language, (3) predictive reasoning over world dynamics in latent space while remaining lightweight.

Result: Lumo-2 consistently outperforms strong VLA and WAM baselines on challenging real-world tasks requiring temporal reasoning, physical understanding, or high control complexity, including long-horizon and dexterous manipulation.

Conclusion: Structured multimodal alignment and predictive reasoning are fundamental principles for advancing embodied intelligence.

Abstract: Learning, at its core, extends beyond memorization to the ability to reason and solve novel problems by navigating a space of possibilities. We introduce Lumo-2, a latent world-action model that generates actions by reasoning over world dynamics in latent space. The learned latent world dynamics capture physically grounded visual transitions, naturally encoding future possibilities and providing a unified substrate for cross-modal alignment. This formulation enables predictive reasoning akin to world modelling while remaining lightweight and focused on physical dynamics relevant to control. Central to our approach is the hypothesis that action generation quality is governed by the geometry of the latent space. We observe that standard reconstruction-based action tokenization objectives induce representations biased toward low-level signal fidelity, leading to misalignment between reconstruction quality and downstream control performance. To address this limitation, we propose a multi-stage modality pre-alignment strategy in which action representations are progressively aligned with latent world dynamics, vision, and language. This process enforces cross-modal consistency, promotes abstraction, and induces a structured latent space for predictive reasoning. We provide a systematic empirical study of latent world modelling and modality alignment, analyzing their roles in scaling laws and out-of-distribution generalization. Results show that Lumo-2 consistently outperforms strong vision-language-action (VLA) and world-action model (WAM) baselines, with gains on challenging real-world tasks requiring temporal reasoning, physical understanding, or high control complexity, including long-horizon and dexterous manipulation. These findings suggest that structured multimodal alignment and predictive reasoning are fundamental principles for advancing embodied intelligence.

</details>


### [187] [CR-Solver: GPU-Accelerated Kinematics Solver for Tendon-driven Continuum Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11340)
*Heqing Yang,Yang Yi,Linqing Zhong,Linjiang Huang,Si Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CR-Solver is a GPU-accelerated, optimization-based motion generation solver for tendon-driven continuum robots, unifying IK, path following, and trajectory planning in a single framework, achieving >95% success rate and millimeter accuracy with significant speedups over CPU solvers, implemented in pure Python.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing motion planning libraries are grounded in rigid-body assumptions, creating a critical gap for fast and practical motion generation tools specifically designed for continuum robots, despite recent advances in sensing and control that heighten the need for precise motion generation.

Method: A two-stage, optimization-based solver (CR-Solver) that unifies inverse kinematics, path following, and trajectory planning within a single constrained nonlinear optimization framework, accelerated by GPU parallel optimization, implemented in pure Python.

Result: Demonstrated significant speedups over traditional CPU-based solvers across three tasks, with a consistently high success rate above 95% and millimeter-level accuracy.

Conclusion: CR-Solver provides a practical, extensible, and accessible foundation for high-performance motion planning of tendon-driven continuum robots, reducing the barrier to adoption through its pure Python implementation.

Abstract: Continuum robots provide intrinsic compliance, high dexterity, and safe physical interaction, enabling navigation and manipulation in confined and unstructured environments. Despite recent advances in sensing and control, heightening the need for precise motion generation, most widely used planning libraries are grounded in rigid-body assumptions, creating a critical gap for fast and practical tools for continuum robots. To address this, we present CR-Solver, a two-stage, optimization-based solver for the motion generation of tendon-driven continuum robots. Our method unifies inverse kinematics, path following, and trajectory planning within a single constrained nonlinear optimization framework. Leveraging GPU-accelerated parallel optimization, CR-Solver delivers fast, accurate, and constraint-aware solutions. We validate our approach on three tasks, demonstrating significant speedups over traditional CPU-based solvers while achieving a consistently high success rate above 95% and millimeter-level accuracy. The solver is implemented in pure Python, reducing the barrier to adoption and offering a practical, extensible foundation for continuum robots' high-performance motion planning.

</details>


### [188] [A Glimpse into Long-term Physical Coexistence with Intelligent Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11377)
*Weiqi Jin,Peijun Tang,Kuncheng Luo,Baifu Huang,Binyan Sun,Haotian Yang,Shangjin Xie,Jianan Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PHILIA is a multi-robot agent framework built on a robot gateway abstraction that decouples high-level reasoning from low-level execution, enabling compositional integration of diverse user interfaces, robot embodiments, and policy backends for long-term physical human-robot coexistence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-term physical coexistence with intelligent robots requires more than capable robot policies; persistent robotic assistants must support diverse interfaces, maintain long-horizon memory, coordinate across embodiments, and translate human intent into safe execution.

Method: PHILIA introduces a robot gateway abstraction that decouples low-frequency, high-semantic agent reasoning from high-frequency, low-level robot execution. It exposes robot-local runtimes, onboard perception, navigation, speaker, and policies through a unified capability interface, enabling plug-and-play integration of interfaces, embodiments, and policy backends.

Result: Validated on Astribot S1 robots, with the gateway contract designed to support heterogeneous robot platforms. Demonstrated representative use cases spanning interactive household scenarios from simple organization to complex long-horizon and dexterous service tasks such as packing a backpack and lifting a garbage bag.

Conclusion: The architecture makes the user experience compositional—advances in interfaces, embodiments, policies, navigation, or interaction algorithms can improve overall experience without system redesign. Human-in-the-loop confirmation and adjustment during execution is essential for effective assistance.

Abstract: Long-term physical coexistence with intelligent robots requires more than capable robot policies. A persistent robotic assistant must support diverse user-facing interfaces, maintain long-horizon memory of people and preferences, coordinate across robot embodiments, and translate human intent into safe physical execution. We introduce PHILIA, a multi-robot agent built around a robot gateway abstraction. PHILIA retains the rich interaction and tool ecosystem of OpenClaw while exposing robot-local runtimes, onboard perception, navigation, speaker, and robot policies through a unified capability interface. This design decouples low-frequency, high-semantic agent reasoning from high-frequency, low-level robot execution, enabling plug-and-play integration of user interfaces, robot embodiments, and policy backends. As a result, the user experience becomes compositional: advances in user interfaces, robot embodiments, robot policies, navigation, or interaction algorithms can improve the overall experience without redesigning the system. We validate the architecture on Astribot S1 robots while designing the robot gateway contract to support future heterogeneous robot platforms through a shared capability interface for observation, task execution, navigation, speech playback, status monitoring, and task cancellation. We present representative use cases in which agent memory and scene understanding are grounded in robot actions. These span interactive household scenarios, ranging from simple organization to challenging long-horizon and dexterous service tasks, such as packing a backpack and lifting a garbage bag. We highlight the human-robot interaction flow, where contextual understanding of user intent and preferences, together with human-in-the-loop confirmation or adjustment during execution, is essential for effective assistance.

</details>


### [189] [From Sketch Prior to Trajectories: A Mission-Oriented Coordinated Navigation Framework for Indoor UAV Swarm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11386)
*Xinhang Xu,Ruiyang Liu,Haotian Jin,Yi Wang,Hongming Shen,Jianping Li,Lihua Xie*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a mission-oriented coordinated navigation framework for multi-UAV swarms in indoor environments that leverages sketch-map priors (floor plans, CAD layouts) to generate traversability representations via topological alignment of onboard observations, and uses a layered 2D-3D navigation approach for path planning and trajectory optimization. Validation includes simulations and real-world experiments in multi-room environments under both communication-available and loss conditions, with multi-floor scalability demonstrated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: UAV swarm applications like indoor inspection, security patrol, and logistics are mission-oriented (following prescribed region sequences) rather than exploration-oriented. Existing approaches lack efficient use of pre-deployment sketch-map priors and do not adequately address the planar connectivity nature of indoor multi-floor missions where UAVs operate at fixed altitude layers.

Method: (1) Topological alignment of onboard observations with sketch-map priors; (2) Fusion of aligned prior with online observations to build a mission-oriented traversability map; (3) Layered 2D-3D coordinated navigation: 2D guide path planning for mission-level region transitions, and guide-driven 3D trajectory optimization for dynamic feasibility and collision avoidance.

Result: The framework is validated in structured multi-room indoor environments via both simulation and real-world experiments. It demonstrates effective coordinated navigation under communication-available and communication-loss conditions. Multi-floor simulations confirm scalability to layered indoor structures.

Conclusion: The proposed sketch-map prior-based layered 2D-3D navigation framework is effective for mission-oriented multi-UAV indoor operations, providing robust coordinated navigation even under communication constraints and scaling to multi-floor environments.

Abstract: UAV swarm for applications, such as indoor inspection, security patrol, and logistics delivery, are often mission-oriented rather than exploration-oriented. In these tasks, UAVs are required to visit task-relevant regions in a prescribed sequence, and such region-level mission information can often be obtained from pre-deployment sketch-map priors, such as floor plans, CAD layouts, or evacuation diagrams. Although these tasks are executed in three-dimensional space, UAVs usually fly within a specific altitude layer or a nearly fixed altitude range on each floor, making mission-level region transitions mainly governed by planar connectivity. Based on these observations, this paper proposes a mission-oriented coordinated navigation framework that exploits sketch-map priors for multi-UAV indoor operations. Onboard observations are used to perform topological alignment, and the aligned prior is fused with online observations to construct a mission-oriented traversability representation. A layered 2D--3D coordinated navigation framework is further developed, where 2D guided path planning generates mission-oriented guide paths and guide-driven 3D trajectory optimization produces dynamically feasible and collision-free trajectories. Simulation and real-world experiments validate the effectiveness of the proposed framework in structured multi-room indoor environments and further demonstrate its coordinated navigation capability under both communication-available and communication-loss conditions. Multi-floor simulation results show the scalability of the system to layered indoor structures.

</details>


### [190] [WALA Learning Executable Latent Actions from Action-Labeled Demonstrations and Action-Free Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11397)
*Jiahao Liu,Zhongpu Xia,Shuai Tian,Huangrui Li,Yuhang Zheng,Ning Ma,Xin Fu,Xiaotian Liu,Jing Li,Yixian Li,ShangQing Zhou,Zebin Xing,Linbo Wang,Chaoyue Li,Haoran Li,Dongbin Zhao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: WALA is a framework that learns executable latent actions from both action-labeled robot demonstrations and action-free videos by pretraining a semantic-geometric latent action model using DINOv3 feature space and dense depth space, achieving state-of-the-art results on RoboCasa (75.2%) and strong real-world manipulation performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robot policies require action-labeled demonstrations which are expensive and hard to scale, while large-scale human/robot videos lack executable action labels. The goal is to leverage both data types for policy learning.

Method: WALA pretrains a semantic-geometric latent action model from videos by modeling evolution between current and sparsely sampled future observations. It predicts future deltas in DINOv3 feature space and dense depth space instead of raw pixels. During policy training, the pretrained encoder provides latent action targets, the decoder serves as a trainable latent world model, and the vision-language backbone is jointly supervised by robot action prediction, latent action target matching, and future dynamics prediction.

Result: Achieves strong performance on RoboTwin, sets new SOTA on RoboCasa with 75.2% average success, and improves both policy performance and generalization in real-world manipulation tasks.

Conclusion: Action-free videos can effectively contribute dynamics supervision through latent action modeling, enabling scalable robot policy learning without requiring full action annotations.

Abstract: Generalizable robot policies typically rely on action-labeled robot demonstrations, which are expensive to collect and difficult to scale. In contrast, large-scale human and robot videos contain rich physical interactions but often lack executable robot action labels. We present WALA, a framework for learning executable latent actions from both action-labeled demonstrations and action-free videos. WALA first pretrains a semantic-geometric latent action model from videos by modeling the evolution between current observations and sparsely sampled future observations. Instead of reconstructing raw pixels, WALA predicts future deltas in the DINOv3 feature space and dense depth space, preserving task-relevant semantic and geometric structure while reducing sensitivity to appearance details. During policy training, the pretrained encoder provides stable latent action targets, and the decoder serves as a trainable latent world model. The latent actions generated by the vision-language backbone are jointly supervised by robot action prediction, latent action target matching, and future dynamics prediction. This enables action-labeled demonstrations to provide executable control supervision, while action-free videos contribute dynamics supervision without requiring robot action annotations. Experiments show that WALA achieves strong performance on RoboTwin, sets a new state-of-the-art result on RoboCasa with 75.2% average success, and improves both policy performance and generalization in real-world manipulation tasks.

</details>


### [191] [EDAR: Learning Environment-Dependent Action Representations for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11427)
*Yuecheng Xu,Tong Yang,Jingkai Jia,Chi Zhang,Xuelong Li,Wenqiang Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: EDAR is an environment-dependent action representation that grounds action tokens in both executable control structure and expected visual consequences, improving downstream policy learning especially in long-horizon manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing action representation methods mainly encode the structure of the action stream itself, treating the role of actions in the environment as implicit. However, manipulation is about changing the world: the same action can induce different outcomes under different scene contexts, making action semantics inherently environment-dependent.

Method: EDAR (Environment-Dependent Action Representation) grounds action tokens in both executable control structure and expected visual consequences, coupling motor commands with their environment-conditioned effects to capture interaction semantics rather than merely command-level patterns.

Result: Experiments on simulated and real-robot manipulation benchmarks demonstrate that EDAR improves downstream policy learning, especially in long-horizon manipulation tasks.

Conclusion: Grounding action representations in executable control structure and environment-conditioned visual change is important for effective robotic manipulation.

Abstract: Learning effective action representations is critical for robotic manipulation, where raw control trajectories are often noisy, redundant, and difficult to model directly. Existing methods mainly encode the structure of the action stream itself, treating the role of actions in the environment as implicit. Yet manipulation is about changing the world: the same action segment can induce different outcomes under different scene contexts, making action semantics inherently environment-dependent. We propose EDAR, an Environment-Dependent Action Representation that grounds action tokens in both executable control structure and expected visual consequences. By coupling motor commands with their environment-conditioned effects, EDAR encourages the learned action space to capture interaction semantics rather than merely command-level patterns. Experiments on simulated and real-robot manipulation benchmarks demonstrate that EDAR improves downstream policy learning, especially in long-horizon manipulation. These results highlight the importance of grounding action representations in executable control structure and environment-conditioned visual change.

</details>


### [192] [Towards Human-level Dexterous Teleoperation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11481)
*Puhao Li,Zeyuan Chen,Yingying Wu,Pengkun Wei,Yuyang Li,Tianyu Wang,Jiaxiao Shi,Mingrui Yu,Baoxiong Jia,Song-chun Zhu,Tengyu Liu,Siyuan Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TeleDexter is a hand-object co-tracking controller for dexterous teleoperation that uses single-stage RL with hybrid rewards and random action masking to achieve human-level in-hand dexterity, demonstrating 75% average success rate on seven challenging tasks where baselines fail, and enabling policy learning from demonstrations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humans can naturally perform complex in-hand manipulations (tool use, grasp switching, object repositioning) through dynamic contact transitions, but current robot teleoperation systems lack this level of dexterity. The gap is in precise control of object motion via dynamic hand-object contact during teleoperation.

Method: A hand-object co-tracking controller trained via single-stage RL on consecutive co-tracking subgoals derived from human reference motions, using a hybrid reward (sparse subgoal + dense tracking rewards), random action masking, and domain randomization for zero-shot sim-to-real transfer.

Result: Achieved 75% average success rate across seven dexterous teleoperation tasks (object reorientation and long-horizon tool use) on two dexterous hands, where all baselines consistently failed. Collected demonstrations successfully trained autonomous policies via behavioral cloning.

Conclusion: TeleDexter marks a concrete step towards human-level dexterous teleoperation by enabling learning across diverse interaction modalities rather than frame-wise trajectory imitation, with zero-shot real robot transfer and the ability to bootstrap autonomous policy learning from demonstrations.

Abstract: Humans routinely wield tools, swap grasps, and reposition objects within a single hand, seamlessly orchestrating contact transitions that span translation, reorientation, and finger gaiting. Endowing robot dexterous hands with this level of in-hand dexterity through teleoperation requires precise control of object motion via dynamic hand-object contact, yet current teleoperation systems remain far from this capability. To bridge this gap, we take a major step towards human-level dexterous teleoperation by introducing TeleDexter, a hand-object co-tracking controller that maps operator intent into learned, low-level contact execution. The controller is trained on consecutive co-tracking subgoals derived from human reference motions, utilizing a hybrid reward that couples sparse subgoal objectives with dense tracking rewards to enable learning across diverse interaction modalities rather than frame-wise trajectory imitation. The entire pipeline requires only single-stage RL and, with random action masking and domain randomization, transfers zero-shot to the real robot. We evaluate TeleDexter on seven challenging dexterous teleoperation tasks spanning object reorientation and long-horizon tool use across two dexterous hands, achieving a 75% average success rate where all baselines consistently fail. Furthermore, the collected demonstrations successfully train autonomous policies via behavioral cloning, marking a concrete step towards human-level dexterous teleoperation.

</details>


### [193] [See like a Robot: Robot-Centric Pointmaps for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11498)
*Byungkun Lee,Dongyoon Hwang,Dongjin Kim,Hojoon Lee,Minho Park,Jaegul Choo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VLA models suffer from a frame mismatch between camera observation and robot action coordinates. The paper proposes robot-centric pointmaps—images storing 3D coordinates in the robot frame—to resolve this mismatch, integrating seamlessly into pretrained 2D VLAs. Pointmaps improve pi0.5 and SmolVLA on RoboCasa and outperform baselines, especially under unseen camera placements in real-robot experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models define actions in the robot's 3D coordinate frame but observe scenes in the camera frame, creating a frame mismatch. This is benign under fixed viewpoints but harms generalization across diverse camera setups in large-scale datasets.

Method: The paper introduces robot-centric pointmaps—images where each pixel stores the 3D coordinate of the corresponding scene point in the robot frame. These provide robot-frame 3D geometry while preserving the 2D grid structure required by pretrained 2D VLAs, enabling integration with minimal architectural changes.

Result: Pointmaps improve both pi0.5 and SmolVLA on RoboCasa and outperform camera-viewpoint and 3D-aware baselines. In real-robot experiments, the advantage over RGB-only policies increases when the camera is placed in novel positions unseen during training.

Conclusion: Robot-centric pointmaps effectively resolve the frame mismatch in VLAs, enhance cross-viewpoint generalization, and can be integrated into existing 2D VLA architectures with minimal modifications, leading to more robust robot learning across diverse camera setups.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models predict robot actions from visual observations and language instructions. These actions are defined in the robot's own 3D coordinate frame, yet most VLAs observe the scene in the camera frame, creating a frame mismatch between where the scene is observed and where actions are defined. The mismatch is benign under a fixed viewpoint, where the policy can memorize a single observation-to-action mapping, but grows harder as large-scale datasets aggregate demonstrations across diverse camera setups and the policy must generalize this mapping across viewpoints. We address this mismatch with robot-centric pointmaps, images whose pixels store the 3D coordinates of scene points in the robot frame. Pointmaps provide robot-frame 3D geometry while preserving the dense H x W grid expected by pretrained 2D VLAs, so they integrate into existing VLAs with minimal architectural change. On RoboCasa, pointmaps improve both pi0.5 and SmolVLA and outperform representative camera-viewpoint and 3D-aware baselines. In real-robot experiments, their advantage over an RGB-only policy widens when the camera is moved to a placement unseen during training.

</details>


### [194] [ERR@HRI 3.0 Challenge: Multimodal Detection of Errors and Anticipation in Human-Robot Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11570)
*Maria Teresa Parreira,Micol Spitale,Maia Stiber,Shiye Cao,Amama Mahmood,Chien-Ming Huang,Hatice Gunes,Wendy Ju*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The ERR@HRI 3.0 Challenge provided two crowdsourced, naturalistic video datasets (BAD and Bad Idea) for developing multimodal models to detect and anticipate errors in human-robot interaction. Three teams submitted models that outperformed CNN baselines across detection, prediction, and cross-dataset generalization tracks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing error detection approaches for HRI are limited to specific contexts, controlled settings, or pre-extracted features, lacking generalizability to real-world conditions. The challenge aimed to enable end-to-end innovation for detecting and preventing errors using naturalistic, raw video data.

Method: Two crowdsourced datasets were provided: (1) BAD dataset (45 participants' webcam reactions to robot/human failures), and (2) Bad Idea dataset (29 participants' anticipatory facial responses before failures). Three tracks were designed: bystander reaction detection (Track 1), anticipatory outcome prediction (Track 2), and cross-dataset generalization (Track 3). Participants developed multimodal machine learning models.

Result: Three teams submitted valid models, all surpassing the provided convolutional neural network baselines across all tracks.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate feasibility of building generalizable, context-aware, and anticipatory error detection systems using naturalistic video data, with implications for improving HRI error handling through both reactive detection and proactive anticipation.

Abstract: As robots become increasingly integrated into human environments, their ability to detect and respond to errors remains critical for maintaining user trust and interaction quality. While recent advances in machine learning have improved error detection capabilities, most approaches are limited to specific contexts, controlled settings, or pre-extracted features, limiting their generalizability and applicability to real-world conditions. To address this challenge, the third edition of the ERR@HRI Challenge (ERR@HRI 3.0) provided researchers with two complementary datasets that enable end-to-end innovation in methods for both detecting and preventing errors in human-robot interaction. The challenge offered raw, non-anonymized video data from naturalistic settings: (1) the Bystander Affect Detection (BAD) dataset, containing webcam recordings of 45 participants' spontaneous reactions to robot and human failure scenarios; and (2) the Bad Idea dataset, featuring 29 participants' anticipatory facial responses while predicting action outcomes before failures occur. Both datasets were collected via crowdsourcing, capturing the inherent variability of real-world conditions. This naturalistic variability, while challenging, provides an authentic testbed for developing robust error detection systems. Participants developed multimodal machine learning models for bystander reaction detection (Track 1) and anticipatory outcome prediction (Track 2), with an optional cross-dataset generalization track (Track 3). Three teams submitted valid models, all of which surpassed our convolutional neural network baselines. This paper describes the datasets, tasks, baselines, and results of ERR@HRI 3.0, and discusses implications for building generalizable, context-aware, and anticipatory error detection systems for human-robot interaction.

</details>


### [195] [WarpMPC: Large-Batch MPC on GPU via ADMM with Unrolled $LDL^\top$ Factorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11603)
*Henrik Hose,Se Hwan Jeon,Charles Khazoom,Sangbae Kim,Sebastian Trimpe*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents WarpMPC, a JAX/Warp toolbox for GPU-accelerated SQP solving of large batches (10K-100K+) of MPC problems with identical structure, achieving 8,000-250,000 iterations/sec via unrolled sparse factorizations, optimized memory layout, and dependency-level scheduled backsolves—outperforming baselines by 3-25× and enabling neural network policy training in under 4 minutes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Maximize GPU throughput for solving large batches of structurally identical SQP problems in MPC, to overcome memory bandwidth bottlenecks and redundant computation in batched ADMM solvers.

Method: Unrolls sparse linear factorizations and solves (dominant in ADMM runtime) by exploiting shared sparsity across batch instances; applies optimized memory layout, padding-reducing segmentation of unrolled factorization, and dependency-level scheduled backsolves; additionally accelerates sensitivity computation.

Result: Achieves 8,000-250,000 SQP iterations/sec on nonlinear cartpole, quadrotor, and humanoid robot benchmarks; outperforms baselines by 3-25×; enables MPC dataset synthesis and neural network policy training in under 4 minutes that stabilizes a nano quadrotor in hardware.

Conclusion: The proposed GPU optimizations make batched SQP solving highly practical for MPC, enabling rapid data generation and neural network policy learning with real-world hardware validation.

Abstract: This paper introduces numerical optimizations for maximizing throughput on GPU when solving large batches (10,000 to over 100,000) of sequential quadratic programming (SQP) iterations, where all problems have the same structure. The optimizations are implemented in a toolbox WarpMPC for model-predictive control (MPC) in JAX and Warp. Based on the insight that all MPC problem instances in a batch share the same sparsity in time, cost, and constraints, we propose unrolling sparse linear factorizations and solves, which dominate alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM) solver runtime. We avoid memory access bottlenecks and wasting computations via optimized memory layout, padding-reducing segmentation of the unrolled factorization, and dependency level scheduled backsolves, additionally accelerating sensitivity computation. We achieve throughputs of 8,000 to 250,000 SQP iterations per second on nonlinear cartpole, quadrotor, and humanoid robot benchmarks, outperforming baselines by 3$\times$ to 25$\times$. We illustrate practical usefulness by synthesizing a dataset and training a neural network approximation of an MPC in under 4 minutes that stabilizes a nano quadrotor in hardware experiments.

</details>


### [196] [SKooP: Symmetric Koopman Predictions for Faster and More Generalizable Legged Robot Locomotion with Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11624)
*Evelyn D'Elia,Weishu Zhan,Giulio Turrisi,Giulio Romualdi,Giuseppe L'Erario,Raffaello Camoriano,Wei Pan,Daniele Pucci*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SKooP combines morphological symmetries with a Koopman model learned via autoencoder to improve sample efficiency and policy learning in reinforcement learning for high-dimensional robots, demonstrating faster convergence and higher rewards on bipedal locomotion tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL algorithms suffer from poor sample efficiency, and existing physics-prior-based approaches are mostly validated on low-dimensional benchmarks rather than high-dimensional robots with complex nonlinear dynamics.

Method: SKooP learns a Koopman model of system dynamics via autoencoder alongside the policy. Koopman predictions serve as privileged observations for the critic, providing smoother, more informative features. Group symmetries are incorporated into actor, critic, encoder, and decoder networks to produce equivariant policies.

Result: SKooP consistently reduces convergence time and increases learned reward on challenging bipedal locomotion tasks on a quadruped robot. Learned policies are transferable to different simulation environments.

Conclusion: Combining morphological symmetries with Koopman-based privileged observations is an effective approach for improving RL sample efficiency and performance on high-dimensional robotic systems with complex dynamics.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) algorithms classically suffer from poor sample efficiency. In robotics, a recent line of work has emerged addressing this problem by encoding physics priors in the learning process. However, most of these approaches are validated on well-defined, low-dimensional benchmark systems rather than high-dimensional robots with complex nonlinear dynamics. In this paper, we introduce \textit{SKooP (Symmetric Koopman Predictions)}, an approach combining the advantages of morphological symmetries with those of a Koopman model learned via autoencoder to enhance policy learning. SKooP learns a Koopman model of the system dynamics alongside the policy. The resulting Koopman predictions are used as privileged observations for the critic, allowing the agent to learn based on smoother, more informative features. We also incorporate group symmetries into the actor, critic, encoder and decoder networks to produce a highly equivariant policy. The SKooP approach is validated via in-depth analysis of the learned Koopman models and symmetric policies to showcase how each of these influences the agent's performance. We also show that the learned policies are transferable to different simulation environments. Our results show that SKooP consistently reduces convergence time and increases the learned reward for multiple challenging bipedal locomotion tasks on a quadruped robot. Project page: https://evelyd.github.io/SymmetricKoopmanPredictions/

</details>


### [197] [Breaking the 15% Barrier: A Real-World Data-Driven System for Proactive Social Robot Triggered by User Nonverbal Cues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11633)
*Yuga Yano,Yuki Okafuji,Ryo Miyoshi,Sanae Yamashita,Yoshiki Ohira*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Service robots in retail stores often receive nonverbal cues (e.g., waving, pointing) that trigger robot responses, yet current audio-only dialogue systems miss these. Analysis of a 6-day real-world deployment shows 15.3% of robot utterances were initiated by nonverbal behaviors. The authors define frequent nonverbal cues, build a real-time multi-person multi-label recognizer, and propose a dialogue framework that feeds recognized nonverbal tokens into LLM-based utterance generation, enabling proactive robot responses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current service robot dialogue systems rely on audio-only (STT-LLM-TTS) pipelines, which fail to capture nonverbal customer behaviors (approaching, waving, pointing, showing items) that frequently initiate or guide interactions in real retail environments.

Method: A 6-day in-the-wild deployment with a teleoperated humanoid robot; analysis of customer interaction data; definition of frequent service-relevant nonverbal cues; development of a real-time multi-person, multi-label recognizer from video; a dialogue framework that conditions LLM utterance generation on recognized nonverbal cue tokens, optionally using a vision-language model when items are shown.

Result: 15.3% of robot utterances were triggered by user nonverbal behaviors rather than spoken input. The proposed recognizer and dialogue framework enable real-time proactive responses to nonverbal cues; evaluated offline on nonverbal-triggered turns and demonstrated in an online prototype.

Conclusion: Nonverbal cues are a significant modality in human-robot interaction in retail, and integrating them into the dialogue pipeline improves coverage and proactivity beyond audio-only systems.

Abstract: Service robots in retail stores increasingly rely on cascaded speech pipelines (STT-LLM-TTS), yet many customer-robot interactions are initiated or guided by nonverbal behaviors such as approaching, waving, pointing, or showing items. This paper studies such cues in a real-world store deployment with a teleoperated humanoid robot and shows that a non-negligible portion of robot turns are triggered by nonverbal behaviors rather than spoken input, revealing a limitation of audio-only dialogue systems. In a 6-day in-the-wild deployment, 15.3\% of robot utterances were initiated by users' nonverbal behaviors rather than spoken input. Based on an analysis of observed customer behaviors, we define a set of frequent, service-relevant nonverbal cues and develop a real-time multi-person, multi-label recognizer that runs online from video. We then propose a dialogue framework that conditions LLM-based utterance generation on recognized nonverbal cue tokens, and optionally leverages a vision-language model when items are shown, enabling proactive robot responses without hand-crafted rules. We evaluate the approach offline on nonverbal-triggered turns and demonstrate an online prototype that reacts to users' nonverbal cues in real time.

</details>


### [198] [DA-Nav: Direction-Aware City-Scale Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11638)
*Ye Yuan,Kehan Chen,Xinqiang Yu,Wentao Xu,Heng Wang,Libo Huang,Chuanguang Yang,Yan Huang,Jiawei He,Zhulin An*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DA-Nav is a novel vision-language navigation framework that uses directional instructions from commercial tools like Google Maps to achieve city-scale outdoor navigation, reformulating it as a spatial grounding problem on 2D image planes. It employs Chain-of-Thought reasoning for trajectory recovery and introduces the ReDA dataset. DA-Nav achieves 56.16% success rate in CARLA, outperforms SOTA methods, and generalizes to quadruped and humanoid robots for real-world kilometer-scale navigation without fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: City-scale outdoor navigation is hindered by heavy reliance on dense maps or costly navigation supervision. The paper aims to leverage readily available directional instructions from commercial navigation tools (e.g., Google Maps) to overcome these limitations, while also addressing error accumulation in long-horizon tasks through robust trajectory recovery.

Method: DA-Nav reformulates navigation as a discrete spatial grounding problem on the egocentric 2D image plane. It uses a Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning process with three steps: deviation assessment, action prediction, and target grid selection. The authors also introduce the ReDA dataset containing direction-aware instructions and recovery trajectories to support CoT reasoning.

Result: DA-Nav achieves a 56.16% success rate in unseen urban environments in CARLA, outperforming existing SOTA methods with stronger recovery capability. It also seamlessly adapts to quadruped and humanoid robots for real-world kilometer-scale outdoor navigation without fine-tuning.

Conclusion: The proposed paradigm effectively bridges commercial navigation instructions with executable actions, demonstrating strong performance and generalization across simulation and diverse real-world robot platforms.

Abstract: City-scale outdoor navigation is currently hindered by the heavy reliance on dense maps or costly navigation supervision. In this work, we introduce a novel paradigm for leveraging directional instructions from commercial navigation tools (e.g., Google Maps). To bridge the gap between commercial instructions and executable navigation actions, while mitigating long-horizon error accumulation through robust trajectory recovery, we propose DA-Nav, a Direction-Aware vision-language Navigation framework that reformulates navigation as a discrete spatial grounding problem on the egocentric 2D image plane. To achieve trajectory recovery, DA-Nav employs a Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning process encompassing deviation assessment, action prediction, and target grid selection. We further introduce ReDA, a dataset that provides direction-aware instructions and recovery trajectories to enhance spatial grounding and support CoT recovery reasoning. Extensive experiments in CARLA demonstrate that DA-Nav achieves a high success rate of 56.16% in unseen urban environments, outperforming existing State-of-The-Art (SoTA) methods while maintaining a substantially stronger recovery capability. Furthermore, without fine-tuning, DA-Nav seamlessly adapts to both quadruped and humanoid robots, enabling stable kilometer-scale closed-loop outdoor navigation in complex real world environments.

</details>


### [199] [Xiaomi-Robotics-U0: Unified Embodied Synthesis with World Foundation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11643)
*Xinghang Li,Jun Guo,Qiwei Li,Long Qian,Hang Lai,Yueze Wang,Hongyu Yan,Jiahang Cao,Xi Chen,Jingen Qu,Jiaxi Song,Nan Sun,Hanye Zhao,Futeng Liu,Wanli Peng,Heyun Wang,Yunhong Wang,Caoyu Xia,Jack Zhao,Diyun Xiang,Hangjun Ye,Heng Qu,Huaping Liu,Jason Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Xiaomi-Robotics-U0 is a 38B-parameter multimodal autoregressive model that unifies embodied generation tasks (text-to-image, image editing, scene generation, embodied transfer, video generation) by extending foundation image/video generation models, achieving SOTA on embodied generation tasks and improving real-world robot manipulation success rates from 36.9% to 63.2%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Foundation image/video generation models have strong generalization but lack multi-view consistency, geometric coherence, and robot embodiment constraints needed for embodied AI. Existing adaptation methods sacrifice pre-trained visual knowledge due to limited robot data.

Method: A 38-billion-parameter multimodal autoregressive model that treats embodied generation as an extension of foundation image/video generation, jointly optimizing five tasks: text-to-image generation, image editing, embodied scene generation, embodied transfer, and embodied video generation within a unified framework.

Result: Outperforms GPT-Image-2.0 in human evaluations for embodied scene generation and transfer; ranks first on World Arena for embodied video generation; improves pi_0.5 out-of-distribution success rate from 36.9% to 63.2% on real-world manipulation tasks.
